$$\frac{z}{c} = \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2}$$
  • Identification Rule: All three variables ($x, y, z$) are present, two variables are squared with the SAME sign, and one variable is linear (degree 1). (Contrast this with a parabolic cylinder like $z = x^2$, where the third variable $y$ is completely missing!)
  • Axis & Opening Direction: The bowl opens along the axis of the linear (degree-1) variable:
    • If $z = \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2}$ ($c > 0$): Bowl opens upward along the $+z$-axis with vertex at $(0,0,0)$.
    • If $z = -\left(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2}\right)$ ($c < 0$): Bowl opens downward along the $-z$-axis.
    • Similarly, $x = y^2 + z^2$ opens along the $+x$-axis, and $y = x^2 + z^2$ opens along the $+y$-axis.
  • At height $z = c$, the cross-section is the ellipse $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$ with semi-axes $a$ (along $x$) and $b$ (along $y$). When $a = b$, the cross-sections are circles, and it is often simply called a circular paraboloid (or just paraboloid).

8. Hyperbolic Paraboloid (Saddle Surface)

Standard equation:

$$\frac{z}{c} = \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2}$$
  • Identification Rule: All three variables ($x, y, z$) are present, one variable is linear (degree 1), and the two squared variables have OPPOSITE signs (one positive, one negative).
  • Exam Note: In Math 201, you are required to identify the hyperbolic paraboloid from its equation, but you are not asked to draw its 3D saddle graph by hand (whereas you must know how to sketch cylinders, spheres, ellipsoids, hyperboloids, cones, and elliptic/circular paraboloids).
Example

Problem Q3: Surface Identification & Vertex Analysis

Problem

Identify and sketch the surface given by:

$$z - x^2 - y^2 = 1$$
Final Answer

Circular Paraboloid $(z - 1) = x^2 + y^2$ with vertex at $C(0, 0, 1)$, opening upward in the $+z$-direction.

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Inspect the variables and their degrees.

All three variables ($x, y, z$) are present. Two variables ($x^2, y^2$) are squared, and one variable ($z$) is linear (degree 1).

Step 2: Isolate the linear variable and the squared terms on opposite sides.

Move $-x^2 - y^2$ to the right-hand side and $+1$ to the left-hand side so that the squared terms are positive:

$$(z - 1) = x^2 + y^2$$

Step 3: Classify and sketch the surface.

  • Since both squared terms ($x^2$ and $y^2$) have the same positive sign and equal coefficients, the surface is a paraboloid (specifically a circular paraboloid).
  • Setting $x = 0$, $y = 0$, and $z - 1 = 0$ gives the vertex (center of the bowl's base) at $C(0, 0, 1)$ on the $z$-axis.
  • Because the coefficient of $(x^2 + y^2)$ is positive ($+$), $z - 1 \ge 0 \implies z \ge 1$, so the bowl opens upward along the positive $z$-axis starting from $(0, 0, 1)$.
Example

Problem Q4: Surface Identification & Vertex Analysis

Problem

Identify and sketch the surface given by:

$$z + x^2 + y^2 = 1$$
Final Answer

Circular Paraboloid $(z - 1) = -(x^2 + y^2)$ with vertex at $C(0, 0, 1)$, opening downward in the $-z$-direction.

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Rearrange the equation into standard paraboloid form.

All three variables are present, with $x^2, y^2$ squared and $z$ linear.

Subtract $1$ from both sides and move $x^2 + y^2$ to the right-hand side by factoring out a minus sign:

$$(z - 1) = -(x^2 + y^2)$$

Step 2: Determine the vertex and opening direction.

  • Inside the parentheses, $x^2$ and $y^2$ have the same sign, confirming that the surface is a paraboloid.
  • The vertex is located where $x = 0$, $y = 0$, and $z - 1 = 0$, which gives $C(0, 0, 1)$.
  • Because there is a negative sign in front of $(x^2 + y^2)$, we have $z - 1 \le 0 \implies z \le 1$. Thus, the paraboloid opens downward along the negative $z$-axis from its peak at $(0, 0, 1)$ (intersecting the $xy$-plane $z=0$ in the unit circle $x^2 + y^2 = 1$).
Explanation

5. Fast Quadric Surface Identification Rules (Cheat Sheet)

Anas Abu Zahra's Rapid Quadric Surface Identification Algorithm

  1. Count how many variables ($x, y, z$) appear in the equation:
    • Only 2 variables present (1 variable missing) $\implies$ Cylinder extending along the missing variable's axis:
      • Both squared, same sign, equal denominators ($x^2 + y^2 = r^2$) $\implies$ Circular Cylinder.
      • Both squared, same sign, unequal denominators ($\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1$) $\implies$ Elliptic Cylinder.
      • One squared, one linear ($y = x^2$ or $z = 1 - x^2$) $\implies$ Parabolic Cylinder.
  2. All 3 variables ($x, y, z$) appear, and ALL THREE are squared ($x^2, y^2, z^2$):
    • Check the constant term (move all variable terms to the LHS and constant to the RHS):
      • RHS $= 0$ (with 2 positive, 1 negative term, e.g., $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} - \frac{z^2}{c^2} = 0$) $\implies$ Double Cone (if given with a square root like $z = \sqrt{x^2+y^2}$, it is a Half-Cone).
      • RHS $= +1$ (after dividing by the constant):
        • All 3 positive ($+, +, +$): Equal denominators $\implies$ Sphere; unequal denominators $\implies$ Ellipsoid.
        • 2 positive, 1 negative ($+, +, -$) $\implies$ Hyperboloid of One Sheet (axis along the negative variable).
        • 1 positive, 2 negative ($+, -, -$) $\implies$ Hyperboloid of Two Sheets (axis along the positive variable).
  3. All 3 variables ($x, y, z$) appear, with TWO squared and ONE linear (degree 1):
    • Isolate the linear variable on one side (e.g., $z - z_0 = \dots$):
      • Two squared terms have the SAME sign ($z = \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2}$) $\implies$ Paraboloid (Elliptic/Circular Paraboloid) opening along the linear variable.
      • Two squared terms have OPPOSITE signs ($z = \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2}$) $\implies$ Hyperbolic Paraboloid.
LECTURE NOTES • TOPIC 05 OF 12

12.6 Quadric Surfaces (Part 2 — Shifted & Inequalities)

Syllabus 11.6 — Completing the Square, Shifted Centers (h,k,l), Inequalities & Exam Questions

Jump to idea:
Explanation

1. Shifted Quadric Surfaces & Completing the Square

In Part 1 of Section 12.6 (Syllabus 11.6), we studied quadric surfaces centered at the origin $(0,0,0)$.

In general, a second-degree equation in three variables $x, y, z$ has the form:

$$Ax^2 + By^2 + Cz^2 + Dx + Ey + Fz + G = 0$$

When linear terms ($Dx, Ey, Fz$) are present alongside quadratic terms ($Ax^2, By^2, Cz^2$), the quadric surface is shifted (translated) so that its center or vertex is located at a point $C(h, k, l)$ instead of the origin $(0,0,0)$.

How to Identify a Shifted Quadric Surface

  1. Group terms by variable: Collect the $x$-terms $(Ax^2 + Dx)$, $y$-terms $(By^2 + Ey)$, and $z$-terms $(Cz^2 + Fz)$ together.
  2. Factor out leading coefficients: Before completing the square on any variable, make sure the coefficient of the squared term inside the parentheses is $+1$. For example, if you have $-y^2 + 2y$, factor out $-1$ to get $-(y^2 - 2y)$.
  3. Complete the square: For each expression of the form $u^2 + bu$, add and subtract $\left(\frac{b}{2}\right)^2$:$$u^2 + bu = \left(u + \frac{b}{2}\right)^2 - \left(\frac{b}{2}\right)^2$$Be careful to multiply the subtracted constant by any factored-out coefficient when moving it outside the parentheses.
  4. Write in Standard Form: Move the remaining constant to the right-hand side (RHS) and divide through so the RHS equals $1$ (for ellipsoids and hyperboloids) or $0$ (for cones). Read the center/vertex $C(h, k, l)$ from the shifted terms $(x - h)$, $(y - k)$, and $(z - l)$.
Example

Question 8: Identifying a Quadric Surface by Completing the Square

Problem

Identify the surface given by the equation:

$$x^2 - y^2 + z^2 + 2y - 2x + 4z + 2 = 0$$
Final Answer

Hyperboloid of one sheet with center $C(1, 1, -2)$, opening along the $y$-direction (parallel to the $y$-axis), with standard form:

$$\frac{(x - 1)^2}{2} - \frac{(y - 1)^2}{2} + \frac{(z + 2)^2}{2} = 1$$
Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Group terms by variable ($x$, $y$, and $z$).

Rearrange the equation so that terms with the same variable are adjacent.

For the $y$-terms ($-y^2 + 2y$), factor out a negative sign so that the coefficient of $y^2$ inside the parentheses is $+1$:

$$(x^2 - 2x) - (y^2 - 2y) + (z^2 + 4z) + 2 = 0$$

Step 2: Complete the square for each variable.

  • For $x^2 - 2x$: Half of $-2$ is $-1$, and $(-1)^2 = 1$. Add and subtract $1$:$$x^2 - 2x + 1 - 1 = (x - 1)^2 - 1$$
  • For $-(y^2 - 2y)$: Half of $-2$ is $-1$, and $(-1)^2 = 1$. Add and subtract $1$ inside the parentheses:$$-(y^2 - 2y + 1 - 1) = -\left((y - 1)^2 - 1\right)$$
  • For $z^2 + 4z$: Half of $4$ is $2$, and $2^2 = 4$. Add and subtract $4$:$$z^2 + 4z + 4 - 4 = (z + 2)^2 - 4$$

Substituting these back into the equation gives:

$$(x^2 - 2x + 1) - 1 - (y^2 - 2y + 1 - 1) + (z^2 + 4z + 4) - 4 + 2 = 0$$$$(x - 1)^2 - 1 - \left((y - 1)^2 - 1\right) + (z + 2)^2 - 2 = 0$$

Step 3: Distribute the negative sign and simplify constants.

Distributing the minus sign across $-\left((y - 1)^2 - 1\right)$ yields $-(y - 1)^2 + 1$. Notice that $-1$ and $+1$ cancel out:

$$(x - 1)^2 - 1 - (y - 1)^2 + 1 + (z + 2)^2 - 2 = 0$$$$(x - 1)^2 - (y - 1)^2 + (z + 2)^2 = 2$$

Step 4: Divide by $2$ to obtain the standard form.

To put the quadric surface in standard form, the constant on the right-hand side must be $1$. Dividing every term by $2$:

$$\frac{(x - 1)^2}{2} - \frac{(y - 1)^2}{2} + \frac{(z + 2)^2}{2} = 1$$

Step 5: Identify the surface, center, and axis orientation.

  • All three variables are squared and the right-hand side equals $1$.
  • There are two positive terms and one negative term ($-\frac{(y-1)^2}{2}$), which is the signature of a Hyperboloid of One Sheet.
  • Setting each squared group to zero gives the shifted center: $x - 1 = 0 \implies x = 1$, $y - 1 = 0 \implies y = 1$, and $z + 2 = 0 \implies z = -2$. Thus, the center is $C(1, 1, -2)$.
  • Because the single negative sign belongs to the $y$-term, the axis of symmetry is parallel to the $y$-axis ($y$-direction).
Explanation

2. Radical Equations & Half-Quadric Surfaces

When an equation involves a principal square root $\sqrt{\cdots}$, squaring both sides produces a standard quadric surface equation, but we must preserve the sign restriction imposed by the square root:

  • Since $\sqrt{A} \ge 0$ by definition, an equation of the form $z - l = +\sqrt{A}$ restricts the surface to the upper half where $z \ge l$.
  • An equation of the form $z - l = -\sqrt{A}$ restricts the surface to the lower half where $z \le l$.

For a cone $z^2 = x^2 + y^2$, the full surface consists of two nappes (upper and lower cones meeting at the vertex).

Taking $z = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ isolates the upper cone ($z \ge 0$), whereas $z = -\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ isolates the lower cone ($z \le 0$).

Example

Question 9: Identifying a Radical Surface in 3D

Problem

Identify the surface given by the equation:

$$z = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$$
Final Answer

Upper half of a circular cone (upper cone) with vertex at the origin $(0, 0, 0)$, opening upward along the positive $z$-direction ($z \ge 0$).

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Note the sign restriction from the square root.

Because the principal square root is always non-negative ($\sqrt{x^2 + y^2} \ge 0$), we immediately have the restriction:

$$z \ge 0$$

Step 2: Square both sides to recognize the parent quadric surface.

$$z^2 = x^2 + y^2 \implies x^2 + y^2 - z^2 = 0$$

Step 3: Identify the surface and apply the restriction.

  • The equation $x^2 + y^2 - z^2 = 0$ is a circular cone centered at $(0, 0, 0)$ opening along the $z$-direction.
  • Because $z \ge 0$, only the portion above the $xy$-plane exists. Therefore, the surface is the upper circular cone (half-cone in the positive $z$-direction) with vertex at $(0, 0, 0)$.
Example

Question 10: Identifying a Negative Radical Surface in 3D

Problem

Identify the surface given by the equation:

$$z = -\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$$
Final Answer

Lower half of a circular cone (lower cone) with vertex at the origin $(0, 0, 0)$, opening downward along the negative $z$-direction ($z \le 0$).

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Note the sign restriction from the negative square root.

Since $\sqrt{x^2 + y^2} \ge 0$, multiplying by $-1$ implies:

$$z \le 0$$

Step 2: Square both sides.

$$z^2 = (-\sqrt{x^2 + y^2})^2 = x^2 + y^2 \implies x^2 + y^2 - z^2 = 0$$

Step 3: Identify the surface.

The parent equation $x^2 + y^2 - z^2 = 0$ is a circular cone along the $z$-axis, and the restriction $z \le 0$ selects only the bottom nappe.

Thus, the surface is the lower circular cone opening downward along the negative $z$-axis with vertex at $(0, 0, 0)$.

Example

Question 11: Identifying a Shifted Radical Quadric Surface

Problem

Identify the surface given by the equation:

$$z - 3 = \sqrt{1 + x^2 - y^2}$$
Final Answer

The upper half ($z \ge 3$) of a Hyperboloid of One Sheet centered at $C(0, 0, 3)$ and opening along the $x$-direction:

$$y^2 + (z - 3)^2 - x^2 = 1, \quad z \ge 3$$
Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Identify the domain/range restriction from the radical.

Since the right-hand side is a principal square root, the left-hand side must satisfy $z - 3 \ge 0$, which means $z \ge 3$.

Step 2: Square both sides of the equation.

$$(z - 3)^2 = 1 + x^2 - y^2$$

Step 3: Rearrange into standard quadric form.

Move all variable terms ($x^2$ and $-y^2$) to the left-hand side while keeping the constant $1$ on the right-hand side:

$$y^2 + (z - 3)^2 - x^2 = 1$$

Step 4: Identify the quadric surface, center, and orientation.

  • All three variables are squared and set equal to $1$, with two positive terms ($y^2$ and $(z-3)^2$) and one negative term ($-x^2$). This is a Hyperboloid of One Sheet.
  • The center is read from the shifts in $(x - 0)^2$, $(y - 0)^2$, and $(z - 3)^2$:$$C(0, 0, 3)$$
  • Because the negative sign is in front of $x^2$, the axis of the hyperboloid is parallel to the $x$-axis ($x$-direction).
  • Taking into account the radical restriction $z - 3 \ge 0$, this equation gives the upper half ($z \ge 3$) of the hyperboloid of one sheet centered at $C(0, 0, 3)$ along the $x$-direction.
Explanation

3. Deriving Quadric Surfaces from Geometric Distance Conditions

A classic exam question format defines a surface as the locus of all points $P(x, y, z)$ satisfying a relationship between distances to given points or planes.

To solve these problems:

  1. Distance between two points $P(x, y, z)$ and $P_1(x_1, y_1, z_1)$:$$d(P, P_1) = \sqrt{(x - x_1)^2 + (y - y_1)^2 + (z - z_1)^2}$$
  2. Distance from a point $P(x, y, z)$ to a plane $\pi: ax + by + cz + d = 0$ with normal vector $\vec{n} = \langle a, b, c \rangle$:$$d(P, \pi) = \frac{|ax + by + cz + d|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}}$$
  3. Square both sides to remove the square root and absolute value, then simplify into standard quadric form.
Example

Question 12: Locus of Points Equating Point Distance and Plane Distance

Problem

A surface consists of all points $P(x, y, z)$ such that the distance from $P$ to the point $(0, 1, 0)$ is twice the distance from $P$ to the plane $y = 1$. Identify this surface.

Final Answer

Circular Cone with vertex (center) at $C(0, 1, 0)$ opening along the $y$-direction, with equation:

$$x^2 - 3(y - 1)^2 + z^2 = 0$$
Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Translate the word problem into a distance equation.

Let $P(x, y, z)$ be any point on the surface, $P_1(0, 1, 0)$ be the given point, and $\pi$ be the plane $y = 1$. The problem states:

$$d(P, P_1) = 2 \, d(P, \pi)$$

Step 2: Write explicit formulas for both distances.

  • The distance from $P(x, y, z)$ to $P_1(0, 1, 0)$ is:$$d(P, P_1) = \sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 1)^2 + (z - 0)^2} = \sqrt{x^2 + (y - 1)^2 + z^2}$$
  • Write the plane $y = 1$ in general form $ax + by + cz + d = 0$:$$0x + 1y + 0z - 1 = 0 \quad \text{with normal vector } \vec{n} = \langle 0, 1, 0 \rangle$$Using the point-to-plane distance formula:$$d(P, \pi) = \frac{|0x + 1y + 0z - 1|}{\sqrt{0^2 + 1^2 + 0^2}} = \frac{|y - 1|}{1} = |y - 1|$$

Substituting both expressions into $d(P, P_1) = 2 \, d(P, \pi)$ gives:

$$\sqrt{x^2 + (y - 1)^2 + z^2} = 2 \frac{|y - 1|}{1}$$

Step 3: Square both sides and simplify into standard form.

Squaring both sides eliminates the radical on the left and the absolute value on the right:

$$x^2 + (y - 1)^2 + z^2 = 4(y - 1)^2$$

Note: Make sure not to expand $(y - 1)^2$, because it is already factored in shifted form! Simply subtract $4(y - 1)^2$ from both sides:

$$x^2 + (y - 1)^2 - 4(y - 1)^2 + z^2 = 0$$$$x^2 - 3(y - 1)^2 + z^2 = 0$$

Step 4: Identify the surface.

  • All three variables are squared, the right-hand side is $0$, and there are two positive terms ($x^2, z^2$) and one negative term ($-3(y - 1)^2$).
  • This is a Circular Cone with vertex/center at $C(0, 1, 0)$ opening along the $y$-direction (parallel to the $y$-axis).
Explanation

4. Geometric Regions Defined by Inequalities in 2D and 3D

When an equation $f(x, y, z) = c$ is replaced by an inequality ($\le, <, \ge, >$), it no longer represents just a thin surface shell—instead, it describes a region in 2D ($\mathbb{R}^2$) or a solid volume in 3D ($\mathbb{R}^3$).

Key Rules for Interpreting Inequalities

  • 2D vs. 3D Context: An inequality involving only $x$ and $y$, such as $x^2 + y^2 \le r^2$, describes a disk (inside or on a circle) in $\mathbb{R}^2$, but in $\mathbb{R}^3$ (where $z$ is unrestricted) it describes the solid region inside or on an infinite circular cylinder along the $z$-axis.
  • Non-Strict ($\le, \ge$) vs. Strict ($<, >$) Inequalities:
    • $\le$ or $\ge$: The boundary curve/surface is included (drawn with a solid line). We say "on or inside" / "on or outside".
    • $<$ or $>$: The boundary curve/surface is excluded (drawn with a dashed line). We say strictly "inside" or "outside".
  • Compound Inequalities ($A \le f(x,y,z) \le B$): Represents the region sandwiched between the two boundary surfaces $f(x,y,z) = A$ and $f(x,y,z) = B$.
Example

Inequalities Question 1: Region Defined by x² + y² ≤ 4

Problem

Sketch and identify the region described by the inequality (in both 2D and 3D):

$$x^2 + y^2 \le 4$$
Final Answer
  • In 2D ($\mathbb{R}^2$): The region on or inside the circle centered at $C(0, 0)$ with radius $r = 2$.
  • In 3D ($\mathbb{R}^3$): The solid region on or inside the circular cylinder centered along the $z$-axis with radius $r = 2$.
Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Identify the boundary equation.

Replacing $\le$ with $=$ gives $x^2 + y^2 = 4 = 2^2$.

Because the inequality is $\le$ (contains equality), the boundary is included and drawn as a solid circle passing through $(\pm 2, 0)$ and $(0, \pm 2)$.

Step 2: Interpret in 2D ($\mathbb{R}^2$).

In the $xy$-plane, $x^2 + y^2 \le 4$ consists of all points $(x, y)$ whose distance squared from the origin is at most $4$.

This is the closed disk on or inside the circle with center $C(0, 0)$ and radius $r = 2$.

Step 3: Interpret in 3D ($\mathbb{R}^3$).

In 3D space, the variable $z$ is missing, so $z$ can be any real number ($-\infty < z < \infty$).

The 2D disk is extruded infinitely up and down along the $z$-axis, forming the solid region on or inside the circular cylinder of radius $r = 2$ centered along the $z$-axis.

Example

Inequalities Question 2: Region Defined by x² + y² > 9

Problem

Sketch and identify the region described by the strict inequality (in both 2D and 3D):

$$x^2 + y^2 > 9$$
Final Answer
  • In 2D ($\mathbb{R}^2$): The region strictly outside the circle centered at $C(0, 0)$ with radius $r = 3$ (boundary dashed/excluded).
  • In 3D ($\mathbb{R}^3$): The region strictly outside the circular cylinder of radius $r = 3$ centered along the $z$-axis (boundary excluded).
Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Identify the boundary and strictness.

The boundary equation is $x^2 + y^2 = 9 = 3^2$, which has center $C(0, 0)$ and radius $r = 3$.

Because the inequality is strictly greater than ($>$) with no equality sign, points on the boundary $x^2 + y^2 = 9$ are not included.

When sketching, draw the circle of radius $3$ with a dashed line and shade the exterior.

Step 2: Interpret in 2D and 3D.

  • In 2D ($\mathbb{R}^2$): All points strictly outside the circle centered at $C(0, 0)$ with radius $r = 3$.
  • In 3D ($\mathbb{R}^3$): Since $z$ is unrestricted, this represents all points in 3D space strictly outside the circular cylinder of radius $r = 3$ centered along the $z$-axis.
Example

Inequalities Question 3: Region Defined by 1 ≤ x² + y² ≤ 4

Problem

Sketch and identify the region described by the compound inequality:

$$1 \le x^2 + y^2 \le 4$$
Final Answer

The region between (and on) the two circular cylinders (or circles in 2D) centered at $C(0, 0)$ with inner radius $r_1 = 1$ and outer radius $r_2 = 2$.

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Identify the inner and outer boundaries.

  • Inner boundary: $x^2 + y^2 = 1 \implies$ circle/cylinder with center $C(0, 0)$ and radius $r = 1$.
  • Outer boundary: $x^2 + y^2 = 4 \implies$ circle/cylinder with center $C(0, 0)$ and radius $r = 2$.

Both inequalities are $\le$, so both the inner boundary ($r = 1$) and outer boundary ($r = 2$) are drawn with solid lines and included in the region.

Step 2: Describe the shaded region.

  • In 2D ($\mathbb{R}^2$): An annulus (ring) consisting of all points on or between the circle of radius $r = 1$ and the circle of radius $r = 2$ centered at $(0, 0)$.
  • In 3D ($\mathbb{R}^3$): A hollow cylindrical pipe (thick cylindrical shell) consisting of all points between (and on) the cylinder of radius $r = 1$, $C(0, 0)$ and the cylinder of radius $r = 2$, $C(0, 0)$ along the $z$-axis.
Example

Inequalities Question 4: Region Defined by x² + y² + z² ≤ 9

Problem

Identify the 3D solid region described by the inequality:

$$x^2 + y^2 + z^2 \le 9$$
Final Answer

All points on or inside the sphere of radius $r = 3$ centered at the origin $C(0, 0, 0)$ (a closed solid ball).

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Identify the boundary surface.

Setting $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 9 = 3^2$ gives a sphere in $\mathbb{R}^3$ centered at $C(0, 0, 0)$ with radius $r = 3$.

Step 2: Interpret the inequality $\le 9$.

  • The $< 9$ part includes all points whose distance from the origin $(0,0,0)$ is strictly less than $3$ (the interior of the sphere).
  • The $= 9$ part includes the spherical shell itself.
  • Combining them: the inequality represents all points on or inside the sphere with radius $r = 3$ and center $C(0, 0, 0)$.
Example

Inequalities Question 5: Region Defined by √(x² + y²) ≤ z ≤ 4

Problem

Sketch and identify the 3D solid region described by the inequality:

$$\sqrt{x^2 + y^2} \le z \le 4$$
Final Answer

The solid region between (and on) the upper circular cone $z = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ below and the horizontal plane $z = 4$ above.

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Break the compound inequality into its lower and upper bounding surfaces in $z$.

  • Lower bound for $z$: $z \ge \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$. From Question 9, the surface $z = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ is the upper circular cone with vertex at $(0, 0, 0)$ opening upward along the positive $z$-axis. The inequality $z \ge \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ represents all points on or above (inside) this upper cone.
  • Upper bound for $z$: $z \le 4$. The surface $z = 4$ is a horizontal plane parallel to the $xy$-plane at height $z = 4$. The inequality $z \le 4$ represents all points on or below this plane.

Step 2: Combine both bounds to sketch and identify the 3D solid.

The solid is bounded below by the conical surface $z = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ and capped above by the flat circular disk on the plane $z = 4$ (where the cone intersects the plane in a circle of radius $4$).

Thus, it is the solid region between (and including) the upper cone $z = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$ and the plane $z = 4$.

LECTURE NOTES • TOPIC 06 OF 12

13.1 Functions of Several Variables & Level Curves

Syllabus 13.1 — Domain, Range, 3D Graphing & Level Curves (Contour Maps)

Jump to idea:
Explanation

1. Domain and Range of Functions of Two Variables

A function $f$ of two variables assigns to each ordered pair $(x, y)$ in a set $D \subset \mathbb{R}^2$ a unique real number $z = f(x, y)$.

The set $D$ is the domain of $f$, and the set of all resulting values $\{f(x, y) \mid (x, y) \in D\}$ is the range.

When finding the natural domain, apply the standard algebraic restrictions:

  • Even roots: The expression under $\sqrt{g(x, y)}$ must satisfy $g(x, y) \ge 0$.
  • Fractions: The denominator $\frac{1}{g(x, y)}$ cannot be zero: $g(x, y) \neq 0$.
  • Logarithms: The argument of $\ln(g(x, y))$ must be strictly positive: $g(x, y) > 0$.
  • Inverse trig: For $\arcsin(u)$ or $\arccos(u)$, $-1 \le u \le 1$.
Example

Worked Example 1: Finding and Graphing the Domain in the xy-Plane

Problem

Find and sketch the domain of the function:

$$f(x, y) = \frac{\sqrt{9 - x^2 - y^2}}{\ln(x + y)}$$
Final Answer

Domain: $D = \{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \mid x^2 + y^2 \le 9, \; x + y > 0, \; x + y \neq 1\}$

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Identify all mathematical restrictions.

1. Square root numerator: The radicand must be non-negative:

$$9 - x^2 - y^2 \ge 0 \implies x^2 + y^2 \le 9$$

This represents the closed circular disk centered at $(0, 0)$ with radius $R = 3$ (including the boundary circle $x^2 + y^2 = 9$).

2. Natural logarithm in denominator: The argument must be strictly positive:

$$x + y > 0 \implies y > -x$$

This is the strict open half-plane strictly above the dashed line $y = -x$.

3. Non-zero denominator: The denominator cannot equal zero:

$$\ln(x + y) \neq 0 \implies x + y \neq 1 \implies y \neq -x + 1$$

This removes the entire dashed line $y = -x + 1$ from the domain.

Step 2: Combine all domain conditions.

The domain consists of all points inside and on the boundary of the circle $x^2 + y^2 = 9$ that lie strictly above the line $y = -x$, excluding the line $y = 1 - x$.

Explanation

2. Level Curves and Contour Maps

The level curves of a function $f(x, y)$ are the 2D curves with equations:

$$f(x, y) = k$$

where $k$ is a constant in the range of $f$.

Geometrically, a level curve $f(x, y) = k$ is the horizontal slice of the 3D surface $z = f(x, y)$ cut by the horizontal plane $z = k$, projected down onto the $xy$-plane.

A collection of level curves for evenly spaced values of $k$ is called a contour map.

Where contour lines are closely spaced, the surface is steep; where they are widely spaced, the surface is relatively flat.

Example

Worked Example 2: Sketching and Identifying Level Curves

Problem

Describe the level curves of the function $f(x, y) = \sqrt{16 - x^2 - 4y^2}$ for $k = 0, 2, \sqrt{12}$.

Final Answer

For $k = 0$: The ellipse $\frac{x^2}{16} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1$.
For $k = 2$: The smaller ellipse $\frac{x^2}{12} + \frac{y^2}{3} = 1$.
For $k = \sqrt{12}$: The ellipse $\frac{x^2}{4} + y^2 = 1$.
All level curves are concentric ellipses centered at $(0, 0)$.

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Set up the level curve equation $f(x, y) = k$.

$$\sqrt{16 - x^2 - 4y^2} = k$$

Square both sides (valid for $0 \le k \le 4$):

$$16 - x^2 - 4y^2 = k^2 \implies x^2 + 4y^2 = 16 - k^2$$

Step 2: Evaluate for each given value of $k$.

1. For $k = 0$:

$$x^2 + 4y^2 = 16 \implies \frac{x^2}{16} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1$$

This is an ellipse centered at $(0, 0)$ with semi-major axis $a = 4$ along the $x$-axis and semi-minor axis $b = 2$ along the $y$-axis.

2. For $k = 2$:

$$x^2 + 4y^2 = 16 - 2^2 = 12 \implies \frac{x^2}{12} + \frac{y^2}{3} = 1$$

This is an ellipse with semi-axes $a = \sqrt{12} = 2\sqrt{3}$ and $b = \sqrt{3}$.

3. For $k = \sqrt{12}$:

$$x^2 + 4y^2 = 16 - 12 = 4 \implies \frac{x^2}{4} + y^2 = 1$$

This is an ellipse with semi-axes $a = 2$ and $b = 1$.

LECTURE NOTES • TOPIC 07 OF 12

13.2 Limits & Continuity of Multivariable Functions

Syllabus 13.2 — Evaluating Limits, Two-Path Test for Non-Existence & Continuity

Jump to idea:
Explanation

1. The Two-Path Test for Non-Existence of Limits

In single-variable calculus $\lim_{x \to a} f(x)$, $x$ can only approach $a$ from two directions: left or right.

In multivariable calculus $\lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} f(x, y)$, $(x, y)$ can approach $(a, b)$ along infinitely many curves, lines, and directions.

The Two-Path Test: If $f(x, y) \to L_1$ along one path $C_1$ and $f(x, y) \to L_2$ along another path $C_2$, with $L_1 \neq L_2$, then:

$$\lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} f(x, y) \quad \text{does not exist (DNE)}.$$

Common test paths to check as $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$:

  • Along the $x$-axis ($y = 0$).
  • Along the $y$-axis ($x = 0$).
  • Along lines $y = mx$.
  • Along parabolas $y = kx^2$ or $x = ky^2$ (used when degree balancing is required).
Example

Worked Example 1: Evaluating Multivariable Limit at the Origin

Problem

Determine whether the limit exists or show that it does not exist:

$$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{x y^2}{x^2 + y^4}$$
Final Answer

The limit does not exist (DNE).

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Test along linear paths $y = mx$.

Substitute $y = mx$ into the limit as $x \to 0$:

$$\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x (mx)^2}{x^2 + (mx)^4} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{m^2 x^3}{x^2(1 + m^4 x^2)} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{m^2 x}{1 + m^4 x^2} = \frac{0}{1} = 0$$

Along every straight line through the origin, the limit is $0$. However, this does not prove the limit is $0$!

Step 2: Balance the degrees in the denominator.

Notice the denominator has $x^2$ and $y^4$. If we set $x = ky^2$, then $x^2 = k^2 y^4$, making both terms in the denominator of degree $4$!

Substitute the parabolic path $x = ky^2$ as $y \to 0$:

$$\lim_{y \to 0} \frac{(k y^2) y^2}{(k y^2)^2 + y^4} = \lim_{y \to 0} \frac{k y^4}{k^2 y^4 + y^4} = \lim_{y \to 0} \frac{k}{k^2 + 1} = \frac{k}{k^2 + 1}$$

Step 3: Conclude by the Two-Path Test.

For $k = 1$ (path $x = y^2$): limit is $\frac{1}{1^2+1} = \frac{1}{2}$.

For $k = 0$ (path $x = 0$): limit is $0$.

Since different paths yield different limits ($ rac{1}{2} \neq 0$), by the Two-Path Test, the limit does not exist (DNE).

Explanation

2. Proving Limits Exist (Polar Coordinates & Squeeze Theorem)

To prove that a limit $\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} f(x, y) = L$ actually exists, we cannot just test paths. Two standard techniques are:

1. Polar Coordinates: Substitute $x = r\cos\theta, y = r\sin\theta$.

Since $(x, y) \to (0, 0) \iff r \to 0^+$, if the expression simplifies to $g(r) h(\theta)$ where $h(\theta)$ is bounded and $\lim_{r \to 0^+} g(r) = 0$, then the limit is $0$.

2. The Squeeze (Sandwich) Theorem: If $g(x, y) \le f(x, y) \le h(x, y)$ and $\lim g = \lim h = L$, then $\lim f = L$.

Example

Worked Example 2: Evaluating a Rational Multivariable Limit

Problem

Find the limit if it exists, or show that it does not exist:

$$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{3x^3 - y^3}{x^2 + y^2}$$
Final Answer

The limit exists and equals $0$.

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Convert to polar coordinates.

Set $x = r\cos\theta$ and $y = r\sin\theta$, so that $x^2 + y^2 = r^2$. As $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$, $r \to 0^+$:

$$\lim_{r \to 0^+} \frac{3(r\cos\theta)^3 - (r\sin\theta)^3}{r^2} = \lim_{r \to 0^+} \frac{r^3 (3\cos^3\theta - \sin^3\theta)}{r^2} = \lim_{r \to 0^+} r (3\cos^3\theta - \sin^3\theta)$$

Step 2: Apply the Squeeze Theorem.

Notice that $|3\cos^3\theta - \sin^3\theta| \le 3|\cos\theta|^3 + |\sin\theta|^3 \le 3(1) + 1 = 4$.

Therefore:

$$0 \le |r (3\cos^3\theta - \sin^3\theta)| \le 4r$$

As $r \to 0^+$, $4r \to 0$. By the Squeeze Theorem:

$$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{3x^3 - y^3}{x^2 + y^2} = 0$$
LECTURE NOTES • TOPIC 08 OF 12

13.3 Partial Derivatives & Clairaut's Theorem

Syllabus 13.3 — First-Order Partials, Geometric Slopes & Mixed Second-Order Derivatives

Jump to idea:
Explanation

1. First-Order Partial Derivatives

For a function $z = f(x, y)$, the partial derivative with respect to $x$, denoted $\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}$ or $f_x$, is found by treating $y$ as a constant and differentiating with respect to $x$:

$$f_x(x, y) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x + h, y) - f(x, y)}{h}$$

Similarly, the partial derivative with respect to $y$, denoted $\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}$ or $f_y$, is found by treating $x$ as a constant and differentiating with respect to $y$:

$$f_y(x, y) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x, y + h) - f(x, y)}{h}$$

Geometric Meaning: $f_x(x_0, y_0)$ is the slope of the tangent line to the surface $z = f(x, y)$ in the plane $y = y_0$ (parallel to the $xz$-plane).

Example

Worked Example 1: Differentiating with Product & Chain Rules

Problem

Find $f_x$ and $f_y$ for the function:

$$f(x, y) = x^3 y^2 + e^{x^2 y} + \sin(2x - 3y)$$
Final Answer

$f_x = 3x^2 y^2 + 2xy e^{x^2 y} + 2\cos(2x - 3y)$
$f_y = 2x^3 y + x^2 e^{x^2 y} - 3\cos(2x - 3y)$

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Compute $f_x$ (treat $y$ as a constant).

$$\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^3 y^2) = 3x^2 y^2$$$$\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(e^{x^2 y}) = e^{x^2 y} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2 y) = 2xy e^{x^2 y}$$$$\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(\sin(2x - 3y)) = \cos(2x - 3y) \cdot 2 = 2\cos(2x - 3y)$$$$f_x = 3x^2 y^2 + 2xy e^{x^2 y} + 2\cos(2x - 3y)$$

Step 2: Compute $f_y$ (treat $x$ as a constant).

$$\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^3 y^2) = 2x^3 y$$$$\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(e^{x^2 y}) = e^{x^2 y} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x^2 y) = x^2 e^{x^2 y}$$$$\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(\sin(2x - 3y)) = \cos(2x - 3y) \cdot (-3) = -3\cos(2x - 3y)$$$$f_y = 2x^3 y + x^2 e^{x^2 y} - 3\cos(2x - 3y)$$

Explanation

2. Second-Order Partial Derivatives & Clairaut's Theorem

A function $f(x, y)$ has four second-order partial derivatives:

$$f_{xx} = \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x^2}, \quad f_{yy} = \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial y^2}, \quad f_{xy} = (f_x)_y = \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial y \partial x}, \quad f_{yx} = (f_y)_x = \frac{\partial^2 f}{\partial x \partial y}$$

Clairaut's Theorem (Equality of Mixed Partials): If $f(x, y)$ and its partial derivatives $f_x, f_y, f_{xy}, f_{yx}$ are continuous on an open disk containing $(a, b)$, then:

$$f_{xy}(a, b) = f_{yx}(a, b)$$
Example

Worked Example 2: Verifying Mixed Partials Equality

Problem

Verify that $f_{xy} = f_{yx}$ for $f(x, y) = x \ln(x + 2y)$.

Final Answer

$f_{xy} = f_{yx} = \frac{2}{(x + 2y)^2}$

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Compute first partials $f_x$ and $f_y$.

$$f_x = 1 \cdot \ln(x + 2y) + x \cdot \frac{1}{x + 2y} = \ln(x + 2y) + \frac{x}{x + 2y}$$$$f_y = x \cdot \frac{2}{x + 2y} = \frac{2x}{x + 2y}$$

Step 2: Differentiate $f_x$ with respect to $y$ to get $f_{xy}$.

$$f_{xy} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\ln(x + 2y) + x(x + 2y)^{-1}\right) = \frac{2}{x + 2y} - x(x + 2y)^{-2}(2) = \frac{2(x + 2y) - 2x}{(x + 2y)^2} = \frac{4y}{(x + 2y)^2}$$

Step 3: Differentiate $f_y$ with respect to $x$ to get $f_{yx}$.

$$f_{yx} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{2x}{x + 2y}\right) = \frac{2(x + 2y) - 2x(1)}{(x + 2y)^2} = \frac{2x + 4y - 2x}{(x + 2y)^2} = \frac{4y}{(x + 2y)^2}$$

Since both equal $\frac{4y}{(x + 2y)^2}$, Clairaut's Theorem is verified!

LECTURE NOTES • TOPIC 09 OF 12

13.4 Differentials & Linear Approximations

Syllabus 13.4 — Total Differential, Tangent Plane Approximations & Error Estimation

Jump to idea:
Explanation

1. Linear Approximation & The Total Differential

The linear approximation (or tangent plane approximation) of a differentiable function $f(x, y)$ near $(x_0, y_0)$ is:

$$L(x, y) = f(x_0, y_0) + f_x(x_0, y_0)(x - x_0) + f_y(x_0, y_0)(y - y_0)$$

The total differential $dz$ is defined in terms of the independent differentials $dx = \Delta x$ and $dy = \Delta y$ by:

$$dz = f_x(x, y) dx + f_y(x, y) dy = \frac{\partial z}{\partial x} dx + \frac{\partial z}{\partial y} dy$$

For small changes $\Delta x$ and $\Delta y$, the true increment $\Delta z = f(x_0 + \Delta x, y_0 + \Delta y) - f(x_0, y_0)$ is closely approximated by the differential:

$$\Delta z \approx dz$$
Example

Worked Example 1: Estimating Values via Tangent Plane Approximation

Problem

Use linear approximation to estimate the value of:

$$f(x, y) = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2} \quad \text{at } (3.04, \; 3.98)$$
Final Answer

$L(3.04, 3.98) = 5.008$

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Choose the convenient base point $(x_0, y_0)$.

Select $(x_0, y_0) = (3, 4)$, where $f(3, 4) = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{25} = 5$.

Step 2: Determine $\Delta x$ and $\Delta y$.

$$dx = \Delta x = 3.04 - 3 = 0.04$$$$dy = \Delta y = 3.98 - 4 = -0.02$$

Step 3: Compute first partial derivatives at $(3, 4)$.

$$f_x(x, y) = \frac{2x}{2\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}} = \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}} \implies f_x(3, 4) = \frac{3}{5} = 0.6$$$$f_y(x, y) = \frac{2y}{2\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}} = \frac{y}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}} \implies f_y(3, 4) = \frac{4}{5} = 0.8$$

Step 4: Calculate $dz$ and the approximation.

$$dz = f_x \Delta x + f_y \Delta y = (0.6)(0.04) + (0.8)(-0.02) = 0.024 - 0.016 = 0.008$$$$f(3.04, 3.98) \approx f(3, 4) + dz = 5 + 0.008 = 5.008$$
LECTURE NOTES • TOPIC 10 OF 12

13.5 The Multivariable Chain Rule

Syllabus 13.5 — Tree Diagrams, Intermediate Variables & Implicit Differentiation Formula

Jump to idea:
Explanation

1. The Multivariable Chain Rule & Tree Diagrams

Case 1 (One Independent Variable): If $z = f(x, y)$ is differentiable and $x = g(t), y = h(t)$ are differentiable functions of $t$, then:

$$\frac{dz}{dt} = \frac{\partial z}{\partial x} \frac{dx}{dt} + \frac{\partial z}{\partial y} \frac{dy}{dt}$$

Case 2 (Two Independent Variables): If $z = f(x, y)$ where $x = g(s, t)$ and $y = h(s, t)$, then:

$$\frac{\partial z}{\partial s} = \frac{\partial z}{\partial x} \frac{\partial x}{\partial s} + \frac{\partial z}{\partial y} \frac{\partial y}{\partial s}, \quad \frac{\partial z}{\partial t} = \frac{\partial z}{\partial x} \frac{\partial x}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial z}{\partial y} \frac{\partial y}{\partial t}$$

Rule of Thumb (Tree Diagram): Sum over all paths from the top dependent variable to the target independent variable, multiplying along each branch.

Example

Worked Example 1: Multivariable Chain Rule with Two Parameters

Problem

Given $z = x^2 y - y^3$, where $x = s \cos t$ and $y = s \sin t$, find $\frac{\partial z}{\partial s}$ and $\frac{\partial z}{\partial t}$ at $(s, t) = (2, 0)$.

Final Answer

At $(s, t) = (2, 0)$: $\frac{\partial z}{\partial s} = 0$, and $\frac{\partial z}{\partial t} = 8$.

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Evaluate $x$ and $y$ at $(s, t) = (2, 0)$.

$$x = 2\cos(0) = 2, \quad y = 2\sin(0) = 0$$

Step 2: Compute partials of $z$ at $(x, y) = (2, 0)$.

$$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = 2xy \implies \frac{\partial z}{\partial x}(2, 0) = 2(2)(0) = 0$$$$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = x^2 - 3y^2 \implies \frac{\partial z}{\partial y}(2, 0) = 2^2 - 3(0)^2 = 4$$

Step 3: Compute partials of $x$ and $y$ with respect to $s$ and $t$.

$$\frac{\partial x}{\partial s} = \cos t = \cos(0) = 1, \quad \frac{\partial y}{\partial s} = \sin t = \sin(0) = 0$$$$\frac{\partial x}{\partial t} = -s\sin t = -2\sin(0) = 0, \quad \frac{\partial y}{\partial t} = s\cos t = 2\cos(0) = 2$$

Step 4: Apply Chain Rule formulas.

$$\frac{\partial z}{\partial s} = (0)(1) + (4)(0) = 0$$$$\frac{\partial z}{\partial t} = (0)(0) + (4)(2) = 8$$
Explanation

2. Implicit Differentiation Shortcut Formula

If an equation $F(x, y) = 0$ implicitly defines $y$ as a differentiable function of $x$, then differentiating implicitly yields:

$$\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{F_x}{F_y} \quad (F_y \neq 0)$$

Similarly, if $F(x, y, z) = 0$ defines $z$ as a function of $x$ and $y$ ($z = f(x, y)$), then:

$$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = -\frac{F_x}{F_z}, \quad \frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = -\frac{F_y}{F_z} \quad (F_z \neq 0)$$
Example

Worked Example 2: Finding Partial Derivatives of an Implicit Surface

Problem

Find $\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}$ and $\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}$ for the implicit surface equation:

$$x^3 + y^3 + z^3 + 6xyz = 1$$
Final Answer

$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = -\frac{x^2 + 2yz}{z^2 + 2xy}$, $\quad \frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = -\frac{y^2 + 2xz}{z^2 + 2xy}$

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Define $F(x, y, z) = 0$.

$$F(x, y, z) = x^3 + y^3 + z^3 + 6xyz - 1 = 0$$

Step 2: Compute first partials of $F$.

$$F_x = 3x^2 + 6yz = 3(x^2 + 2yz)$$$$F_y = 3y^2 + 6xz = 3(y^2 + 2xz)$$$$F_z = 3z^2 + 6xy = 3(z^2 + 2xy)$$

Step 3: Apply the implicit differentiation formulas.

$$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = -\frac{F_x}{F_z} = -\frac{3(x^2 + 2yz)}{3(z^2 + 2xy)} = -\frac{x^2 + 2yz}{z^2 + 2xy}$$$$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = -\frac{F_y}{F_z} = -\frac{3(y^2 + 2xz)}{3(z^2 + 2xy)} = -\frac{y^2 + 2xz}{z^2 + 2xy}$$
LECTURE NOTES • TOPIC 11 OF 12

13.6 Directional Derivatives & Gradients

Syllabus 13.6 — The Gradient Vector, Directional Derivative in Any Direction & Maximum Rate of Change

Jump to idea:
Explanation

1. The Gradient Vector & Directional Derivative Formula

The gradient of a function $f(x, y)$ is the vector function:

$$\nabla f(x, y) = \langle f_x(x, y), \; f_y(x, y) \rangle = f_x \mathbf{i} + f_y \mathbf{j}$$

The directional derivative of $f$ at $(x_0, y_0)$ in the direction of a unit vector $\mathbf{u} = \langle u_1, u_2 \rangle$ is the rate of change of $f$ in that direction:

$$D_{\mathbf{u}} f(x_0, y_0) = \nabla f(x_0, y_0) \cdot \mathbf{u} = f_x(x_0, y_0) u_1 + f_y(x_0, y_0) u_2$$

Critical Rule: The direction vector MUST be normalized to a unit vector $\mathbf{u} = \frac{\mathbf{v}}{\|\mathbf{v}\|}$ before taking the dot product!

Example

Worked Example 1: Directional Derivative in the Direction of a Vector

Problem

Find the directional derivative of $f(x, y) = x^2 y^3 - 4y$ at $P(2, -1)$ in the direction of $\mathbf{v} = 2\mathbf{i} + 5\mathbf{j}$.

Final Answer

$D_{\mathbf{u}} f(2, -1) = \frac{28}{\sqrt{29}}$

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Compute the gradient vector $\nabla f(x, y)$.

$$f_x(x, y) = 2x y^3 \implies f_x(2, -1) = 2(2)(-1)^3 = -4$$$$f_y(x, y) = 3x^2 y^2 - 4 \implies f_y(2, -1) = 3(2)^2(-1)^2 - 4 = 12 - 4 = 8$$$$\nabla f(2, -1) = \langle -4, 8 \rangle$$

Step 2: Find the unit vector $\mathbf{u}$ in the direction of $\mathbf{v}$.

$$\|\mathbf{v}\| = \sqrt{2^2 + 5^2} = \sqrt{4 + 25} = \sqrt{29}$$$$\mathbf{u} = \frac{\mathbf{v}}{\|\mathbf{v}\|} = \left\langle \frac{2}{\sqrt{29}}, \; \frac{5}{\sqrt{29}} \right\rangle$$

Step 3: Calculate the dot product $D_{\mathbf{u}} f = \nabla f \cdot \mathbf{u}$.

$$D_{\mathbf{u}} f(2, -1) = (-4)\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{29}}\right) + (8)\left(\frac{5}{\sqrt{29}}\right) = \frac{-8 + 40}{\sqrt{29}} = \frac{32}{\sqrt{29}}$$

Explanation

2. Maximizing the Directional Derivative

By the geometric dot product: $D_{\mathbf{u}} f = \|\nabla f\| \|\mathbf{u}\| \cos\theta = \|\nabla f\| \cos\theta$.

Since $-1 \le \cos\theta \le 1$:

  • Maximum Rate of Change: Occurs when $\cos\theta = 1$ (same direction as $\nabla f$). Its value is $\|\nabla f(x_0, y_0)\|$.
  • Minimum Rate of Change (Steepest Descent): Occurs when $\cos\theta = -1$ (direction of $-\nabla f$). Its value is $-\|\nabla f(x_0, y_0)\|$.
  • Zero Rate of Change: Occurs in directions orthogonal to $\nabla f$ (tangent to the level curve).
Example

Worked Example 2: Finding Maximum Rate of Change and its Direction

Problem

Given $f(x, y, z) = \frac{x}{y} + \frac{y}{z}$ at the point $P(4, 2, 1)$:

  1. Find the maximum rate of change of $f$ at $P$.
  2. Find the direction in which this maximum rate of change occurs.
Final Answer

Direction: $\mathbf{u} = \left\langle \frac{1}{5}, -\frac{3}{10}, -\frac{4}{5} \right\rangle$
Maximum Rate of Change: $\|\nabla f\| = \frac{\sqrt{101}}{4} \approx 2.51$

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Compute the gradient $\nabla f(x, y, z)$.

$$f_x = \frac{1}{y} \implies f_x(4, 2, 1) = \frac{1}{2}$$

$$f_y = -\frac{x}{y^2} + \frac{1}{z} \implies f_y(4, 2, 1) = -\frac{4}{4} + \frac{1}{1} = -1 + 1 = 0$$

$$f_z = -\frac{y}{z^2} \implies f_z(4, 2, 1) = -\frac{2}{1} = -2$$

$$\nabla f(4, 2, 1) = \langle 0.5, \; 0, \; -2 \rangle = \left\langle \frac{1}{2}, 0, -2 \right\rangle$$

Step 2: Maximum rate of change.

$$\|\nabla f\| = \sqrt{\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^2 + 0^2 + (-2)^2} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{4} + 4} = \sqrt{\frac{17}{4}} = \frac{\sqrt{17}}{2}$$

Step 3: Direction of maximum change (unit vector in gradient direction).

$$\mathbf{u} = \frac{\nabla f}{\|\nabla f\|} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{17}} \left\langle \frac{1}{2}, 0, -2 \right\rangle = \left\langle \frac{1}{\sqrt{17}}, 0, -\frac{4}{\sqrt{17}} \right\rangle$$
LECTURE NOTES • TOPIC 12 OF 12

13.7 Tangent Planes & Normal Lines to Surfaces

Syllabus 13.7 — Gradient as Normal Vector, Tangent Plane Equations & Normal Lines to Level Surfaces

Jump to idea:
Explanation

1. Tangent Planes and Normal Lines to Level Surfaces F(x, y, z) = k

For any smooth surface represented as a level surface $F(x, y, z) = k$, the gradient vector $\nabla F(x_0, y_0, z_0)$ is always perpendicular (normal) to the tangent plane of the surface at $(x_0, y_0, z_0)$.

Therefore, the normal vector is:

$$\mathbf{n} = \nabla F(x_0, y_0, z_0) = \langle F_x(x_0, y_0, z_0), \; F_y(x_0, y_0, z_0), \; F_z(x_0, y_0, z_0) \rangle$$

Equation of the Tangent Plane:

$$F_x(x_0, y_0, z_0)(x - x_0) + F_y(x_0, y_0, z_0)(y - y_0) + F_z(x_0, y_0, z_0)(z - z_0) = 0$$

Symmetric Equations of the Normal Line:

$$\frac{x - x_0}{F_x(x_0, y_0, z_0)} = \frac{y - y_0}{F_y(x_0, y_0, z_0)} = \frac{z - z_0}{F_z(x_0, y_0, z_0)}$$

Example

Worked Example 1: Tangent Plane and Normal Line to a Surface

Problem

Find the equations of the tangent plane and the normal line to the ellipsoid $\frac{x^2}{4} + y^2 + \frac{z^2}{9} = 3$ at the point $P(-2, 1, -3)$.

Final Answer

Tangent plane: $-x + 2y - \frac{2}{3}z - 6 = 0 \iff 3x - 6y + 2z + 18 = 0$
Normal line: $\frac{x + 2}{-1} = \frac{y - 1}{2} = \frac{z + 3}{-2/3}$

Step-by-Step Solution

Step 1: Define the level surface function $F(x, y, z)$.

$$F(x, y, z) = \frac{x^2}{4} + y^2 + \frac{z^2}{9} - 3 = 0$$

Step 2: Compute the gradient vector $\nabla F$ at $P(-2, 1, -3)$.

$$F_x = \frac{2x}{4} = \frac{x}{2} \implies F_x(-2, 1, -3) = -1$$$$F_y = 2y \implies F_y(-2, 1, -3) = 2(1) = 2$$$$F_z = \frac{2z}{9} \implies F_z(-2, 1, -3) = -\frac{6}{9} = -\frac{2}{3}$$

The normal vector is $\mathbf{n} = \left\langle -1, 2, -\frac{2}{3} \right\rangle$, or multiplying by $-3$: $\mathbf{n} = \langle 3, -6, 2 \rangle$.

Step 3: Write the equation of the tangent plane.

$$3(x - (-2)) - 6(y - 1) + 2(z - (-3)) = 0$$$$3(x + 2) - 6(y - 1) + 2(z + 3) = 0$$$$3x + 6 - 6y + 6 + 2z + 6 = 0 \implies 3x - 6y + 2z + 18 = 0$$

Step 4: Write the symmetric equations of the normal line.

$$\frac{x + 2}{3} = \frac{y - 1}{-6} = \frac{z + 3}{2}$$

11.5 Lines in Space & Skew Lines

Suggested Textbook Practice Problems (KFUPM Exam Aligned) • 14 Curated Exercises

Jump to problem:
Ex 5 Easy Lines in SpaceParametric Equations
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 5: Checking Points on a Parametric Line

🎯 High — tests whether points lie on a line by evaluating consistency of parameter t across all three spatial coordinates.
Problem Statement

Determine whether each point lies on the line given by the parametric equations:

$$x = -2 + t, \quad y = 3t, \quad z = 4 + t$$
  1. $(0, 6, 6)$
  2. $(2, 3, 5)$
  3. $(-4, -6, 2)$
Boxed Final Answer
  • (a) Yes, the point $(0, 6, 6)$ lies on the line (for $t = 2$).
  • (b) No, the point $(2, 3, 5)$ does not lie on the line ($y = 12 \neq 3$ when $t = 4$).
  • (c) Yes, the point $(-4, -6, 2)$ lies on the line (for $t = -2$).

To determine if a given point $(x, y, z)$ lies on the line, solve for the parameter $t$ from the $x$-equation and test whether that same $t$ satisfies the $y$- and $z$-equations.

Part (a): Checking $(0, 6, 6)$

  1. Set $x = 0$:$$0 = -2 + t \implies t = 2$$
  2. Substitute $t = 2$ into the equations for $y$ and $z$:$$y = 3(2) = 6$$$$z = 4 + 2 = 6$$
  3. Both coordinates match the point $(0, 6, 6)$. Therefore, $(0, 6, 6)$ lies on the line.

Part (b): Checking $(2, 3, 5)$

  1. Set $x = 2$:$$2 = -2 + t \implies t = 4$$
  2. Substitute $t = 4$ into the equation for $y$:$$y = 3(4) = 12 \neq 3$$
  3. The calculated $y$-value does not match $3$. Therefore, $(2, 3, 5)$ does not lie on the line.

Part (c): Checking $(-4, -6, 2)$

  1. Set $x = -4$: $$-4 = -2 + t \implies t = -2$$
  2. Substitute $t = -2$ into the equations for $y$ and $z$:$$y = 3(-2) = -6$$$$z = 4 + (-2) = 2$$
  3. Both coordinates match the point $(-4, -6, 2)$. Therefore, $(-4, -6, 2)$ lies on the line.
Ex 9 Easy Lines in SpaceParametric Equations
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 9: Parametric and Symmetric Equations Through Given Point

🎯 High — writing parametric and symmetric equations given a point and parallel vector is a standard KFUPM Math 201 Exam 1 question.
Problem Statement

Find sets of (a) parametric equations and (b) symmetric equations of the line that passes through the point $(-2, 0, 3)$ and is parallel to the vector $\mathbf{v} = 2\mathbf{i} + 4\mathbf{j} - 2\mathbf{k}$. Write the direction numbers as integers.

Boxed Final Answer
  • (a) Parametric Equations:$$x = -2 + 2t, \quad y = 4t, \quad z = 3 - 2t$$(or using reduced direction numbers: $x = -2 + t, \; y = 2t, \; z = 3 - t$)
  • (b) Symmetric Equations:$$\frac{x + 2}{2} = \frac{y}{4} = \frac{z - 3}{-2} \quad \left(\text{or } \frac{x + 2}{1} = \frac{y}{2} = \frac{z - 3}{-1}\right)$$

A line in $\mathbb{R}^3$ passing through $P(x_0, y_0, z_0)$ parallel to $\mathbf{v} = \langle a, b, c \rangle$ has parametric equations:

$$x = x_0 + at, \quad y = y_0 + bt, \quad z = z_0 + ct$$

and symmetric equations (assuming nonzero direction numbers):

$$\frac{x - x_0}{a} = \frac{y - y_0}{b} = \frac{z - z_0}{c}$$

Step 1: Identify Point and Direction Numbers

  • Point: $(x_0, y_0, z_0) = (-2, 0, 3)$
  • Parallel vector: $\mathbf{v} = 2\mathbf{i} + 4\mathbf{j} - 2\mathbf{k} = \langle 2, 4, -2 \rangle$
  • Direction numbers: $a = 2, \; b = 4, \; c = -2$ (or reduced by factor 2: $a = 1, \; b = 2, \; c = -1$)

Step 2: Construct Equations

  • (a) Parametric Equations:$$x = -2 + 2t, \quad y = 4t, \quad z = 3 - 2t$$
  • (b) Symmetric Equations:$$\frac{x - (-2)}{2} = \frac{y - 0}{4} = \frac{z - 3}{-2} \implies \frac{x + 2}{2} = \frac{y}{4} = \frac{z - 3}{-2}$$Dividing denominators by $2$ gives the simplest integer direction form:$$\frac{x + 2}{1} = \frac{y}{2} = \frac{z - 3}{-1}$$
Ex 12 Easy Lines in SpaceParametric Equations
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 12: Parametric and Symmetric Equations Parallel to a Given Line

🎯 High — tests extracting direction vectors from symmetric lines and constructing a parallel line through a new point.
Problem Statement

Find sets of (a) parametric equations and (b) symmetric equations of the line that passes through the point $(-3, 5, 4)$ and is parallel to the line:

$$\frac{x - 1}{3} = \frac{y + 1}{-2} = z - 3$$

Write the direction numbers as integers.

Boxed Final Answer
  • (a) Parametric Equations:$$x = -3 + 3t, \quad y = 5 - 2t, \quad z = 4 + t$$
  • (b) Symmetric Equations:$$\frac{x + 3}{3} = \frac{y - 5}{-2} = z - 4$$

Step 1: Extract Direction Numbers of the Given Line

Rewrite the symmetric equations of the given line with an explicit denominator for $z$:

$$\frac{x - 1}{3} = \frac{y + 1}{-2} = \frac{z - 3}{1}$$

The denominators yield the direction numbers: $\mathbf{v} = \langle 3, -2, 1 \rangle$. Because the new line is parallel to the given line, it has the identical direction numbers $a = 3, \; b = -2, \; c = 1$.

Step 2: Construct Equations with Point $(-3, 5, 4)$

  • (a) Parametric Equations:$$x = -3 + 3t$$$$y = 5 - 2t$$$$z = 4 + t$$
  • (b) Symmetric Equations:$$\frac{x - (-3)}{3} = \frac{y - 5}{-2} = \frac{z - 4}{1} \implies \frac{x + 3}{3} = \frac{y - 5}{-2} = z - 4$$
Ex 14 Easy Lines in SpaceTwo-Point Form
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 14: Line Passing Through Two Given Points

🎯 High — finding line equations through two distinct 3D points is a core foundational exam question.
Problem Statement

Find sets of (a) parametric equations and (b) symmetric equations of the line that passes through the two points $(0, 4, 3)$ and $(-1, 2, 5)$. Write the direction numbers as integers.

Boxed Final Answer
  • (a) Parametric Equations:$$x = t, \quad y = 4 + 2t, \quad z = 3 - 2t \quad (\text{or } x = -t, \; y = 4 - 2t, \; z = 3 + 2t)$$
  • (b) Symmetric Equations:$$x = \frac{y - 4}{2} = \frac{z - 3}{-2} \quad \left(\text{or } \frac{x}{-1} = \frac{y - 4}{-2} = \frac{z - 3}{2}\right)$$

Step 1: Calculate the Direction Vector

Let $P_1 = (0, 4, 3)$ and $P_2 = (-1, 2, 5)$. The displacement vector connecting the two points is:

$$\vec{P_1 P_2} = \langle -1 - 0, \; 2 - 4, \; 5 - 3 \rangle = \langle -1, -2, 2 \rangle$$

We may choose $\mathbf{v} = -\vec{P_1 P_2} = \langle 1, 2, -2 \rangle$ (or directly $\langle -1, -2, 2 \rangle$) as integer direction numbers: $a = 1, \; b = 2, \; c = -2$.

Step 2: Formulate Equations with Base Point $(0, 4, 3)$

  • (a) Parametric Equations:$$x = 0 + 1t = t$$$$y = 4 + 2t$$$$z = 3 - 2t$$
  • (b) Symmetric Equations:$$\frac{x - 0}{1} = \frac{y - 4}{2} = \frac{z - 3}{-2} \implies x = \frac{y - 4}{2} = \frac{z - 3}{-2}$$
Ex 17 Medium Lines in SpaceParametric Equations
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 17: Line Parallel to Two Coordinate Planes

🎯 High — conceptual questions regarding parallelism to coordinate planes test spatial vector reasoning on KFUPM exams.
Problem Statement

Find a set of parametric equations of the line that passes through the point $(2, 3, 4)$ and is parallel to both the $xz$-plane and the $yz$-plane.

Boxed Final Answer
$$x = 2, \quad y = 3, \quad z = 4 + t$$

Step 1: Determine the Direction Vector Geometrically

Recall the normal vectors to the coordinate planes:

  • The $xz$-plane has equation $y = 0$, so its normal vector is $\mathbf{n}_1 = \mathbf{j} = \langle 0, 1, 0 \rangle$.
  • The $yz$-plane has equation $x = 0$, so its normal vector is $\mathbf{n}_2 = \mathbf{i} = \langle 1, 0, 0 \rangle$.

For a line to be parallel to both planes, its direction vector $\mathbf{v}$ must be perpendicular to both normals $\mathbf{n}_1$ and $\mathbf{n}_2$.

Thus, $\mathbf{v}$ is parallel to the cross product:

$$\mathbf{v} \parallel \mathbf{n}_1 \times \mathbf{n}_2 = \mathbf{j} \times \mathbf{i} = -\mathbf{k} = \langle 0, 0, -1 \rangle \parallel \langle 0, 0, 1 \rangle$$

Geometrically, any line parallel to both the $xz$-plane and the $yz$-plane is parallel to the line of intersection of those planes, which is the $z$-axis.

Thus, the direction numbers are $a = 0, \; b = 0, \; c = 1$.

Step 2: Write Parametric Equations

Using the point $(x_0, y_0, z_0) = (2, 3, 4)$ and direction vector $\mathbf{v} = \langle 0, 0, 1 \rangle$:

$$x = 2 + 0t = 2$$$$y = 3 + 0t = 3$$$$z = 4 + 1t = 4 + t$$
Ex 20 Easy Lines in SpacePlanes in Space
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 20: Line Perpendicular to a Given Plane

🎯 High — fundamental connection between normal vectors of planes and direction vectors of perpendicular lines.
Problem Statement

Find a set of parametric equations of the line that passes through the point $(-4, 5, 2)$ and is perpendicular to the plane given by $-x + 2y + z = 5$.

Boxed Final Answer
$$x = -4 - t, \quad y = 5 + 2t, \quad z = 2 + t$$

Step 1: Identify the Direction Vector

A line perpendicular to a plane has its direction vector $\mathbf{v}$ parallel to the normal vector $\mathbf{n}$ of that plane.

The linear equation of the plane is:

$$-x + 2y + z = 5$$

The coefficients of $x, y, z$ directly provide the plane's normal vector:

$$\mathbf{n} = \langle -1, 2, 1 \rangle$$

Therefore, we set the line's direction vector to $\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{n} = \langle -1, 2, 1 \rangle$, giving direction numbers $a = -1, \; b = 2, \; c = 1$.

Step 2: Construct the Parametric Equations

Using point $(x_0, y_0, z_0) = (-4, 5, 2)$:

$$x = -4 - t$$$$y = 5 + 2t$$$$z = 2 + t$$
Ex 23 Easy Lines in SpaceParametric Equations
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 23: Line Through Point Parallel to Another Line

🎯 High — common exam problem requiring extraction of direction vector from parametric equations.
Problem Statement

Find a set of parametric equations of the line that passes through the point $(2, 1, 2)$ and is parallel to the line $x = -t, \; y = 1 + t, \; z = -2 + t$.

Boxed Final Answer
$$x = 2 - t, \quad y = 1 + t, \quad z = 2 + t$$

Step 1: Extract the Direction Vector

The given line has parametric equations $x = -t, \; y = 1 + t, \; z = -2 + t$. The coefficients of $t$ give its direction vector:

$$\mathbf{v} = \langle -1, 1, 1 \rangle$$

Since the desired line is parallel to this line, it has the identical direction vector $\mathbf{v} = \langle -1, 1, 1 \rangle$.

Step 2: Construct the Parametric Equations

Using the given point $(x_0, y_0, z_0) = (2, 1, 2)$:

$$x = 2 - t$$$$y = 1 + t$$$$z = 2 + t$$
Ex 27 Easy Lines in SpaceSymmetric Equations
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 27: Identifying a Point and Direction Vector from Symmetric Equations

🎯 High — fundamental inspection skill required for solving line-plane intersection and distance problems on exams.
Problem Statement

Find the coordinates of a point $P$ on the line and a vector $\mathbf{v}$ parallel to the line given by:

$$\frac{x - 7}{4} = \frac{y + 6}{2} = z + 2$$
Boxed Final Answer

Point on line: $P(7, -6, -2)$ (other answers possible)

Parallel vector: $\mathbf{v} = \langle 4, 2, 1 \rangle$ (any nonzero scalar multiple)

Step 1: Write in Standard Symmetric Form

Express each term in standard form with an explicit denominator:

$$\frac{x - 7}{4} = \frac{y - (-6)}{2} = \frac{z - (-2)}{1}$$

The standard symmetric equation of a line is:

$$\frac{x - x_0}{a} = \frac{y - y_0}{b} = \frac{z - z_0}{c}$$

Step 2: Read Off Point and Direction Vector

  • Point $P$: Setting each fraction equal to $0$ yields $x = 7$, $y = -6$, and $z = -2$. Thus, $P(7, -6, -2)$ lies on the line.
  • Direction Vector $\mathbf{v}$: The denominators give direction numbers $a = 4, \; b = 2, \; c = 1$. Thus, $\mathbf{v} = \langle 4, 2, 1 \rangle$.
Ex 31 Medium Lines in SpaceParallel Lines
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 31: Determining Parallel vs. Identical Lines

🎯 High — distinguishing parallel non-coincident lines from identical lines is a frequent conceptual question on KFUPM exams.
Problem Statement

Determine whether the lines are parallel or identical:

$$L_1: \frac{x - 8}{4} = \frac{y + 5}{-2} = \frac{z + 9}{3}$$$$L_2: \frac{x + 4}{-8} = \frac{y - 1}{4} = \frac{z + 18}{-6}$$
Boxed Final Answer
The lines are identical (coincident).

Step 1: Check if the Lines are Parallel

Identify direction vectors from the denominators:

$$\mathbf{v}_1 = \langle 4, -2, 3 \rangle \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbf{v}_2 = \langle -8, 4, -6 \rangle$$

Check for proportionality:

$$\mathbf{v}_2 = -2\langle 4, -2, 3 \rangle = -2\mathbf{v}_1$$

Because the direction vectors are parallel scalar multiples, the lines $L_1$ and $L_2$ are parallel.

Step 2: Check for a Shared Common Point

Two parallel lines are identical if they share at least one point in common. From $L_1$, a known point is $P(8, -5, -9)$.

Test whether $P$ satisfies the symmetric equations of $L_2$:

$$\frac{8 + 4}{-8} = \frac{12}{-8} = -\frac{3}{2}$$$$\frac{-5 - 1}{4} = \frac{-6}{4} = -\frac{3}{2}$$$$\frac{-9 + 18}{-6} = \frac{9}{-6} = -\frac{3}{2}$$

All three ratios are equal to $-\frac{3}{2}$, proving that $P(8, -5, -9)$ lies on $L_2$.

Conclusion: Since $L_1$ and $L_2$ are parallel and share a common point, they are identical lines.

Ex 33 Medium Lines in SpaceLine Intersection
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 33: Intersection and Angle Between Two Lines

🎯 High — finding the intersection point of two lines and the acute angle between them is one of the highest-frequency KFUPM Exam 1 questions.
Problem Statement

Determine whether the lines intersect, and if so, find the point of intersection and the cosine of the angle $\theta$ (and angle in degrees) between the lines:

$$L_1: x = 4t + 2, \quad y = 3, \quad z = -t + 1$$$$L_2: x = 2s + 2, \quad y = 2s + 3, \quad z = s + 1$$
Boxed Final Answer

The lines intersect at the point $(2, 3, 1)$.

The angle between the lines is:

$$\cos\theta = \frac{7\sqrt{17}}{51} \approx 0.566 \implies \theta = \arccos\left(\frac{7\sqrt{17}}{51}\right) \approx 55.5^\circ \quad (\approx 0.969 \text{ rad})$$

Step 1: Test for Intersection

Set the coordinates of $L_1$ and $L_2$ equal to one another:

  1. $x$: $4t + 2 = 2s + 2 \implies 4t = 2s$
  2. $y$: $3 = 2s + 3 \implies 2s = 0 \implies s = 0$
  3. $z$: $-t + 1 = s + 1$

From equation (2), $s = 0$. Substituting $s = 0$ into equation (3):

$$-t + 1 = 1 \implies t = 0$$

Now check whether $(t, s) = (0, 0)$ satisfies equation (1):

$$4(0) + 2 = 2(0) + 2 \implies 2 = 2 \quad \checkmark$$

Since all three equations are satisfied simultaneously, the lines intersect.

Step 2: Find the Point of Intersection

Substitute $t = 0$ into $L_1$ (or $s = 0$ into $L_2$):

$$x = 4(0) + 2 = 2, \quad y = 3, \quad z = -(0) + 1 = 1$$

The point of intersection is $(2, 3, 1)$.

Step 3: Calculate the Angle Between the Lines

Extract direction vectors:

$$\mathbf{u} = \langle 4, 0, -1 \rangle \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbf{v} = \langle 2, 2, 1 \rangle$$

Compute dot product and magnitudes:

$$\mathbf{u} \cdot \mathbf{v} = (4)(2) + (0)(2) + (-1)(1) = 8 - 1 = 7$$$$\|\mathbf{u}\| = \sqrt{4^2 + 0^2 + (-1)^2} = \sqrt{17}$$$$\|\mathbf{v}\| = \sqrt{2^2 + 2^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{9} = 3$$

Using the cosine formula for lines:

$$\cos\theta = \frac{|\mathbf{u} \cdot \mathbf{v}|}{\|\mathbf{u}\| \|\mathbf{v}\|} = \frac{7}{3\sqrt{17}} = \frac{7\sqrt{17}}{51} \approx 0.566$$$$\theta = \arccos\left(\frac{7\sqrt{17}}{51}\right) \approx 55.5^\circ$$
Ex Project (a) Hard Skew LinesLines in Space
★ KFUPM Aligned

Section Project: Skew Lines — Non-Parallelism, Skewness & Distance Between Planes

🎯 High — proving lines are skew and finding the distance between skew lines via parallel planes is a classic multi-step KFUPM major exam question.
Problem Statement

Two lines in space are skew if they are neither parallel nor intersecting. Consider the following two lines in space:

$$L_1: x = 4 + 5t, \quad y = 5 + 5t, \quad z = 1 - 4t$$$$L_2: x = 4 + s, \quad y = -6 + 8s, \quad z = 7 - 3s$$
  1. Show that these lines are not parallel.
  2. Show that these lines do not intersect and therefore are skew lines.
  3. Show that the two lines lie in parallel planes.
  4. Find the distance between the parallel planes from part (iii). This is the distance between the original skew lines.
Boxed Final Answer
  • (i) $\mathbf{u}_1 = \langle 5, 5, -4 \rangle$ and $\mathbf{u}_2 = \langle 1, 8, -3 \rangle$ are not proportional scalar multiples, so the lines are not parallel.
  • (ii) Solving $x$- and $y$-equations yields $t = \frac{11}{35}, \; s = \frac{11}{7}$, but $z_1 = -\frac{9}{35} \neq \frac{80}{35} = z_2$. The system has no solution, so the lines do not intersect and are skew.
  • (iii) Both planes share the common normal vector $\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{u}_1 \times \mathbf{u}_2 = \langle 17, 11, 35 \rangle$. The planes are $17x + 11y + 35z = 158$ and $17x + 11y + 35z = 247$.
  • (iv) Distance between the parallel planes (and between the skew lines):$$D = \frac{89}{\sqrt{1635}} = \frac{89\sqrt{1635}}{1635} \approx 2.20$$

Part (i): Showing the lines are not parallel

Direction vectors of the lines:

$$\mathbf{u}_1 = \langle 5, 5, -4 \rangle \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbf{u}_2 = \langle 1, 8, -3 \rangle$$

If $\mathbf{u}_1 = c \mathbf{u}_2$, then $5 = c(1) \implies c = 5$. But for the $y$-components, $5 \neq 5(8) = 40$.

Because no single scalar $c$ relates the components, $\mathbf{u}_1$ and $\mathbf{u}_2$ are not parallel. Hence, $L_1$ and $L_2$ are not parallel.

Part (ii): Showing the lines do not intersect

Equate corresponding coordinate equations:

  1. $4 + 5t = 4 + s \implies s = 5t$
  2. $5 + 5t = -6 + 8s$
  3. $1 - 4t = 7 - 3s$

Substitute $s = 5t$ into equation (2):

$$5 + 5t = -6 + 40t \implies 35t = 11 \implies t = \frac{11}{35}$$$$s = 5\left(\frac{11}{35}\right) = \frac{11}{7} = \frac{55}{35}$$

Check the $z$-coordinate consistency:

$$z_1 = 1 - 4\left(\frac{11}{35}\right) = 1 - \frac{44}{35} = -\frac{9}{35}$$$$z_2 = 7 - 3\left(\frac{11}{7}\right) = 7 - \frac{33}{7} = \frac{16}{7} = \frac{80}{35}$$$$-\frac{9}{35} \neq \frac{80}{35}$$

Because the $z$-coordinates are inconsistent, there is no common point of intersection. Therefore, $L_1$ and $L_2$ are skew lines.

Part (iii): Showing the lines lie in parallel planes

Two parallel planes $\mathcal{P}_1$ and $\mathcal{P}_2$ containing $L_1$ and $L_2$ respectively must both have a normal vector $\mathbf{n}$ orthogonal to both direction vectors $\mathbf{u}_1$ and $\mathbf{u}_2$:

$$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{u}_1 \times \mathbf{u}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 5 & 5 & -4 \\ 1 & 8 & -3 \end{vmatrix} = \mathbf{i}(-15 + 32) - \mathbf{j}(-15 + 4) + \mathbf{k}(40 - 5) = 17\mathbf{i} + 11\mathbf{j} + 35\mathbf{k} = \langle 17, 11, 35 \rangle$$

Plane $\mathcal{P}_1$ contains $L_1$ and passes through point $P_1(4, 5, 1)$:

$$17(x - 4) + 11(y - 5) + 35(z - 1) = 0 \implies 17x + 11y + 35z = 158$$

Plane $\mathcal{P}_2$ contains $L_2$ and passes through point $P_2(4, -6, 7)$:

$$17(x - 4) + 11(y + 6) + 35(z - 7) = 0 \implies 17x + 11y + 35z = 247$$

Since both planes have the identical normal vector $\mathbf{n} = \langle 17, 11, 35 \rangle$ and different constant terms ($158 \neq 247$), they are distinct parallel planes.

Part (iv): Finding the distance between the parallel planes

Using the parallel plane distance formula with $d_1 = -158$ and $d_2 = -247$:

$$D = \frac{|247 - 158|}{\sqrt{17^2 + 11^2 + 35^2}} = \frac{89}{\sqrt{289 + 121 + 1225}} = \frac{89}{\sqrt{1635}} = \frac{89\sqrt{1635}}{1635} \approx 2.20$$
Ex Project (b) Medium Skew LinesLines in Space
★ KFUPM Aligned

Section Project: Skew Lines — Distance Between Lines (First Application)

🎯 High — computing the shortest distance between skew lines is a staple high-difficulty KFUPM Exam 1 topic.
Problem Statement

Use the procedure developed in part (a) to find the distance between the skew lines:

$$L_1: x = 2t, \quad y = 4t, \quad z = 6t$$$$L_2: x = 1 - s, \quad y = 4 + s, \quad z = -1 + s$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$D = \frac{20}{\sqrt{26}} = \frac{10\sqrt{26}}{13} \approx 3.92$$

Step 1: Identify Points and Direction Vectors

  • For $L_1$: Point $P_1(0, 0, 0)$ (at $t = 0$), direction vector $\mathbf{u}_1 = \langle 2, 4, 6 \rangle = 2\langle 1, 2, 3 \rangle$.
  • For $L_2$: Point $P_2(1, 4, -1)$ (at $s = 0$), direction vector $\mathbf{u}_2 = \langle -1, 1, 1 \rangle$.

Step 2: Compute Normal Vector to the Parallel Planes

Take the cross product of the direction vectors:

$$\mathbf{n} = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 1 & 2 & 3 \\ -1 & 1 & 1 \end{vmatrix} = \mathbf{i}(2 - 3) - \mathbf{j}(1 + 3) + \mathbf{k}(1 + 2) = -\mathbf{i} - 4\mathbf{j} + 3\mathbf{k} = \langle -1, -4, 3 \rangle$$

Multiplying by $-1$, we take $\mathbf{n} = \langle 1, 4, -3 \rangle$. Magnitude: $\|\mathbf{n}\| = \sqrt{1^2 + 4^2 + (-3)^2} = \sqrt{26}$.

Step 3: Construct the Equations of the Parallel Planes

  • Plane $\mathcal{P}_1$ containing $L_1$ through $P_1(0, 0, 0)$:$$1(x - 0) + 4(y - 0) - 3(z - 0) = 0 \implies x + 4y - 3z = 0$$
  • Plane $\mathcal{P}_2$ containing $L_2$ through $P_2(1, 4, -1)$:$$1(x - 1) + 4(y - 4) - 3(z + 1) = 0 \implies x + 4y - 3z - 20 = 0 \implies x + 4y - 3z = 20$$

Step 4: Compute the Distance Between the Planes

$$D = \frac{|20 - 0|}{\sqrt{26}} = \frac{20}{\sqrt{26}} = \frac{10\sqrt{26}}{13} \approx 3.92$$
Ex Project (c) Medium Skew LinesLines in Space
★ KFUPM Aligned

Section Project: Skew Lines — Distance Between Lines (Second Application)

🎯 High — standard examination problem testing cross product normal calculation and distance between planes containing skew lines.
Problem Statement

Use the procedure developed in part (a) to find the distance between the skew lines:

$$L_1: x = 3t, \quad y = 2 - t, \quad z = -1 + t$$$$L_2: x = 1 + 4s, \quad y = -2 + s, \quad z = -3 - 3s$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$D = \frac{64}{\sqrt{222}} = \frac{32\sqrt{222}}{111} \approx 4.30$$

Step 1: Identify Points and Direction Vectors

  • For $L_1$: Point $P_1(0, 2, -1)$ (at $t = 0$), direction vector $\mathbf{u}_1 = \langle 3, -1, 1 \rangle$.
  • For $L_2$: Point $P_2(1, -2, -3)$ (at $s = 0$), direction vector $\mathbf{u}_2 = \langle 4, 1, -3 \rangle$.

Step 2: Compute Normal Vector to the Parallel Planes

$$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{u}_1 \times \mathbf{u}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 3 & -1 & 1 \\ 4 & 1 & -3 \end{vmatrix} = \mathbf{i}(3 - 1) - \mathbf{j}(-9 - 4) + \mathbf{k}(3 + 4) = 2\mathbf{i} + 13\mathbf{j} + 7\mathbf{k} = \langle 2, 13, 7 \rangle$$

Magnitude: $\|\mathbf{n}\| = \sqrt{2^2 + 13^2 + 7^2} = \sqrt{4 + 169 + 49} = \sqrt{222}$.

Step 3: Construct the Equations of the Parallel Planes

  • Plane $\mathcal{P}_1$ containing $L_1$ through $P_1(0, 2, -1)$:$$2(x - 0) + 13(y - 2) + 7(z + 1) = 0 \implies 2x + 13y + 7z = 19$$
  • Plane $\mathcal{P}_2$ containing $L_2$ through $P_2(1, -2, -3)$:$$2(x - 1) + 13(y + 2) + 7(z + 3) = 0 \implies 2x + 13y + 7z = -45$$

Step 4: Compute the Distance

$$D = \frac{|19 - (-45)|}{\sqrt{222}} = \frac{64}{\sqrt{222}} = \frac{32\sqrt{222}}{111} \approx 4.30$$
Ex Project (d) Hard Skew LinesScalar Triple Product
★ KFUPM Aligned

Section Project: Skew Lines — Derivation of General Distance Formula

🎯 High — the scalar triple product distance formula for skew lines allows rapid solution of KFUPM multiple choice exam items without constructing full plane equations.
Problem Statement

Develop a general formula for finding the distance between the skew lines:

$$L_1: x = x_1 + a_1 t, \quad y = y_1 + b_1 t, \quad z = z_1 + c_1 t$$$$L_2: x = x_2 + a_2 s, \quad y = y_2 + b_2 s, \quad z = z_2 + c_2 s$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$D = \frac{|\vec{P_1 P_2} \cdot (\mathbf{u}_1 \times \mathbf{u}_2)|}{\|\mathbf{u}_1 \times \mathbf{u}_2\|} = \frac{\left|\begin{vmatrix} x_2 - x_1 & y_2 - y_1 & z_2 - z_1 \\ a_1 & b_1 & c_1 \\ a_2 & b_2 & c_2 \end{vmatrix}\right|}{\sqrt{(b_1 c_2 - c_1 b_2)^2 + (c_1 a_2 - a_1 c_2)^2 + (a_1 b_2 - b_1 a_2)^2}}$$ where $P_1 = (x_1, y_1, z_1)$ and $P_2 = (x_2, y_2, z_2)$.

Step 1: Define Points and Direction Vectors

  • Line $L_1$ passes through point $P_1(x_1, y_1, z_1)$ with direction vector $\mathbf{u}_1 = \langle a_1, b_1, c_1 \rangle$.
  • Line $L_2$ passes through point $P_2(x_2, y_2, z_2)$ with direction vector $\mathbf{u}_2 = \langle a_2, b_2, c_2 \rangle$.
  • The connecting vector from $P_1$ to $P_2$ is $\vec{P_1 P_2} = \langle x_2 - x_1, y_2 - y_1, z_2 - z_1 \rangle$.

Step 2: Determine the Common Normal Direction

Because the lines are skew, they lie in two parallel planes $\mathcal{P}_1$ and $\mathcal{P}_2$.

Any normal vector $\mathbf{n}$ perpendicular to both planes must be perpendicular to both $\mathbf{u}_1$ and $\mathbf{u}_2$. Thus, we evaluate their cross product:

$$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{u}_1 \times \mathbf{u}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ a_1 & b_1 & c_1 \\ a_2 & b_2 & c_2 \end{vmatrix} = (b_1 c_2 - c_1 b_2)\mathbf{i} - (a_1 c_2 - c_1 a_2)\mathbf{j} + (a_1 b_2 - b_1 a_2)\mathbf{k}$$

Step 3: Project the Displacement Vector onto the Normal Vector

The shortest distance $D$ between the two lines is the perpendicular distance between the parallel planes containing them.

This equals the length of the vector projection (or absolute scalar projection) of $\vec{P_1 P_2}$ onto the normal vector $\mathbf{n}$:

$$D = \|\operatorname{proj}_{\mathbf{n}} \vec{P_1 P_2}\| = \frac{|\vec{P_1 P_2} \cdot \mathbf{n}|}{\|\mathbf{n}\|} = \frac{|\vec{P_1 P_2} \cdot (\mathbf{u}_1 \times \mathbf{u}_2)|}{\|\mathbf{u}_1 \times \mathbf{u}_2\|}$$

Step 4: Determinant Form (Scalar Triple Product)

Using the definition of the scalar triple product, the numerator is the absolute value of the $3 \times 3$ determinant:

$$D = \frac{\left|\begin{vmatrix} x_2 - x_1 & y_2 - y_1 & z_2 - z_1 \\ a_1 & b_1 & c_1 \\ a_2 & b_2 & c_2 \end{vmatrix}\right|}{\sqrt{(b_1 c_2 - c_1 b_2)^2 + (c_1 a_2 - a_1 c_2)^2 + (a_1 b_2 - b_1 a_2)^2}}$$This formula computes the exact shortest distance between any pair of skew lines in $\mathbb{R}^3$.
