11.5 Planes in Space & Intersections

Suggested Textbook Practice Problems (KFUPM Exam Aligned) • 13 Curated Exercises

Jump to problem:
Ex 38 Easy Planes in SpacePoint in Plane
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 38: Checking Points in a Plane

🎯 Medium — foundational test of whether coordinates satisfy a 3D linear plane equation.
Problem Statement
Determine whether each point lies in the plane $2x + y + 3z - 6 = 0$: **(a)** $(3, 6, -2)$ **(b)** $(-1, 5, -1)$ **(c)** $(2, 1, 0)$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{(a) Lies in the plane, (b) Does not lie in the plane, (c) Does not lie in the plane}}$$
Substitute each given point $(x, y, z)$ into the linear plane equation $2x + y + 3z - 6 = 0$: **(a)** For the point $(3, 6, -2)$: $$2(3) + (6) + 3(-2) - 6 = 6 + 6 - 6 - 6 = 0$$ Since the equation is satisfied ($0 = 0$), the point $(3, 6, -2)$ **lies in the plane**. **(b)** For the point $(-1, 5, -1)$: $$2(-1) + (5) + 3(-1) - 6 = -2 + 5 - 3 - 6 = -6 \neq 0$$ Since the result is non-zero, the point $(-1, 5, -1)$ **does not lie in the plane**. **(c)** For the point $(2, 1, 0)$: $$2(2) + (1) + 3(0) - 6 = 4 + 1 + 0 - 6 = -1 \neq 0$$ Since the result is non-zero, the point $(2, 1, 0)$ **does not lie in the plane**.
Ex 43 Easy Planes in SpaceNormal Vector
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 43: Equation of a Plane Perpendicular to a Given Line

🎯 High — recurring KFUPM Exam 1 question testing that a line's direction vector serves as the plane's normal vector.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the plane that passes through the point $(-1, 4, 0)$ and is perpendicular to the line $$x = -1 + 2t, \quad y = 5 - t, \quad z = 3 - 2t$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{2x - y - 2z + 6 = 0 \quad \text{or} \quad 2x - y - 2z = -6}$$
A plane perpendicular to a line has a normal vector $\mathbf{n}$ that is parallel to the direction vector $\mathbf{u}$ of the line. 1. **Determine the direction vector of the line:** From the parametric equations: $$x = -1 + 2t, \quad y = 5 - 1t, \quad z = 3 - 2t$$ the direction numbers are the coefficients of $t$: $$\mathbf{u} = \langle 2, -1, -2 \rangle$$ 2. **Set the normal vector of the plane:** Since the plane is perpendicular to the line, we choose: $$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{u} = \langle 2, -1, -2 \rangle$$ 3. **Apply the point-normal equation of the plane:** The standard equation of a plane through $P_0(x_0, y_0, z_0) = (-1, 4, 0)$ with normal $\mathbf{n} = \langle a, b, c \rangle = \langle 2, -1, -2 \rangle$ is: $$a(x - x_0) + b(y - y_0) + c(z - z_0) = 0$$ Substituting the coordinates: $$2(x - (-1)) - 1(y - 4) - 2(z - 0) = 0$$ $$2(x + 1) - (y - 4) - 2z = 0$$ $$2x + 2 - y + 4 - 2z = 0$$ $$2x - y - 2z + 6 = 0 \implies 2x - y - 2z = -6$$
Ex 45 Medium Planes in SpaceThree Points
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 45: Equation of a Plane Passing Through Three Points

🎯 Very High — quintessential KFUPM Exam 1 problem requiring cross product of two vectors lying in the plane.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the plane that passes through the three points $(0, 0, 0)$, $(2, 0, 3)$, and $(-3, -1, 5)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{3x - 19y - 2z = 0}$$
Let the three points be $P(0, 0, 0)$, $Q(2, 0, 3)$, and $R(-3, -1, 5)$. 1. **Determine two displacement vectors in the plane:** Using the origin $P(0, 0, 0)$ as the common base point: $$\mathbf{u} = \vec{PQ} = \langle 2 - 0, 0 - 0, 3 - 0 \rangle = \langle 2, 0, 3 \rangle$$ $$\mathbf{v} = \vec{PR} = \langle -3 - 0, -1 - 0, 5 - 0 \rangle = \langle -3, -1, 5 \rangle$$ 2. **Compute the normal vector via the cross product:** A normal vector $\mathbf{n}$ perpendicular to both $\mathbf{u}$ and $\mathbf{v}$ is: $$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{u} \times \mathbf{v} = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 2 & 0 & 3 \\ -3 & -1 & 5 \end{vmatrix}$$ Expanding across the first row: $$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{i}\begin{vmatrix} 0 & 3 \\ -1 & 5 \end{vmatrix} - \mathbf{j}\begin{vmatrix} 2 & 3 \\ -3 & 5 \end{vmatrix} + \mathbf{k}\begin{vmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ -3 & -1 \end{vmatrix}$$ $$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{i}(0 - (-3)) - \mathbf{j}(10 - (-9)) + \mathbf{k}(-2 - 0)$$ $$\mathbf{n} = 3\mathbf{i} - 19\mathbf{j} - 2\mathbf{k} = \langle 3, -19, -2 \rangle$$ 3. **Write the equation of the plane:** Using the point-normal equation with base point $P(0, 0, 0)$: $$a(x - x_0) + b(y - y_0) + c(z - z_0) = 0$$ $$3(x - 0) - 19(y - 0) - 2(z - 0) = 0$$ $$3x - 19y - 2z = 0$$
Ex 48 Easy Planes in SpaceCoordinate Planes
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 48: Equation of a Plane Parallel to a Coordinate Plane

🎯 Medium — standard conceptual question on coordinate planes ($x = c$, $y = c$, $z = c$) and normal vectors.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the plane that passes through the point $(1, 2, 3)$ and is parallel to the $yz$-plane.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{x = 1 \quad \text{or} \quad x - 1 = 0}$$
1. **Identify the normal vector of the $yz$-plane:** The coordinate $yz$-plane has equation $x = 0$, and its normal vector is directed along the $x$-axis: $$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{i} = \langle 1, 0, 0 \rangle$$ 2. **Formulate the equation of the parallel plane:** Since the desired plane is parallel to the $yz$-plane, it shares the normal direction $\mathbf{n} = \langle 1, 0, 0 \rangle$. Using the point-normal equation with the given point $P_0(1, 2, 3)$: $$a(x - x_0) + b(y - y_0) + c(z - z_0) = 0$$ $$1(x - 1) + 0(y - 2) + 0(z - 3) = 0$$ $$x - 1 = 0 \implies x = 1$$
Ex 51 Medium Planes in SpaceIntersecting Lines
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 51: Equation of a Plane Containing Two Intersecting Lines

🎯 Very High — common KFUPM Exam 1 problem requiring cross product of direction vectors of coplanar lines.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the plane that contains the lines given by $$\frac{x - 1}{-2} = y - 4 = z \quad \text{and} \quad \frac{x - 2}{-3} = \frac{y - 1}{4} = \frac{z - 2}{-1}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{x + y + z - 5 = 0 \quad \text{or} \quad x + y + z = 5}$$
1. **Extract direction vectors of both lines:** From the symmetric equations of the first line $L_1$: $$\frac{x - 1}{-2} = \frac{y - 4}{1} = \frac{z - 0}{1}$$ The direction vector is $\mathbf{u} = \langle -2, 1, 1 \rangle$. From the symmetric equations of the second line $L_2$: $$\frac{x - 2}{-3} = \frac{y - 1}{4} = \frac{z - 2}{-1}$$ The direction vector is $\mathbf{v} = \langle -3, 4, -1 \rangle$. 2. **Compute the normal vector of the plane:** Since the plane contains both lines, its normal vector $\mathbf{n}$ must be perpendicular to both $\mathbf{u}$ and $\mathbf{v}$: $$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{u} \times \mathbf{v} = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ -2 & 1 & 1 \\ -3 & 4 & -1 \end{vmatrix}$$ $$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{i}(-1 - 4) - \mathbf{j}(2 - (-3)) + \mathbf{k}(-8 - (-3))$$ $$\mathbf{n} = -5\mathbf{i} - 5\mathbf{j} - 5\mathbf{k} = \langle -5, -5, -5 \rangle$$ Dividing by $-5$ gives the simplified normal vector: $$\mathbf{n} = \langle 1, 1, 1 \rangle$$ 3. **Select a point on the plane:** From $L_1$, setting each symmetric fraction to $0$ gives the point $P_0(1, 4, 0)$, which lies on $L_1$ and therefore on the plane. 4. **Write the equation of the plane:** Using $\mathbf{n} = \langle 1, 1, 1 \rangle$ and $P_0(1, 4, 0)$: $$1(x - 1) + 1(y - 4) + 1(z - 0) = 0$$ $$x - 1 + y - 4 + z = 0$$ $$x + y + z - 5 = 0 \implies x + y + z = 5$$
Ex 53 Medium Planes in SpacePerpendicular Planes
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 53: Equation of a Plane Through Two Points Perpendicular to Another Plane

🎯 High — tests geometric synthesis in KFUPM Exam 1: the normal vector of an orthogonal plane lies parallel to the target plane.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the plane that passes through the points $(2, 2, 1)$ and $(-1, 1, -1)$ and is perpendicular to the plane $$2x - 3y + z = 3$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{7x + y - 11z - 5 = 0 \quad \text{or} \quad 7x + y - 11z = 5}$$
Let $P = (2, 2, 1)$, $Q = (-1, 1, -1)$, and let the given plane be $\mathcal{P}_1: 2x - 3y + z = 3$. 1. **Find a vector lying in the target plane:** The displacement vector between the two given points lies entirely in the desired plane $\mathcal{P}$: $$\mathbf{v} = \vec{QP} = \langle 2 - (-1), 2 - 1, 1 - (-1) \rangle = \langle 3, 1, 2 \rangle$$ 2. **Identify the normal vector of the perpendicular plane:** The normal vector to $\mathcal{P}_1$ is: $$\mathbf{n}_1 = \langle 2, -3, 1 \rangle$$ Since the desired plane $\mathcal{P}$ is perpendicular to $\mathcal{P}_1$, the vector $\mathbf{n}_1$ is parallel to $\mathcal{P}$. 3. **Compute the normal vector of the target plane:** The normal vector $\mathbf{n}$ must be perpendicular to both $\mathbf{v}$ and $\mathbf{n}_1$: $$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{v} \times \mathbf{n}_1 = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 3 & 1 & 2 \\ 2 & -3 & 1 \end{vmatrix}$$ $$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{i}(1 - (-6)) - \mathbf{j}(3 - 4) + \mathbf{k}(-9 - 2)$$ $$\mathbf{n} = 7\mathbf{i} + \mathbf{j} - 11\mathbf{k} = \langle 7, 1, -11 \rangle$$ 4. **Write the equation of the plane:** Using base point $P(2, 2, 1)$ and normal vector $\mathbf{n} = \langle 7, 1, -11 \rangle$: $$7(x - 2) + 1(y - 2) - 11(z - 1) = 0$$ $$7x - 14 + y - 2 - 11z + 11 = 0$$ $$7x + y - 11z - 5 = 0 \implies 7x + y - 11z = 5$$ *Verification with point $Q(-1, 1, -1)$:* $7(-1) + (1) - 11(-1) = -7 + 1 + 11 = 5$ (satisfied).
Ex 57 Medium Planes in SpaceEquidistant Points
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 57: Equation of the Equidistant Plane Between Two Points

🎯 Medium — tests understanding of geometric loci and perpendicular bisector planes in $\mathbb{R}^3$.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the plane that contains all the points that are equidistant from the points $(2, 2, 0)$ and $(0, 2, 2)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{x - z = 0}$$
We present two rigorous approaches: **Method 1: Geometric Derivation (Perpendicular Bisector Plane)** 1. The set of all points equidistant from two distinct points $A(2, 2, 0)$ and $B(0, 2, 2)$ forms the perpendicular bisector plane of the line segment $AB$. 2. The midpoint $M$ of $AB$ lies on the plane: $$M = \left( \frac{2 + 0}{2}, \frac{2 + 2}{2}, \frac{0 + 2}{2} \right) = (1, 2, 1)$$ 3. The normal vector $\mathbf{n}$ is parallel to the segment vector $\vec{BA}$: $$\mathbf{n} = \vec{BA} = \langle 2 - 0, 2 - 2, 0 - 2 \rangle = \langle 2, 0, -2 \rangle$$ Dividing by $2$ gives the simplified normal vector $\mathbf{n} = \langle 1, 0, -1 \rangle$. 4. Writing the point-normal equation through $M(1, 2, 1)$: $$1(x - 1) + 0(y - 2) - 1(z - 1) = 0$$ $$x - 1 - z + 1 = 0 \implies x - z = 0$$ **Method 2: Algebraic Derivation (Distance Formula)** Let $(x, y, z)$ be any point equidistant from $(2, 2, 0)$ and $(0, 2, 2)$: $$\sqrt{(x - 2)^2 + (y - 2)^2 + (z - 0)^2} = \sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 2)^2 + (z - 2)^2}$$ Squaring both sides: $$(x - 2)^2 + (y - 2)^2 + z^2 = x^2 + (y - 2)^2 + (z - 2)^2$$ Subtract $(y - 2)^2$ from both sides: $$(x - 2)^2 + z^2 = x^2 + (z - 2)^2$$ $$x^2 - 4x + 4 + z^2 = x^2 + z^2 - 4z + 4$$ Subtract $x^2 + z^2 + 4$: $$-4x = -4z \implies x - z = 0$$
Ex 62 Easy Planes in SpaceParallel Planes
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 62: Determining Whether Two Planes Are Parallel or Identical

🎯 Medium — standard KFUPM Exam 1 conceptual question distinguishing parallel distinct planes from coincident planes.
Problem Statement
Determine whether the planes are parallel or identical: $$2x - y + 3z = 8$$ $$8x - 4y + 12z = 5$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{Parallel (not identical)}}$$
1. **Determine the normal vectors:** For the first plane $\mathcal{P}_1: 2x - y + 3z = 8$: $$\mathbf{n}_1 = \langle 2, -1, 3 \rangle$$ For the second plane $\mathcal{P}_2: 8x - 4y + 12z = 5$: $$\mathbf{n}_2 = \langle 8, -4, 12 \rangle$$ 2. **Check for parallelism:** Observe that: $$\mathbf{n}_2 = \langle 8, -4, 12 \rangle = 4 \langle 2, -1, 3 \rangle = 4\mathbf{n}_1$$ Because their normal vectors are non-zero scalar multiples of each other, the two planes are **parallel**. 3. **Check whether the planes are identical:** Multiply the entire equation of $\mathcal{P}_1$ by $4$: $$4(2x - y + 3z) = 4(8) \implies 8x - 4y + 12z = 32$$ Comparing this with $\mathcal{P}_2: 8x - 4y + 12z = 5$, the linear terms match but the constant terms differ ($32 \neq 5$). Alternatively, select a point on $\mathcal{P}_1$, such as $P(4, 0, 0)$. Testing $P(4, 0, 0)$ in $\mathcal{P}_2$: $$8(4) - 4(0) + 12(0) = 32 \neq 5$$ Because $P$ does not lie on $\mathcal{P}_2$, the two planes share no points in common. Therefore, the planes are **parallel but not identical**.
Ex 67 Hard Planes in SpaceLine of Intersection
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 67: Angle Between Two Planes and Parametric Equations of Line of Intersection

🎯 Highest — one of the most prominent multi-step problems in KFUPM Exam 1 testing cross product and intersection line derivation.
Problem Statement
For the two planes $$3x - y + z = 7 \quad \text{and} \quad 4x + 6y + 3z = 2$$ **(a)** Find the angle between the two planes. **(b)** Find a set of parametric equations for the line of intersection of the planes.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\begin{aligned} \text{(a) } & \theta = \arccos\left(\frac{9\sqrt{671}}{671}\right) \approx 69.67^\circ \\ \text{(b) } & x = 2 - 9t, \; y = -1 - 5t, \; z = 22t \end{aligned}}$$
**(a) Find the angle between the two planes:** 1. The normal vectors are: $$\mathbf{n}_1 = \langle 3, -1, 1 \rangle \quad \text{and} \quad \mathbf{n}_2 = \langle 4, 6, 3 \rangle$$ 2. Compute their magnitudes: $$\|\mathbf{n}_1\| = \sqrt{3^2 + (-1)^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{9 + 1 + 1} = \sqrt{11}$$ $$\|\mathbf{n}_2\| = \sqrt{4^2 + 6^2 + 3^2} = \sqrt{16 + 36 + 9} = \sqrt{61}$$ 3. Compute the dot product: $$\mathbf{n}_1 \cdot \mathbf{n}_2 = (3)(4) + (-1)(6) + (1)(3) = 12 - 6 + 3 = 9$$ 4. The acute angle $\theta$ between the planes satisfies: $$\cos\theta = \frac{|\mathbf{n}_1 \cdot \mathbf{n}_2|}{\|\mathbf{n}_1\| \|\mathbf{n}_2\|} = \frac{9}{\sqrt{11}\sqrt{61}} = \frac{9}{\sqrt{671}} = \frac{9\sqrt{671}}{671}$$ $$\theta = \arccos\left(\frac{9\sqrt{671}}{671}\right) \approx 69.67^\circ \approx 1.216 \text{ rad}$$ **(b) Find a set of parametric equations for the line of intersection:** 1. **Direction vector of the line of intersection:** The direction vector $\mathbf{u}$ is perpendicular to both normal vectors: $$\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{n}_1 \times \mathbf{n}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 3 & -1 & 1 \\ 4 & 6 & 3 \end{vmatrix}$$ $$\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{i}((-1)(3) - (1)(6)) - \mathbf{j}((3)(3) - (1)(4)) + \mathbf{k}((3)(6) - (-1)(4))$$ $$\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{i}(-3 - 6) - \mathbf{j}(9 - 4) + \mathbf{k}(18 + 4) = -9\mathbf{i} - 5\mathbf{j} + 22\mathbf{k} = \langle -9, -5, 22 \rangle$$ 2. **Find a specific point on the intersection line:** Set $z = 0$ in the equations of both planes: $$\begin{cases} 3x - y = 7 & (1) \\ 4x + 6y = 2 & (2) \end{cases}$$ Multiply equation (1) by $6$: $$18x - 6y = 42$$ Add this result to equation (2): $$22x = 44 \implies x = 2$$ Substitute $x = 2$ back into (1): $$3(2) - y = 7 \implies 6 - y = 7 \implies y = -1$$ Thus, $P_0(2, -1, 0)$ lies on both planes. 3. **Parametric equations:** Using point $P_0(2, -1, 0)$ and direction vector $\mathbf{u} = \langle -9, -5, 22 \rangle$: $$x = 2 - 9t, \quad y = -1 - 5t, \quad z = 22t$$ *(Note: Using the opposite direction vector $\langle 9, 5, -22 \rangle$ yields the equally valid parametric equations $x = 2 + 9t, y = -1 + 5t, z = -22t$).*
Ex 71 Medium Planes in SpaceAngle Between Planes
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 71: Comparing Planes: Parallelism, Orthogonality, and Angle

🎯 High — standard KFUPM Exam 1 multiple choice question classifying pairs of planes and computing the acute angle.
Problem Statement
Determine whether the planes are parallel, orthogonal, or neither. If they are neither parallel nor orthogonal, find the angle between the planes: $$x - 3y + 6z = 4 \quad \text{and} \quad 5x + y - z = 4$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{Neither; } \theta = \arccos\left(\frac{2\sqrt{138}}{207}\right) \approx 83.5^\circ}$$
1. **Identify the normal vectors:** For $\mathcal{P}_1: x - 3y + 6z = 4$, the normal is $\mathbf{n}_1 = \langle 1, -3, 6 \rangle$. For $\mathcal{P}_2: 5x + y - z = 4$, the normal is $\mathbf{n}_2 = \langle 5, 1, -1 \rangle$. 2. **Check for parallelism:** Comparing component ratios: $\frac{1}{5} \neq \frac{-3}{1}$. Since $\mathbf{n}_1$ is not a scalar multiple of $\mathbf{n}_2$, the planes are **not parallel**. 3. **Check for orthogonality:** Compute the dot product: $$\mathbf{n}_1 \cdot \mathbf{n}_2 = (1)(5) + (-3)(1) + (6)(-1) = 5 - 3 - 6 = -4$$ Since $\mathbf{n}_1 \cdot \mathbf{n}_2 \neq 0$, the planes are **not orthogonal**. Thus, the planes are **neither parallel nor orthogonal**. 4. **Calculate the angle between the planes:** Compute the lengths of the normal vectors: $$\|\mathbf{n}_1\| = \sqrt{1^2 + (-3)^2 + 6^2} = \sqrt{1 + 9 + 36} = \sqrt{46}$$ $$\|\mathbf{n}_2\| = \sqrt{5^2 + 1^2 + (-1)^2} = \sqrt{25 + 1 + 1} = \sqrt{27} = 3\sqrt{3}$$ The cosine of the acute angle $\theta$ between the planes is: $$\cos\theta = \frac{|\mathbf{n}_1 \cdot \mathbf{n}_2|}{\|\mathbf{n}_1\| \|\mathbf{n}_2\|} = \frac{|-4|}{\sqrt{46} \cdot 3\sqrt{3}} = \frac{4}{3\sqrt{138}} = \frac{4\sqrt{138}}{3 \times 138} = \frac{4\sqrt{138}}{414} = \frac{2\sqrt{138}}{207}$$ $$\theta = \arccos\left(\frac{2\sqrt{138}}{207}\right) \approx 83.50^\circ \approx 1.457 \text{ rad}$$
Ex 72 Medium Planes in SpaceAngle Between Planes
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 72: Comparing Planes: Parallelism, Orthogonality, and Angle

🎯 High — recurring KFUPM Exam 1 style where the radical term in the cosine angle expression simplifies cleanly.
Problem Statement
Determine whether the planes are parallel, orthogonal, or neither. If they are neither parallel nor orthogonal, find the angle between the planes: $$3x + 2y - z = 7 \quad \text{and} \quad x - 4y + 2z = 0$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{Neither; } \theta = \arccos\left(\frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}\right) \approx 65.9^\circ}$$
1. **Identify the normal vectors:** For $\mathcal{P}_1: 3x + 2y - z = 7$, the normal is $\mathbf{n}_1 = \langle 3, 2, -1 \rangle$. For $\mathcal{P}_2: x - 4y + 2z = 0$, the normal is $\mathbf{n}_2 = \langle 1, -4, 2 \rangle$. 2. **Check for parallelism:** Comparing component ratios: $\frac{3}{1} \neq \frac{2}{-4} = -\frac{1}{2}$. The normal vectors are not proportional, so the planes are **not parallel**. 3. **Check for orthogonality:** Compute the dot product: $$\mathbf{n}_1 \cdot \mathbf{n}_2 = (3)(1) + (2)(-4) + (-1)(2) = 3 - 8 - 2 = -7$$ Since $\mathbf{n}_1 \cdot \mathbf{n}_2 \neq 0$, the planes are **not orthogonal**. Thus, the planes are **neither parallel nor orthogonal**. 4. **Calculate the angle between the planes:** Compute the magnitudes of the normal vectors: $$\|\mathbf{n}_1\| = \sqrt{3^2 + 2^2 + (-1)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 4 + 1} = \sqrt{14}$$ $$\|\mathbf{n}_2\| = \sqrt{1^2 + (-4)^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{1 + 16 + 4} = \sqrt{21}$$ The product of the norms is: $$\|\mathbf{n}_1\| \|\mathbf{n}_2\| = \sqrt{14}\sqrt{21} = \sqrt{2 \times 7 \times 3 \times 7} = 7\sqrt{6}$$ The cosine of the acute angle $\theta$ is: $$\cos\theta = \frac{|\mathbf{n}_1 \cdot \mathbf{n}_2|}{\|\mathbf{n}_1\| \|\mathbf{n}_2\|} = \frac{|-7|}{7\sqrt{6}} = \frac{7}{7\sqrt{6}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}$$ $$\theta = \arccos\left(\frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}\right) \approx 65.91^\circ \approx 1.150 \text{ rad}$$
Ex 80 Easy Planes in SpaceIntercepts
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 80: Analytical Intercepts and Traces of a Plane

🎯 High — essential foundation for solid sketching and triple integral limits in Math 201 Exam 2 / Final.
Problem Statement
Determine the intercepts and traces of the plane given by $$3x + 6y + 2z = 6$$ and describe its geometry in the first octant.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\begin{aligned} \text{Intercepts: } & (2, 0, 0), \; (0, 1, 0), \; (0, 0, 3) \\ \text{Traces: } & xy\text{-trace: } x + 2y = 2 \\ & xz\text{-trace: } 3x + 2z = 6 \\ & yz\text{-trace: } 3y + z = 3 \end{aligned}}$$
To analytically classify and determine the characteristics of the plane $3x + 6y + 2z = 6$: 1. **Find the axis intercepts:** - **$x$-intercept:** Set $y = 0$ and $z = 0$: $$3x = 6 \implies x = 2 \implies (2, 0, 0)$$ - **$y$-intercept:** Set $x = 0$ and $z = 0$: $$6y = 6 \implies y = 1 \implies (0, 1, 0)$$ - **$z$-intercept:** Set $x = 0$ and $y = 0$: $$2z = 6 \implies z = 3 \implies (0, 0, 3)$$ 2. **Express in intercept form:** Dividing the equation $3x + 6y + 2z = 6$ by $6$ yields: $$\frac{x}{2} + \frac{y}{1} + \frac{z}{3} = 1$$ which directly verifies that the intercepts on the $x$-, $y$-, and $z$-axes are $2$, $1$, and $3$ respectively. 3. **Determine the traces in the coordinate planes:** - **$xy$-trace (set $z = 0$):** $$3x + 6y = 6 \implies x + 2y = 2$$ This is a straight line in the $xy$-plane connecting $(2, 0, 0)$ and $(0, 1, 0)$. - **$xz$-trace (set $y = 0$):** $$3x + 2z = 6$$ This is a straight line in the $xz$-plane connecting $(2, 0, 0)$ and $(0, 0, 3)$. - **$yz$-trace (set $x = 0$):** $$6y + 2z = 6 \implies 3y + z = 3$$ This is a straight line in the $yz$-plane connecting $(0, 1, 0)$ and $(0, 0, 3)$. 4. **Geometric description:** The normal vector to the plane is $\mathbf{n} = \langle 3, 6, 2 \rangle$. In the first octant ($x \ge 0, y \ge 0, z \ge 0$), the plane forms a triangular face bounded by the three traces, enclosing a tetrahedron with the coordinate planes having vertices at the origin and the three intercepts.
Ex 83 Medium Planes in SpaceLine-Plane Intersection
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 83: Determining the Intersection of a Line and a Plane

🎯 Very High — core KFUPM Exam 1 topic testing whether a line intersects a plane in one point, no points, or infinitely many points.
Problem Statement
Find the point(s) of intersection (if any) of the plane and the line. Also, determine whether the line lies in the plane: $$\text{Plane: } x + 3y - z = 6$$ $$\text{Line: } \frac{x + 7}{2} = y - 4 = \frac{z + 1}{5}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{The line lies entirely in the plane (infinitely many points of intersection)}}$$
1. **Parametrize the line:** Set the symmetric equations equal to parameter $t$: $$\frac{x + 7}{2} = t \implies x = -7 + 2t$$ $$y - 4 = t \implies y = 4 + t$$ $$\frac{z + 1}{5} = t \implies z = -1 + 5t$$ 2. **Substitute into the plane equation:** Substitute the parametric expressions for $x, y, z$ into the plane equation $x + 3y - z = 6$: $$(-7 + 2t) + 3(4 + t) - (-1 + 5t) = 6$$ 3. **Solve for $t$:** $$-7 + 2t + 12 + 3t + 1 - 5t = 6$$ $$(-7 + 12 + 1) + (2 + 3 - 5)t = 6$$ $$6 + 0t = 6 \implies 6 = 6$$ 4. **Interpretation:** The equality $6 = 6$ is an identity that holds true for every real value of $t \in \mathbb{R}$. Consequently, every point on the line satisfies the equation of the plane. *Geometric verification:* - The direction vector of the line is $\mathbf{u} = \langle 2, 1, 5 \rangle$ and the normal vector of the plane is $\mathbf{n} = \langle 1, 3, -1 \rangle$. - Their dot product is $\mathbf{u} \cdot \mathbf{n} = (2)(1) + (1)(3) + (5)(-1) = 2 + 3 - 5 = 0$, confirming that the line is parallel to the plane. - Testing the line base point $(-7, 4, -1)$ in the plane gives $(-7) + 3(4) - (-1) = 6 = 6$, confirming that the point lies on the plane. Therefore, the line **lies entirely in the plane** (having infinitely many points of intersection).

11.5 Distances in Space

Suggested Textbook Practice Problems (KFUPM Exam Aligned) • 7 Curated Exercises

Ex 89 Medium Distances in SpacePoint-to-Plane Distance
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 89: Distance Between a Point and a Plane

🎯 High — directly tested on KFUPM Major Exam I & II (e.g. Term 221 Exam 1 Q14, Term 252 Exam II Q12), evaluating the perpendicular distance from a given point to a plane.
Problem Statement

Find the distance between the point $(2, 8, 4)$ and the plane

$$2x + y + z = 5$$
Boxed Final Answer

$$\boxed{D = \frac{11\sqrt{6}}{6} \approx 4.49}$$

We are given the point $Q(2, 8, 4)$ and the plane $2x + y + z = 5$. We present two standard methods to calculate the perpendicular distance $D$.

Method 1: Vector Projection (Theorem 11.13)

Let $P$ be any convenient point on the plane. Setting $x = 0$ and $y = 0$, we find:

$$2(0) + (0) + z = 5 \implies z = 5 \implies P(0, 0, 5)$$

The displacement vector from $P$ to the given point $Q(2, 8, 4)$ is:

$$\vec{PQ} = \langle 2 - 0, 8 - 0, 4 - 5 \rangle = \langle 2, 8, -1 \rangle$$

The normal vector $\mathbf{n}$ to the plane $2x + y + z = 5$ is given by the coefficients of $x, y, z$:

$$\mathbf{n} = \langle 2, 1, 1 \rangle$$

The perpendicular distance $D$ from $Q$ to the plane is the magnitude of the scalar projection of $\vec{PQ}$ onto $\mathbf{n}$:

$$D = \frac{|\vec{PQ} \cdot \mathbf{n}|}{\|\mathbf{n}\|}$$

Compute the dot product in the numerator:

$$\vec{PQ} \cdot \mathbf{n} = (2)(2) + (8)(1) + (-1)(1) = 4 + 8 - 1 = 11$$

Compute the norm of the normal vector in the denominator:

$$\|\mathbf{n}\| = \sqrt{2^2 + 1^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{4 + 1 + 1} = \sqrt{6}$$

Substituting these values gives:

$$D = \frac{|11|}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{11}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{11\sqrt{6}}{6}$$

Method 2: Direct Point-to-Plane Formula

Writing the plane in standard Cartesian form $ax + by + cz + d = 0$:

$$2x + y + z - 5 = 0 \implies a = 2, \; b = 1, \; c = 1, \; d = -5$$

For point $Q(x_0, y_0, z_0) = (2, 8, 4)$, the standard distance formula gives:

$$D = \frac{|a x_0 + b y_0 + c z_0 + d|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}} = \frac{|2(2) + 1(8) + 1(4) - 5|}{\sqrt{2^2 + 1^2 + 1^2}} = \frac{|4 + 8 + 4 - 5|}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{11}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{11\sqrt{6}}{6}$$
Ex 92 Medium Distances in SpaceParallel Planes
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 92: Distance Between Two Parallel Planes

🎯 High — recurring KFUPM Exam staple (e.g. Term 212 Exam 1 Q15, Term 222 Exam 1 Q13), testing verification of parallelism and application of the parallel planes distance formula.
Problem Statement

Verify that the two planes are parallel and find the distance between the planes:

$$\mathcal{P}_1: \quad 2x + 7y + z = 13$$$$\mathcal{P}_2: \quad 2x + 7y + z = 9$$
Boxed Final Answer

$$\boxed{D = \frac{2\sqrt{6}}{9} \approx 0.544}$$

We are given the equations of two planes:

$$\mathcal{P}_1: 2x + 7y + z = 13 \quad \text{and} \quad \mathcal{P}_2: 2x + 7y + z = 9$$

Step 1: Verify that the Planes are Parallel

Extract the normal vectors from the coefficients of $x, y, z$:

$$\mathbf{n}_1 = \langle 2, 7, 1 \rangle, \quad \mathbf{n}_2 = \langle 2, 7, 1 \rangle$$

Since $\mathbf{n}_1 = 1 \cdot \mathbf{n}_2$, the normal vectors are identical (and hence linearly dependent), confirming that the planes $\mathcal{P}_1$ and $\mathcal{P}_2$ are parallel.

Step 2: Calculate the Distance (Point-to-Plane Method)

Choose an arbitrary point $P$ lying on plane $\mathcal{P}_1$. Setting $x = 0$ and $y = 0$:

$$2(0) + 7(0) + z = 13 \implies P(0, 0, 13) \in \mathcal{P}_1$$

Choose an arbitrary point $Q$ lying on plane $\mathcal{P}_2$. Setting $x = 0$ and $y = 0$:

$$2(0) + 7(0) + z = 9 \implies Q(0, 0, 9) \in \mathcal{P}_2$$

Form the displacement vector $\vec{PQ}$ connecting the two planes:

$$\vec{PQ} = \langle 0 - 0, 0 - 0, 9 - 13 \rangle = \langle 0, 0, -4 \rangle$$

The perpendicular distance between the two planes is the scalar projection of $\vec{PQ}$ onto $\mathbf{n}_1$:

$$D = \frac{|\vec{PQ} \cdot \mathbf{n}_1|}{\|\mathbf{n}_1\|} = \frac{|(0)(2) + (0)(7) + (-4)(1)|}{\sqrt{2^2 + 7^2 + 1^2}} = \frac{|-4|}{\sqrt{4 + 49 + 1}} = \frac{4}{\sqrt{54}}$$

Step 3: Simplify the Radical

Notice that $\sqrt{54} = \sqrt{9 \times 6} = 3\sqrt{6}$. Rationalizing the denominator:

$$D = \frac{4}{3\sqrt{6}} = \frac{4\sqrt{6}}{3 \times 6} = \frac{2\sqrt{6}}{9}$$

Alternative: Direct Parallel Planes Formula

For parallel planes $ax + by + cz = d_1$ and $ax + by + cz = d_2$ with matching normal coefficients, the distance is directly:

$$D = \frac{|d_1 - d_2|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}} = \frac{|13 - 9|}{\sqrt{2^2 + 7^2 + 1^2}} = \frac{4}{\sqrt{54}} = \frac{2\sqrt{6}}{9}$$
Ex 96 Medium Distances in SpacePoint-to-Line Distance
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 96: Distance Between a Point and a Line

🎯 High — standard KFUPM Major Exam I question (e.g. Term 231 Exam 1 Q16, Term 242 Exam 1 Q10), testing Theorem 11.14: $D = \frac{\|\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{u}\|}{\|\mathbf{u}\|}$.
Problem Statement

Find the distance between the point and the line given by the set of parametric equations:

Point: $(1, -2, 4)$

Line: $x = 2t, \quad y = t - 3, \quad z = 2t + 2$

Boxed Final Answer

$$\boxed{D = \frac{\sqrt{5}}{3} \approx 0.745}$$

We are given the point $Q(1, -2, 4)$ and the line $L$ parameterized by:

$$x = 2t, \quad y = t - 3, \quad z = 2t + 2$$

Step 1: Identify Direction Vector and a Base Point on the Line

The coefficients of $t$ define the direction vector $\mathbf{u}$ of the line:

$$\mathbf{u} = \langle 2, 1, 2 \rangle$$

Setting the parameter $t = 0$ provides a base point $P$ on $L$:

$$P = (2(0), 0 - 3, 2(0) + 2) = (0, -3, 2)$$

Step 2: Form the Vector $\vec{PQ}$

Construct the vector connecting point $P$ on the line to the target point $Q(1, -2, 4)$:

$$\vec{PQ} = \langle 1 - 0, -2 - (-3), 4 - 2 \rangle = \langle 1, 1, 2 \rangle$$

Step 3: Compute the Cross Product $\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{u}$

Set up the cross product determinant:

$$\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{u} = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 1 & 1 & 2 \\ 2 & 1 & 2 \end{vmatrix}$$

Expanding by minors across the first row:

$$\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{u} = \mathbf{i}\big((1)(2) - (2)(1)\big) - \mathbf{j}\big((1)(2) - (2)(2)\big) + \mathbf{k}\big((1)(1) - (1)(2)\big)$$$$\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{u} = \mathbf{i}(2 - 2) - \mathbf{j}(2 - 4) + \mathbf{k}(1 - 2) = 0\mathbf{i} + 2\mathbf{j} - 1\mathbf{k} = \langle 0, 2, -1 \rangle$$

Step 4: Compute Magnitudes and Distance

By Theorem 11.14, the perpendicular distance from point $Q$ to line $L$ is:

$$D = \frac{\|\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{u}\|}{\|\mathbf{u}\|}$$

Calculate the Euclidean norms:

$$\|\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{u}\| = \sqrt{0^2 + 2^2 + (-1)^2} = \sqrt{0 + 4 + 1} = \sqrt{5}$$$$\|\mathbf{u}\| = \sqrt{2^2 + 1^2 + 2^2} = \sqrt{4 + 1 + 4} = \sqrt{9} = 3$$

Therefore, the exact distance is:

$$D = \frac{\sqrt{5}}{3}$$
Ex 99 Hard Distances in SpaceParallel Lines
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 99: Distance Between Two Parallel Lines

🎯 High — featured in KFUPM Exam 1 advanced geometry sections, verifying parallelism via proportionality and reducing the problem to point-to-line distance.
Problem Statement

Verify that the two lines are parallel and find the distance between the lines:

$$L_1: \quad x = 2 - t, \quad y = 3 + 2t, \quad z = 4 + t$$$$L_2: \quad x = 3t, \quad y = 1 - 6t, \quad z = 4 - 3t$$
Boxed Final Answer

$$\boxed{D = \frac{\sqrt{66}}{3} \approx 2.708}$$

We are given two parameterized lines in $\mathbb{R}^3$:

$$L_1: x = 2 - t, \; y = 3 + 2t, \; z = 4 + t$$$$L_2: x = 3s, \; y = 1 - 6s, \; z = 4 - 3s$$

Step 1: Verify that the Lines are Parallel

Extract the direction vectors from the parametric equations:

$$\mathbf{v}_1 = \langle -1, 2, 1 \rangle, \quad \mathbf{v}_2 = \langle 3, -6, -3 \rangle$$

Notice the proportionality between components:

$$\mathbf{v}_2 = -3 \langle -1, 2, 1 \rangle = -3 \mathbf{v}_1$$

Since $\mathbf{v}_2$ is a non-zero scalar multiple of $\mathbf{v}_1$, the lines $L_1$ and $L_2$ are parallel.

Step 2: Choose Representative Points on Each Line

Setting parameter $t = 0$ on line $L_1$ gives point $Q$:

$$Q = (2, 3, 4) \in L_1$$

Setting parameter $s = 0$ on line $L_2$ gives point $P$:

$$P = (0, 1, 4) \in L_2$$

The displacement vector from $P$ to $Q$ is:

$$\vec{PQ} = \langle 2 - 0, 3 - 1, 4 - 4 \rangle = \langle 2, 2, 0 \rangle$$

Step 3: Compute the Cross Product $\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{v}_1$

The distance between the parallel lines equals the perpendicular distance from point $Q$ on $L_1$ to the line $L_2$:

$$\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{v}_1 = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 2 & 2 & 0 \\ -1 & 2 & 1 \end{vmatrix}$$

Expanding the determinant:

$$\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{v}_1 = \mathbf{i}\big((2)(1) - (0)(2)\big) - \mathbf{j}\big((2)(1) - (0)(-1)\big) + \mathbf{k}\big((2)(2) - (2)(-1)\big)$$$$\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{v}_1 = 2\mathbf{i} - 2\mathbf{j} + (4 + 2)\mathbf{k} = \langle 2, -2, 6 \rangle$$

Step 4: Compute Magnitudes and Distance

Calculate the norms:

$$\|\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{v}_1\| = \sqrt{2^2 + (-2)^2 + 6^2} = \sqrt{4 + 4 + 36} = \sqrt{44} = 2\sqrt{11}$$$$\|\mathbf{v}_1\| = \sqrt{(-1)^2 + 2^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{1 + 4 + 1} = \sqrt{6}$$

Apply the point-to-line distance formula:

$$D = \frac{\|\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{v}_1\|}{\|\mathbf{v}_1\|} = \frac{2\sqrt{11}}{\sqrt{6}} = \frac{2\sqrt{66}}{6} = \frac{\sqrt{66}}{3}$$

Check with direction vector $\mathbf{v}_2$: $\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{v}_2 = \langle -6, 6, -18 \rangle \implies \|\vec{PQ} \times \mathbf{v}_2\| = \sqrt{396} = 6\sqrt{11}$, and $\|\mathbf{v}_2\| = \sqrt{54} = 3\sqrt{6}$. Hence $D = \frac{6\sqrt{11}}{3\sqrt{6}} = \frac{\sqrt{66}}{3}$, yielding an identical result.

Ex 110 Medium Distances in SpaceLine Parallel to Plane
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 110: Distance Between a Line and a Parallel Plane

🎯 High — frequent KFUPM exam question testing verification of line-plane parallelism via dot product $(\mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{v} = 0)$ and computing distance via point-to-plane projection.
Problem Statement

Show that the plane $2x - y - 3z = 4$ is parallel to the line

$$x = -2 + 2t, \quad y = -1 + 4t, \quad z = 4$$

and find the distance between them.

Boxed Final Answer

$$\boxed{D = \frac{19\sqrt{14}}{14} \approx 5.078}$$

We are given the plane $\mathcal{P}: 2x - y - 3z = 4$ and the parameterized line $L$:

$$x = -2 + 2t, \quad y = -1 + 4t, \quad z = 4$$

Step 1: Verify that the Line is Parallel to the Plane

Extract the normal vector $\mathbf{n}$ of the plane and the direction vector $\mathbf{v}$ of the line:

$$\mathbf{n} = \langle 2, -1, -3 \rangle, \quad \mathbf{v} = \langle 2, 4, 0 \rangle$$

Compute their dot product to check orthogonality:

$$\mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{v} = (2)(2) + (-1)(4) + (-3)(0) = 4 - 4 + 0 = 0$$

Because $\mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{v} = 0$, the line's direction is perpendicular to the plane's normal, meaning the line is parallel to the plane (or contained entirely within it).

To verify the line does not lie inside the plane, evaluate the point at $t = 0$, $Q(-2, -1, 4)$, in the plane's equation:

$$2(-2) - (-1) - 3(4) = -4 + 1 - 12 = -15 \neq 4$$

Since the point does not satisfy the plane equation, the line is strictly parallel to the plane.

Step 2: Calculate the Distance Between the Line and the Plane

Because the line is parallel to the plane, the perpendicular distance is uniform and equals the distance from any point on the line to the plane.

Using $Q(x_0, y_0, z_0) = (-2, -1, 4)$ and the Cartesian plane equation $2x - y - 3z - 4 = 0$:

$$D = \frac{|a x_0 + b y_0 + c z_0 + d|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}}$$Substitute the coordinates:$$D = \frac{|2(-2) - 1(-1) - 3(4) - 4|}{\sqrt{2^2 + (-1)^2 + (-3)^2}} = \frac{|-4 + 1 - 12 - 4|}{\sqrt{4 + 1 + 9}} = \frac{|-19|}{\sqrt{14}} = \frac{19}{\sqrt{14}}$$Rationalizing the denominator:$$D = \frac{19\sqrt{14}}{14}$$
Ex 108 Medium-Hard Distances in SpaceSpheres
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 108: Finding an Equation of a Sphere Tangent to a Plane

🎯 Very High — directly tested on KFUPM Major Exam I & II (e.g. Term 202 Exam 1 Q9, Term 221 Exam 1 Q12), requiring computation of the sphere radius using point-to-plane distance.
Problem Statement

Find the standard equation of the sphere with center $(-3, 2, 4)$ that is tangent to the plane given by

$$2x + 4y - 3z = 8$$
Boxed Final Answer

$$\boxed{(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 + (z - 4)^2 = \frac{324}{29}}$$

A sphere is tangent to a plane if and only if the radius $r$ of the sphere is precisely equal to the perpendicular distance $D$ from its center $C(-3, 2, 4)$ to the tangent plane.

Step 1: Compute the Radius via the Point-to-Plane Distance Formula

Write the tangent plane in standard form $ax + by + cz + d = 0$:

$$2x + 4y - 3z - 8 = 0 \implies a = 2, \; b = 4, \; c = -3, \; d = -8$$

For the sphere center $C(x_0, y_0, z_0) = (-3, 2, 4)$, the perpendicular distance is:

$$r = D = \frac{|a x_0 + b y_0 + c z_0 + d|}{\sqrt{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}}$$Substitute the coordinates into the formula:$$r = \frac{|2(-3) + 4(2) - 3(4) - 8|}{\sqrt{2^2 + 4^2 + (-3)^2}} = \frac{|-6 + 8 - 12 - 8|}{\sqrt{4 + 16 + 9}} = \frac{|-18|}{\sqrt{29}} = \frac{18}{\sqrt{29}}$$

Step 2: Construct the Standard Sphere Equation

The standard equation of a sphere with center $(h, k, l)$ and radius $r$ is:

$$(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 + (z - l)^2 = r^2$$

Squaring the radius $r = \frac{18}{\sqrt{29}}$:

$$r^2 = \left(\frac{18}{\sqrt{29}}\right)^2 = \frac{324}{29}$$

With center $(h, k, l) = (-3, 2, 4)$, the equation becomes:

$$(x - (-3))^2 + (y - 2)^2 + (z - 4)^2 = \frac{324}{29}$$$$(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 + (z - 4)^2 = \frac{324}{29}$$
Ex 119 Hard Distances in SpaceSkew Lines
★ KFUPM Aligned

Section Project: Shortest Distance Between Two Skew Lines

🎯 Very High — a classic KFUPM Exam 1 advanced question (e.g. Term 211 Exam 1 Q15, Term 222 Exam 1 Q14, Term 241 Exam 1 Q13), tested via both the parallel planes construction and the scalar triple product formula $D = \frac{|\vec{P_1 P_2} \cdot (\mathbf{v}_1 \times \mathbf{v}_2)|}{\|\mathbf{v}_1 \times \mathbf{v}_2\|}$.
Problem Statement

Two lines in space are skew if they are neither parallel nor intersecting. Consider the following two lines in space:

$$L_1: \quad x = 4 + 5t, \quad y = 5 + 5t, \quad z = 1 - 4t$$$$L_2: \quad x = 4 + s, \quad y = -6 + 8s, \quad z = 7 - 3s$$

(a) Show that these lines are not parallel.

(b) Show that these lines do not intersect, and therefore are skew lines.

(c) Find the shortest distance between the two skew lines.

Boxed Final Answer

$$\boxed{D = \frac{89\sqrt{1635}}{1635} \approx 2.201}$$

Two lines in three dimensions are skew if they are neither parallel nor intersecting.

The shortest distance between two skew lines is the perpendicular distance between two parallel planes that contain them.

Part (a): Verify Non-Parallelism

Extract the direction vectors of the lines:

$$\mathbf{v}_1 = \langle 5, 5, -4 \rangle, \quad \mathbf{v}_2 = \langle 1, 8, -3 \rangle$$

Check for proportionality between the components:

$$\frac{5}{1} = 5 \neq \frac{5}{8}$$

Because no single scalar $k$ satisfies $\mathbf{v}_1 = k \mathbf{v}_2$, the lines $L_1$ and $L_2$ are not parallel.

Part (b): Verify Non-Intersection

Set the coordinates equal to test for a common intersection point:

$$4 + 5t = 4 + s \implies s = 5t \quad \text{--- (1)}$$$$5 + 5t = -6 + 8s \quad \text{--- (2)}$$$$1 - 4t = 7 - 3s \quad \text{--- (3)}$$

Substituting (1) into (2):

$$5 + 5t = -6 + 8(5t) = -6 + 40t \implies 35t = 11 \implies t = \frac{11}{35}, \quad s = 5\left(\frac{11}{35}\right) = \frac{11}{7}$$

Substitute $t = \frac{11}{35}$ and $s = \frac{11}{7}$ into the $z$-equation (3) to verify consistency:

$$\text{LHS} = 1 - 4\left(\frac{11}{35}\right) = 1 - \frac{44}{35} = -\frac{9}{35}$$$$\text{RHS} = 7 - 3\left(\frac{11}{7}\right) = 7 - \frac{33}{7} = \frac{16}{7} = \frac{80}{35}$$

Since $-\frac{9}{35} \neq \frac{80}{35}$, the system has no solution. The lines do not intersect. Since they are neither parallel nor intersecting, $L_1$ and $L_2$ are skew lines.

Part (c): Shortest Distance Between the Skew Lines

The common perpendicular direction $\mathbf{n}$ is orthogonal to both lines and is given by the cross product of their direction vectors:

$$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{v}_1 \times \mathbf{v}_2 = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 5 & 5 & -4 \\ 1 & 8 & -3 \end{vmatrix}$$

Expanding across the first row:

$$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{i}\big((5)(-3) - (-4)(8)\big) - \mathbf{j}\big((5)(-3) - (-4)(1)\big) + \mathbf{k}\big((5)(8) - (5)(1)\big)$$$$\mathbf{n} = \mathbf{i}(-15 + 32) - \mathbf{j}(-15 + 4) + \mathbf{k}(40 - 5) = 17\mathbf{i} + 11\mathbf{j} + 35\mathbf{k} = \langle 17, 11, 35 \rangle$$

Select base points $P_1$ on $L_1$ (at $t = 0$) and $P_2$ on $L_2$ (at $s = 0$):

$$P_1(4, 5, 1) \in L_1, \quad P_2(4, -6, 7) \in L_2$$

Construct the displacement vector connecting the two lines:

$$\vec{P_1 P_2} = \langle 4 - 4, -6 - 5, 7 - 1 \rangle = \langle 0, -11, 6 \rangle$$

The shortest distance $D$ between two skew lines is the scalar projection of $\vec{P_1 P_2}$ onto the common normal $\mathbf{n}$:

$$D = \frac{|\vec{P_1 P_2} \cdot \mathbf{n}|}{\|\mathbf{n}\|} = \frac{|\vec{P_1 P_2} \cdot (\mathbf{v}_1 \times \mathbf{v}_2)|}{\|\mathbf{v}_1 \times \mathbf{v}_2\|}$$

Evaluate the numerator dot product:

$$\vec{P_1 P_2} \cdot \mathbf{n} = (0)(17) + (-11)(11) + (6)(35) = 0 - 121 + 210 = 89$$

Evaluate the denominator norm:

$$\|\mathbf{n}\| = \sqrt{17^2 + 11^2 + 35^2} = \sqrt{289 + 121 + 1225} = \sqrt{1635}$$

Thus, the exact shortest distance between the two skew lines is:

$$D = \frac{89}{\sqrt{1635}} = \frac{89\sqrt{1635}}{1635}$$

11.6 Surfaces in Space (Part 1)

Suggested Textbook Practice Problems • 5 Curated Exercises

Jump to problem:
Ex 6 Medium Quadric SurfacesHyperboloid of Two Sheets
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 6: Classification and Traces of a Second-Degree Equation

🎯 A classic KFUPM Math 201 Major 1 question type. Students are presented with an unnormalized second-degree equation and asked to identify the surface type or find the trace in a given plane (e.g., 'The trace of $15x^2 - 4y^2 + 15z^2 = -4$ in the plane $y = 2$ is: (a) a circle, (b) an ellipse, (c) a hyperbola, (d) empty'). Dividing by $-4$ carefully to avoid sign errors is the key algebraic step.
Problem Statement
Consider the quadric surface given by the second-degree equation: $$15x^2 - 4y^2 + 15z^2 = -4$$ (a) Write the equation in canonical standard form. (b) Classify the quadric surface. (c) Identify the axis of symmetry and the coordinates of the vertices. (d) Find and describe the traces in the coordinate planes ($xy$-plane, $yz$-plane, and $xz$-plane), as well as traces parallel to the $xz$-plane ($y = k$).
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{Hyperboloid of two sheets centered at }(0,0,0) \text{ with axis of symmetry along the } y\text{-axis and vertices at } (0, \pm 1, 0)}$$ Standard Form: $$\frac{y^2}{1} - \frac{x^2}{4/15} - \frac{z^2}{4/15} = 1$$
**Step 1: Write the equation in canonical standard form.** The given equation is: $$15x^2 - 4y^2 + 15z^2 = -4$$ To put this into standard form with a positive constant $1$ on the right-hand side, divide every term by $-4$: $$\frac{15x^2}{-4} - \frac{4y^2}{-4} + \frac{15z^2}{-4} = \frac{-4}{-4}$$ $$-\frac{15x^2}{4} + y^2 - \frac{15z^2}{4} = 1$$ Rearranging with the positive term first and writing coefficients as denominators: $$\frac{y^2}{1^2} - \frac{x^2}{4/15} - \frac{z^2}{4/15} = 1 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \frac{y^2}{1^2} - \frac{x^2}{(2/\sqrt{15})^2} - \frac{z^2}{(2/\sqrt{15})^2} = 1$$ **Step 2: Classify the quadric surface.** In this standard equation: 1. All three variables ($x, y, z$) appear to the second power. 2. Exactly two terms have negative signs ($-x^2$ and $-z^2$) and one term has a positive sign ($+y^2$). 3. The right-hand side is $+1$. This matches the canonical signature of a **hyperboloid of two sheets**: $$\frac{y^2}{b^2} - \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{z^2}{c^2} = 1$$ Because the coefficients of $x^2$ and $z^2$ are equal ($a = c = 2/\sqrt{15}$), the surface is a circular hyperboloid of two sheets (a surface of revolution about the $y$-axis). **Step 3: Determine the axis of symmetry and vertices.** - **Axis of symmetry:** The axis corresponds to the single variable with the positive coefficient, which is the **$y$-axis**. - **Vertices:** Setting $x = 0$ and $z = 0$ yields: $$y^2 = 1 \implies y = \pm 1$$ Thus, the two vertices are located at: $$(0, 1, 0) \quad \text{and} \quad (0, -1, 0)$$ - Setting $y = 0$ gives $-\frac{15}{4}(x^2 + z^2) = 1$, which has no real solutions. Hence, there is no part of the surface in the slab $-1 < y < 1$. **Step 4: Trace analysis.** - **$xy$-trace ($z = 0$):** $$y^2 - \frac{15x^2}{4} = 1 \implies \frac{y^2}{1} - \frac{x^2}{4/15} = 1$$ This is a **hyperbola** in the $xy$-plane opening along the $y$-axis with vertices $(0, \pm 1, 0)$. - **$yz$-trace ($x = 0$):** $$y^2 - \frac{15z^2}{4} = 1 \implies \frac{y^2}{1} - \frac{z^2}{4/15} = 1$$ This is a **hyperbola** in the $yz$-plane opening along the $y$-axis with vertices $(0, \pm 1, 0)$. - **$xz$-trace ($y = 0$):** $$-\frac{15}{4}(x^2 + z^2) = 1 \implies x^2 + z^2 = -\frac{4}{15}$$ Since $x^2 + z^2 \ge 0$ for all real $x, z$, there is **no trace** (empty set $\varnothing$). - **Traces in planes parallel to the $xz$-plane ($y = k$):** $$\frac{15}{4}(x^2 + z^2) = k^2 - 1 \implies x^2 + z^2 = \frac{4}{15}(k^2 - 1)$$ - For $|k| < 1$: No real trace. - For $k = \pm 1$: A single point $(0, \pm 1, 0)$ (the vertices). - For $|k| > 1$: A **circle** of radius $r = \frac{2}{\sqrt{15}}\sqrt{k^2 - 1}$.
Ex 9 Easy Quadric SurfacesElliptic Paraboloid
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 9: Canonical Form and Trace Analysis of an Elliptic Paraboloid

🎯 Extremely frequent in KFUPM Major 1 multiple-choice exams. Students are expected to immediately recognize $y = Ax^2 + Bz^2$ as an elliptic paraboloid opening along $+y$, differentiate it from a hyperbolic paraboloid ($y = Ax^2 - Bz^2$), and identify the elliptic nature of traces for $y = k > 0$.
Problem Statement
Consider the quadric surface given by the equation: $$4x^2 - 4y + z^2 = 0$$ (a) Write the equation in standard form by isolating the linear variable. (b) Classify the surface and state its vertex and axis of symmetry. (c) Determine the trace in each coordinate plane ($xy$-plane, $yz$-plane, and $xz$-plane). (d) Describe the cross-sections parallel to the $xz$-plane in planes $y = k$ for $k > 0$.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{Elliptic Paraboloid with vertex at }(0,0,0) \text{ opening along the positive } y\text{-axis}}$$ Standard Form: $$y = x^2 + \frac{z^2}{4} \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \frac{y}{1} = \frac{x^2}{1^2} + \frac{z^2}{2^2}$$
**Step 1: Write in standard form.** The given equation contains two squared variables ($x^2$ and $z^2$) and one linear variable ($y$): $$4x^2 - 4y + z^2 = 0$$ Isolate the linear term $4y$: $$4y = 4x^2 + z^2$$ Divide both sides by $4$: $$y = x^2 + \frac{z^2}{4}$$ In canonical standard form: $$\frac{y}{c} = \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{z^2}{b^2} \implies y = \frac{x^2}{1^2} + \frac{z^2}{2^2}$$ where $a = 1$, $b = 2$, and $c = 1$. **Step 2: Classify the surface, vertex, and axis.** - **Classification:** Because one variable is linear ($y$) and the other two variables are squared with the **same sign** ($+x^2$ and $+z^2/4$), the surface is an **elliptic paraboloid**. - **Vertex:** Setting $x = 0$ and $z = 0$ yields $y = 0$, so the vertex is at the origin: $$(0, 0, 0)$$ - **Axis of symmetry and opening direction:** The axis is the line along which the linear variable lies, which is the **$y$-axis**. Since $x^2 + \frac{z^2}{4} \ge 0$ for all real $x, z$, we must have $y \ge 0$. Hence, the paraboloid opens along the **positive $y$-axis**. **Step 3: Analyze the traces in the coordinate planes.** - **$xy$-trace ($z = 0$):** $$y = x^2$$ This is a **parabola** opening upward along the $+y$-axis with vertex $(0,0,0)$. - **$yz$-trace ($x = 0$):** $$y = \frac{z^2}{4}$$ This is a **parabola** opening upward along the $+y$-axis with vertex $(0,0,0)$. - **$xz$-trace ($y = 0$):** $$x^2 + \frac{z^2}{4} = 0$$ Since both terms are non-negative, the only real solution is $x = 0, z = 0$. The trace is a **single point**: the origin $(0, 0, 0)$. **Step 4: Cross-sections parallel to the $xz$-plane ($y = k$ for $k > 0$).** Substituting $y = k$ into the standard equation: $$x^2 + \frac{z^2}{4} = k$$ Dividing by $k > 0$: $$\frac{x^2}{k} + \frac{z^2}{4k} = 1 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \frac{x^2}{(\sqrt{k})^2} + \frac{z^2}{(2\sqrt{k})^2} = 1$$ These cross-sections are **ellipses** centered at $(0, k, 0)$ with semi-major axis $2\sqrt{k}$ along $z$ and semi-minor axis $\sqrt{k}$ along $x$.
Ex 11 Easy CylindersCircular Cylinder
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 11: Identification and Properties of a Cylindrical Surface

🎯 Tested directly in KFUPM Exam 1: Recognizing that an equation missing a variable in $\mathbb{R}^3$ represents a cylinder, not a 2D curve. Questions often test the direction of rulings (parallel to the missing variable's axis) and the cross-sectional shape.
Problem Statement
Describe and analyze the surface in $\mathbb{R}^3$ given by the equation: $$y^2 + z^2 = 9$$ (a) Identify the type of surface and explain which coordinate variable is unrestricted. (b) Specify the generating curve and the axis of symmetry. (c) Determine the traces of the surface in planes perpendicular to the $x$-axis ($x = k$) and in the coordinate planes.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{Right Circular Cylinder with radius } r = 3 \text{ and axis along the } x\text{-axis (rulings parallel to the } x\text{-axis)}}$$
**Step 1: Identify the missing variable and surface type.** The given equation in $\mathbb{R}^3$ is: $$y^2 + z^2 = 9$$ Notice that the variable **$x$ is completely absent** from the equation. - In $\mathbb{R}^3$, an equation that contains only two variables represents a **cylinder** whose generating lines (rulings) are parallel to the axis of the missing variable. - Because $x$ is missing, $x$ is free to take any real value in $(-\infty, \infty)$. **Step 2: Identify the generating curve and cylinder geometry.** In the $yz$-coordinate plane ($x = 0$), the equation: $$y^2 + z^2 = 9 = 3^2$$ represents a **circle** of radius $r = 3$ centered at the origin $(0, 0)$. Extending this circle parallel to the $x$-axis generates a **right circular cylinder**. - **Central axis:** The **$x$-axis** ($y = 0, z = 0$). - **Radius:** $r = \sqrt{9} = 3$. **Step 3: Analyze traces.** - **Planes perpendicular to the $x$-axis ($x = k$ for any constant $k \in \mathbb{R}$):** $$y^2 + z^2 = 9$$ Every cross-section is an identical **circle** of radius $3$ centered at $(k, 0, 0)$. - **$yz$-trace ($x = 0$):** The circle $y^2 + z^2 = 9$ of radius $3$ centered at $(0,0,0)$. - **$xy$-trace ($z = 0$):** $$y^2 = 9 \implies y = \pm 3$$ This trace consists of **two parallel lines** in the $xy$-plane, $y = 3$ and $y = -3$, running parallel to the $x$-axis. - **$xz$-trace ($y = 0$):** $$z^2 = 9 \implies z = \pm 3$$ This trace consists of **two parallel lines** in the $xz$-plane, $z = 3$ and $z = -3$, running parallel to the $x$-axis.
Ex 12 Easy CylindersParabolic Cylinder
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 12: Identification and Traces of a Parabolic Cylinder

🎯 Commonly tested in KFUPM Math 201 conceptual questions: Distinguishing a parabolic cylinder ($y^2 + z = 6$, where $x$ is missing) from an elliptic or hyperbolic paraboloid ($x^2 + y^2 + z = 6$). KFUPM exams specifically test identifying the rulings and coordinate traces.
Problem Statement
Describe and analyze the surface in $\mathbb{R}^3$ given by the equation: $$y^2 + z = 6$$ (a) Identify the surface type and the missing variable. (b) Find the generating curve in the $yz$-plane, including its vertex, opening direction, and intercepts. (c) Describe the traces of the surface in planes $x = k$ and the traces in the $xy$- and $xz$-coordinate planes.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{Parabolic Cylinder with rulings parallel to the } x\text{-axis and generating parabola } z = 6 - y^2}$$
**Step 1: Identify the missing variable and classify the surface.** The equation in $\mathbb{R}^3$ is: $$y^2 + z = 6$$ - The variable **$x$ is absent**, meaning $x$ can take any real value in $(-\infty, \infty)$. - One variable ($y$) is squared and the other variable ($z$) is linear. In two dimensions, this is a parabola. - Therefore, in three-dimensional space $\mathbb{R}^3$, the surface is a **parabolic cylinder** with rulings parallel to the **$x$-axis**. **Step 2: Analyze the generating curve in the $yz$-plane.** Solve for the linear variable $z$: $$z = 6 - y^2 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad y^2 = -(z - 6)$$ In the $yz$-plane ($x = 0$): - **Curve type:** Parabola. - **Vertex:** $(y, z) = (0, 6)$. - **Axis of the parabola:** The $z$-axis, opening **downward** toward negative $z$ (since the coefficient of $y^2$ is $-1$). - **$y$-intercepts (where $z = 0$):** $$0 = 6 - y^2 \implies y^2 = 6 \implies y = \pm \sqrt{6}$$ Intercepts at $(0, \sqrt{6}, 0)$ and $(0, -\sqrt{6}, 0)$. **Step 3: Analyze traces in 3D space.** - **Planes parallel to the $yz$-plane ($x = k$ for any $k \in \mathbb{R}$):** The cross-section is the identical parabola $z = 6 - y^2$ with vertex at $(k, 0, 6)$. - **$xy$-trace ($z = 0$):** $$y^2 = 6 \implies y = \pm \sqrt{6}$$ This trace consists of **two parallel lines** in the $xy$-plane: $y = \sqrt{6}$ and $y = -\sqrt{6}$, both parallel to the $x$-axis. - **$xz$-trace ($y = 0$):** $$z = 6$$ This trace is a **single horizontal line** in the $xz$-plane parallel to the $x$-axis, passing through the vertices of all the parabolic cross-sections.
Ex 17 Medium Quadric SurfacesHyperboloid of One Sheet
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 17: Canonical Reduction and Traces of a Quadric Surface

🎯 A staple KFUPM exam problem. Students are frequently asked: 'The trace of $16x^2 - y^2 + 16z^2 = 4$ in the $xz$-plane is:' with choices (a) circle of radius $1/2$, (b) circle of radius $2$, (c) ellipse, (d) hyperbola. Recognizing that the trace in the plane perpendicular to the axis of symmetry is a circle of radius $1/2$ is an essential KFUPM skill.
Problem Statement
Consider the quadric surface given by the equation: $$16x^2 - y^2 + 16z^2 = 4$$ (a) Reduce the equation to canonical standard form. (b) Classify the quadric surface. (c) Identify its center and axis of symmetry. (d) Find and describe the traces in the three coordinate planes ($xy$-plane, $xz$-plane, and $yz$-plane).
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{Hyperboloid of one sheet centered at }(0,0,0) \text{ with axis of symmetry along the } y\text{-axis}}$$ Standard Form: $$4x^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} + 4z^2 = 1 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \frac{x^2}{(1/2)^2} - \frac{y^2}{2^2} + \frac{z^2}{(1/2)^2} = 1$$
**Step 1: Reduce to canonical standard form.** The given equation is: $$16x^2 - y^2 + 16z^2 = 4$$ Divide both sides by $4$ so that the right-hand side is $1$: $$\frac{16x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{4} + \frac{16z^2}{4} = 1$$ $$4x^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} + 4z^2 = 1$$ Writing coefficients as squared semi-axes in the denominators: $$\frac{x^2}{1/4} - \frac{y^2}{4} + \frac{z^2}{1/4} = 1 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \frac{x^2}{(1/2)^2} - \frac{y^2}{2^2} + \frac{z^2}{(1/2)^2} = 1$$ **Step 2: Classify the quadric surface.** - All three variables $x, y, z$ are squared. - There are **two positive terms** ($+4x^2$ and $+4z^2$) and **one negative term** ($-\frac{y^2}{4}$). - The constant term on the right-hand side is $+1$. This matches the standard form of a **hyperboloid of one sheet**: $$\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} + \frac{z^2}{c^2} = 1$$ Because the coefficients of $x^2$ and $z^2$ are identical ($a = c = 1/2$), this is a **circular hyperboloid of one sheet** (a surface of revolution obtained by revolving a hyperbola around the $y$-axis). **Step 3: Center and axis of symmetry.** - **Center:** The surface is centered at the origin: $$(0, 0, 0)$$ - **Axis of symmetry:** The axis corresponds to the single variable preceded by the minus sign, which is the **$y$-axis**. **Step 4: Trace analysis.** - **$xy$-trace ($z = 0$):** $$4x^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1 \implies \frac{x^2}{(1/2)^2} - \frac{y^2}{2^2} = 1$$ This is a **hyperbola** in the $xy$-plane with vertices at $(\pm 1/2, 0, 0)$ and opening along the $x$-axis. - **$yz$-trace ($x = 0$):** $$4z^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1 \implies \frac{z^2}{(1/2)^2} - \frac{y^2}{2^2} = 1$$ This is a **hyperbola** in the $yz$-plane with vertices at $(0, 0, \pm 1/2)$ and opening along the $z$-axis. - **$xz$-trace ($y = 0$):** $$4x^2 + 4z^2 = 1 \implies x^2 + z^2 = \frac{1}{4} = \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^2$$ This is a **circle** of radius $r = \frac{1}{2}$ centered at $(0, 0, 0)$. This circle is the narrowest cross-section (throat or waist) of the hyperboloid. - **Traces in planes parallel to the $xz$-plane ($y = k$):** $$x^2 + z^2 = \frac{1}{4}\left(1 + \frac{k^2}{4}\right)$$ For every value of $k \in \mathbb{R}$, this is a **circle** with radius $r = \frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1 + \frac{k^2}{4}}$.
PRACTICE PROBLEMS • TOPIC 05 OF 12

11.6 Surfaces in Space (Part 2)

Suggested Textbook Practice Problems • 4 Curated Exercises

Ex 24 Medium Quadric SurfacesElliptic Cone
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 24: Canonical Classification and Traces of an Elliptic Cone

🎯 Frequently examined in KFUPM Math 201 exams. Students must identify that $x^2 - 2y^2 - 2z^2 = 0$ is a cone (RHS $= 0$) and NOT a hyperboloid. Questions often ask: 'The trace of $x^2 = 2y^2 + 2z^2$ in the plane $z = 0$ is a pair of intersecting lines' or 'in the plane $x = 2$ is a circle of radius $\sqrt{2}$'.
Problem Statement
Consider the quadric surface given by the equation: $$x^2 = 2y^2 + 2z^2$$ (a) Write the equation in canonical standard form. (b) Classify the surface and state the coordinates of its vertex and its axis of symmetry. (c) Find the traces of the surface in the three coordinate planes ($xy$-plane, $xz$-plane, and $yz$-plane). (d) Describe the traces in planes perpendicular to the axis of symmetry ($x = k$ for $k \neq 0$).
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{Right Circular Cone (Elliptic Cone) with vertex at }(0,0,0) \text{ and axis along the } x\text{-axis}}$$ Standard Form: $$\frac{x^2}{2} - y^2 - z^2 = 0 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad x^2 = 2(y^2 + z^2)$$
**Step 1: Write in canonical standard form.** The given equation is: $$x^2 = 2y^2 + 2z^2$$ Rearranging so that all quadratic terms are on one side: $$x^2 - 2y^2 - 2z^2 = 0$$ Dividing by $2$: $$\frac{x^2}{2} - \frac{y^2}{1} - \frac{z^2}{1} = 0 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad \frac{x^2}{(\sqrt{2})^2} - \frac{y^2}{1^2} - \frac{z^2}{1^2} = 0$$ **Step 2: Classify the surface.** - All three variables are squared. - The right-hand side is $0$ (a homogeneous second-degree equation). - One term has a positive sign ($+x^2/2$) and two terms have negative signs ($-y^2$ and $-z^2$). This is the standard form of an **elliptic cone**: $$\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} - \frac{z^2}{c^2} = 0$$ Because the coefficients of $y^2$ and $z^2$ are equal ($b = c = 1$), the cross-sections perpendicular to the axis are circles, making this a **right circular cone**. - **Vertex:** The vertex of the cone is at the origin: $$(0, 0, 0)$$ - **Axis of symmetry:** The axis corresponds to the isolated variable on the opposite side of the sign, which is the **$x$-axis**. **Step 3: Analyze coordinate traces.** - **$xy$-trace ($z = 0$):** $$x^2 = 2y^2 \implies y = \pm \frac{x}{\sqrt{2}} = \pm \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}x$$ This trace consists of **two intersecting straight lines** passing through the origin. - **$xz$-trace ($y = 0$):** $$x^2 = 2z^2 \implies z = \pm \frac{x}{\sqrt{2}} = \pm \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}x$$ This trace also consists of **two intersecting straight lines** passing through the origin. - **$yz$-trace ($x = 0$):** $$2y^2 + 2z^2 = 0 \implies y^2 + z^2 = 0$$ Since the sum of squares can only equal zero when each term is zero, the only real solution is $(y, z) = (0, 0)$. Thus, the trace is a **single point**: the vertex $(0, 0, 0)$. **Step 4: Cross-sections in planes perpendicular to the $x$-axis ($x = k$ for $k \neq 0$).** Substituting $x = k$ into $x^2 = 2(y^2 + z^2)$: $$k^2 = 2(y^2 + z^2) \implies y^2 + z^2 = \frac{k^2}{2} = \left(\frac{|k|}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^2$$ For every non-zero plane $x = k$, the cross-section is a **circle** centered at $(k, 0, 0)$ with radius $r = \frac{|k|}{\sqrt{2}}$.
Ex 45 Medium Locus SurfaceParaboloid
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 45: Surface of Points Equidistant from a Point and a Plane

🎯 A premier KFUPM conceptual problem. In 2D, the locus of points equidistant from a point and a line is a parabola; KFUPM tests whether students can generalize this to 3D: the locus of points equidistant from a point and a plane is a paraboloid. Students must carry out the algebra without making sign errors when squaring $(y - 2)^2$ and $(y + 2)^2$.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the surface consisting of all points $P(x, y, z)$ in $\mathbb{R}^3$ that are equidistant from the point $(0, 2, 0)$ and the plane $y = -2$. Identify the resulting quadric surface, its vertex, and its axis of symmetry.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{y = \frac{x^2}{8} + \frac{z^2}{8} \quad \text{(or } x^2 + z^2 = 8y\text{), which is a Circular Paraboloid with vertex at } (0,0,0) \text{ along the } +y\text{-axis}}$$
**Step 1: Set up the distance formulas.** Let $P(x, y, z)$ be any arbitrary point on the surface. 1. The distance from $P(x, y, z)$ to the fixed point (focus) $F(0, 2, 0)$ is: $$d_1 = \sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 2)^2 + (z - 0)^2} = \sqrt{x^2 + (y - 2)^2 + z^2}$$ 2. The distance from $P(x, y, z)$ to the directrix plane $y = -2$ (or $y + 2 = 0$) is the perpendicular distance: $$d_2 = |y - (-2)| = |y + 2|$$ **Step 2: Equate the two distances ($d_1 = d_2$).** Because the point $P$ is equidistant from the point and the plane: $$\sqrt{x^2 + (y - 2)^2 + z^2} = |y + 2|$$ Square both sides of the equation to eliminate the radical and absolute value: $$x^2 + (y - 2)^2 + z^2 = (y + 2)^2$$ **Step 3: Expand and simplify algebraic terms.** $$x^2 + (y^2 - 4y + 4) + z^2 = y^2 + 4y + 4$$ Subtract $y^2 + 4$ from both sides: $$x^2 - 4y + z^2 = 4y$$ Add $4y$ to both sides: $$x^2 + z^2 = 8y \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad y = \frac{x^2}{8} + \frac{z^2}{8}$$ **Step 4: Identify and classify the quadric surface.** - **Surface type:** The equation $y = \frac{x^2}{8} + \frac{z^2}{8}$ has one linear variable ($y$) and two squared variables with positive coefficients ($x^2$ and $z^2$). Therefore, it is an **elliptic paraboloid**. - Furthermore, because the coefficients of $x^2$ and $z^2$ are identical ($1/8 = 1/8$), the horizontal cross-sections are circles, so it is specifically a **circular paraboloid** (a paraboloid of revolution). - **Vertex:** Setting $x = 0$ and $z = 0$ gives $y = 0$, so the vertex is at: $$(0, 0, 0)$$ - **Axis of symmetry:** The **positive $y$-axis** ($y \ge 0$). - **Cross-sections parallel to the $xz$-plane ($y = k > 0$):** $$x^2 + z^2 = 8k$$ These traces are **circles** of radius $r = \sqrt{8k} = 2\sqrt{2k}$.
Ex 46 Medium Shifted QuadricsElliptic Paraboloid
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 46: Locus of Points Equidistant from a Point and the xy-Plane

🎯 A high-frequency KFUPM question type testing both shifted quadric geometry and locus derivations. In KFUPM exams, questions frequently present multiple-choice options with shifted vertices (e.g., vertex $(0,0,2)$ vs $(0,0,4)$ vs $(0,0,0)$). The vertex is the midpoint between the focus $(0,0,4)$ and the directrix plane $z=0$, so its $z$-coordinate is $\frac{4+0}{2}=2$.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the surface consisting of all points $P(x, y, z)$ that are equidistant from the point $(0, 0, 4)$ and the $xy$-plane. Identify the surface, determine its vertex, and express its equation in standard form.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{z - 2 = \frac{x^2}{8} + \frac{y^2}{8} \quad \text{(or } z = \frac{x^2 + y^2}{8} + 2\text{), which is an Elliptic Paraboloid shifted } 2 \text{ units upward with vertex at } (0,0,2)}$$
**Step 1: Set up the distance formulas.** Let $P(x, y, z)$ be a point on the surface. 1. The distance from $P(x, y, z)$ to the point $F(0, 0, 4)$ is: $$d_1 = \sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 0)^2 + (z - 4)^2} = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + (z - 4)^2}$$ 2. The distance from $P(x, y, z)$ to the $xy$-plane (equation $z = 0$) is the vertical distance: $$d_2 = |z|$$ **Step 2: Equate the distances and eliminate the square root.** Setting $d_1 = d_2$: $$\sqrt{x^2 + y^2 + (z - 4)^2} = |z|$$ Square both sides: $$x^2 + y^2 + (z - 4)^2 = z^2$$ **Step 3: Expand and complete the algebraic reduction.** Expand $(z - 4)^2$: $$x^2 + y^2 + (z^2 - 8z + 16) = z^2$$ Subtract $z^2$ from both sides: $$x^2 + y^2 - 8z + 16 = 0$$ Isolate the linear $z$ terms: $$8z - 16 = x^2 + y^2$$ Factor out $8$ on the left-hand side: $$8(z - 2) = x^2 + y^2$$ Divide both sides by $8$: $$z - 2 = \frac{x^2}{8} + \frac{y^2}{8} \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad z = \frac{x^2}{8} + \frac{y^2}{8} + 2$$ **Step 4: Identify and classify the surface.** - **Classification:** The equation relates a linear term in $z$ to the sum of two positive squared terms in $x$ and $y$. This is an **elliptic paraboloid** (circular paraboloid). - **Shift and Vertex:** The standard vertex form is: $$(z - z_0) = \frac{(x - x_0)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y - y_0)^2}{b^2}$$ Here $(x_0, y_0, z_0) = (0, 0, 2)$. Thus, the surface is an elliptic paraboloid with its vertex shifted $2$ units upward along the $z$-axis to: $$(0, 0, 2)$$ - **Axis and Opening Direction:** Because $x^2 + y^2 \ge 0$, we have $z - 2 \ge 0 \implies z \ge 2$. The paraboloid opens **upward along the positive $z$-axis**. - **Traces:** In horizontal planes $z = k$ with $k > 2$, the cross-sections are circles: $$x^2 + y^2 = 8(k - 2)$$ with radius $r = \sqrt{8(k - 2)}$.
Ex 50 Medium Intersection of SurfacesQuadric Surfaces
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 50: Planar Intersection of Two Quadric Surfaces

🎯 A classic KFUPM Exam 1 conceptual and calculation problem. Students are asked: 'The curve of intersection of $x^2 + 3y^2 - 2z^2 + 2y = 4$ and $2x^2 + 6y^2 - 4z^2 - 3x = 2$ lies in the plane:' with options like $3x + 4y = 6$, $3x - 4y = 6$, $2x + y = 4$, etc. Recognizing that a linear combination eliminates all second-degree terms is the fast exam shortcut.
Problem Statement
Consider the two quadric surfaces: $$S_1: \quad x^2 + 3y^2 - 2z^2 + 2y = 4$$ $$S_2: \quad 2x^2 + 6y^2 - 4z^2 - 3x = 2$$ (a) Show that the curve of intersection of these two surfaces lies entirely in a plane. (b) Find the Cartesian equation of this plane and state its normal vector.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\text{The curve of intersection lies in the plane } 3x + 4y = 6 \text{ with normal vector } \mathbf{n} = \langle 3, 4, 0 \rangle}$$
**Step 1: Analyze the equations of the two surfaces.** We are given two second-degree equations: $$S_1: \quad x^2 + 3y^2 - 2z^2 + 2y = 4 \tag{1}$$ $$S_2: \quad 2x^2 + 6y^2 - 4z^2 - 3x = 2 \tag{2}$$ Notice the quadratic parts of both equations: - Quadratic part of $S_1$: $x^2 + 3y^2 - 2z^2$ - Quadratic part of $S_2$: $2x^2 + 6y^2 - 4z^2 = 2(x^2 + 3y^2 - 2z^2)$ The coefficients of the quadratic terms in $S_2$ are exactly **twice** the corresponding coefficients in $S_1$. **Step 2: Eliminate the quadratic terms by linear combination.** Any point $(x, y, z)$ on the curve of intersection must simultaneously satisfy both equation (1) and equation (2). Multiply equation (1) by $2$: $$2(x^2 + 3y^2 - 2z^2 + 2y) = 2(4)$$ $$2x^2 + 6y^2 - 4z^2 + 4y = 8 \tag{3}$$ Now subtract equation (2) from equation (3): $$(2x^2 + 6y^2 - 4z^2 + 4y) - (2x^2 + 6y^2 - 4z^2 - 3x) = 8 - 2$$ Grouping like terms: $$(2x^2 - 2x^2) + (6y^2 - 6y^2) + (-4z^2 - (-4z^2)) + 4y - (-3x) = 6$$ $$(0) + (0) + (0) + 4y + 3x = 6$$ $$3x + 4y = 6$$ **Step 3: Geometric interpretation.** - The resulting equation: $$3x + 4y = 6$$ is a first-degree (linear) equation in three-dimensional space $\mathbb{R}^3$. - In $\mathbb{R}^3$, the general linear equation $Ax + By + Cz = D$ (where $A, B, C$ are not all zero) always represents a **plane**. - Here, $A = 3$, $B = 4$, and $C = 0$, so the normal vector to the plane is: $$\mathbf{n} = \langle 3, 4, 0 \rangle$$ Because $C = 0$, this plane is perpendicular to the $xy$-plane (parallel to the $z$-axis). - Since any point $(x, y, z)$ on the intersection of $S_1$ and $S_2$ must satisfy this linear equation, the entire curve of intersection lies in the plane $3x + 4y = 6$.

13.1 Functions of Several Variables
