Ex 7 Easy functions-of-several-variablesdefinition-of-function
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 7: Determining Whether an Implicit Equation Defines a Function

🎯 Directly tests the single-valued requirement of multivariable functions, a foundational conceptual check in KFUPM calculus quizzes.
Problem Statement
Determine whether $z$ is a function of $x$ and $y$: $$\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} + z^2 = 1$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{No, } z \text{ is not a function of } x \text{ and } y.}$
To determine whether $z$ is a function of $x$ and $y$, we test if each input pair $(x, y)$ corresponds to a single, unique real value of $z$. **Step 1: Solve for $z$ in terms of $x$ and $y$.** Isolate the $z^2$ term: $$z^2 = 1 - \frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{9}$$ Taking the square root of both sides gives: $$z = \pm \sqrt{1 - \frac{x^2}{4} - \frac{y^2}{9}}$$ **Step 2: Demonstrate non-uniqueness with a counterexample.** Choose a point $(x, y)$ in the interior of the ellipse $\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} < 1$, for instance $(x, y) = (0, 0)$: $$z^2 = 1 - 0 - 0 = 1 \implies z = 1 \quad \text{or} \quad z = -1$$ Because a single input pair $(0, 0)$ yields two distinct real outputs $z = 1$ and $z = -1$, the relation fails the definition of a function. **Geometric Interpretation:** The equation describes an ellipsoid centered at the origin. A vertical line parallel to the $z$-axis through $(0, 0)$ intersects the surface at two points $(0, 0, 1)$ and $(0, 0, -1)$, violating the vertical line test.
Ex 8 Easy functions-of-several-variablesdefinition-of-function
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 8: Solving an Implicit Equation for a Dependent Variable

🎯 Tests algebraic solving for the dependent variable $z$, essential for converting implicit level surfaces into explicit graphable functions.
Problem Statement
Determine whether $z$ is a function of $x$ and $y$: $$z + x \ln y - 8yz = 0$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Yes, } z \text{ is a function of } x \text{ and } y; \quad z = \frac{x \ln y}{8y - 1}}$
To determine whether $z$ is a function of $x$ and $y$, we attempt to solve explicitly for $z$. **Step 1: Group and factor terms containing $z$.** $$z - 8yz + x \ln y = 0$$ $$z(1 - 8y) = -x \ln y$$ **Step 2: Isolate $z$.** Dividing both sides by $1 - 8y$ (valid whenever $y \neq \frac{1}{8}$): $$z = \frac{-x \ln y}{1 - 8y} = \frac{x \ln y}{8y - 1}$$ **Step 3: Check uniqueness.** For every ordered pair $(x, y)$ in the natural domain of this expression (which requires $y > 0$ for $\ln y$ and $y \neq \frac{1}{8}$ for the denominator), there is exactly one corresponding value of $z$. Thus, $z$ is uniquely determined as an explicit function of $x$ and $y$.
Ex 15 Easy function-evaluationtrigonometric-functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 15: Evaluating a Multivariable Trigonometric Function

🎯 Standard introductory drill on multivariable substitution and trigonometric evaluation, tested as preliminary steps in tangent plane problems.
Problem Statement
Evaluate the function at the given values of the independent variables. Simplify the results. $$f(x, y) = x \sin y$$ (a) $f\left(2, \frac{\pi}{4}\right)$ (b) $f(3, 1)$ (c) $f(-3, 0)$ (d) $f\left(4, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{(a) } \sqrt{2}, \quad \text{(b) } 3\sin 1, \quad \text{(c) } 0, \quad \text{(d) } 4}$
Substitute each given pair $(x, y)$ directly into $f(x, y) = x \sin y$: **(a)** For $(x, y) = \left(2, \frac{\pi}{4}\right)$: $$f\left(2, \frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 2 \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 2 \left(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right) = \sqrt{2}$$ **(b)** For $(x, y) = (3, 1)$: $$f(3, 1) = 3 \sin(1)$$ Since $1$ radian is not a standard special angle, $3 \sin 1$ is the exact simplified form. **(c)** For $(x, y) = (-3, 0)$: $$f(-3, 0) = -3 \sin(0) = -3(0) = 0$$ **(d)** For $(x, y) = \left(4, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$: $$f\left(4, \frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 4 \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 4(1) = 4$$
Ex 18 Medium function-evaluationdefinite-integrals
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 18: Evaluating an Integral-Defined Multivariable Function

🎯 Connects single-variable integration to multivariable inputs; frequent in KFUPM calculus conceptual questions involving integral functions.
Problem Statement
Evaluate the function at the given values of the independent variables. Simplify the results. $$g(x, y) = \int_{x}^{y} \frac{1}{t} \, dt$$ (a) $g(4, 1)$ (b) $g(6, 3)$ (c) $g(2, 5)$ (d) $g\left(\frac{1}{2}, 7\right)$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{(a) } -\ln 4, \quad \text{(b) } -\ln 2, \quad \text{(c) } \ln\left(\frac{5}{2}\right), \quad \text{(d) } \ln 14}$
First find a closed-form formula for $g(x, y)$ using the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus. For $t > 0$, the antiderivative of $\frac{1}{t}$ is $\ln t$: $$g(x, y) = \int_{x}^{y} \frac{1}{t} \, dt = \Big[ \ln |t| \Big]_{x}^{y} = \ln|y| - \ln|x| = \ln\left|\frac{y}{x}\right|$$ Since all given test coordinates satisfy $x > 0$ and $y > 0$, we have $g(x, y) = \ln\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)$. **(a)** For $(x, y) = (4, 1)$: $$g(4, 1) = \ln\left(\frac{1}{4}\right) = \ln(4^{-1}) = -\ln 4 = -2\ln 2$$ **(b)** For $(x, y) = (6, 3)$: $$g(6, 3) = \ln\left(\frac{3}{6}\right) = \ln\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = -\ln 2$$ **(c)** For $(x, y) = (2, 5)$: $$g(2, 5) = \ln\left(\frac{5}{2}\right) = \ln 5 - \ln 2$$ **(d)** For $(x, y) = \left(\frac{1}{2}, 7\right)$: $$g\left(\frac{1}{2}, 7\right) = \ln\left(\frac{7}{1/2}\right) = \ln(14)$$
Ex 28 Medium domain-and-rangesquare-root
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 28: Determining the Domain and Range of a Radicand with Conic Boundary

🎯 High-yield KFUPM Exam 1 topic: finding algebraic domains bounded by conic sections and verifying range bounds.
Problem Statement
Find the domain and range of the function: $$f(x, y) = \sqrt{9 - 6x^2 + y^2}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Domain: } \left\{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \;\middle|\; \frac{x^2}{3/2} - \frac{y^2}{9} \le 1\right\}, \quad \text{Range: } [0, \infty) \text{ (or } z \ge 0\text{)}}$
**Step 1: Determine the Domain.** The square root function $\sqrt{u}$ is defined for real outputs if and only if the radicand is non-negative: $$9 - 6x^2 + y^2 \ge 0$$ Subtract $9$ and multiply by $-1$ (which reverses the inequality): $$6x^2 - y^2 \le 9$$ Divide through by $9$ to write the inequality in standard hyperbolic form: $$\frac{6x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{9} \le 1 \iff \frac{x^2}{3/2} - \frac{y^2}{9} \le 1$$ This inequality defines the closed region of the $xy$-plane containing all points lying between and on the two branches of the hyperbola $\frac{x^2}{3/2} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1$ (including the entire $y$-axis where $x = 0$, since $-\frac{y^2}{9} \le 0 \le 1$ holds for all $y \in \mathbb{R}$). Thus: $$\text{Domain: } D = \left\{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \;\middle|\; \frac{x^2}{3/2} - \frac{y^2}{9} \le 1\right\}$$ **Step 2: Determine the Range.** 1. By definition of the principal square root, $z = f(x, y) \ge 0$. 2. The minimum value $z = 0$ is attained at every point on the boundary hyperbola $6x^2 - y^2 = 9$, such as $(\sqrt{3/2}, 0)$ or $(-\sqrt{3/2}, 0)$: $$f(\pm\sqrt{3/2}, 0) = \sqrt{9 - 6(3/2) + 0} = \sqrt{0} = 0$$ 3. To see that $z$ is unbounded above, set $x = 0$ along the $y$-axis: $$f(0, y) = \sqrt{9 + y^2}$$ As $|y| \to \infty$, $f(0, y) = \sqrt{9 + y^2} \to \infty$. Since $f$ is continuous on its connected domain, the Intermediate Value Theorem guarantees that $f(x, y)$ takes all values in $[0, \infty)$. Therefore: $$\text{Range: } [0, \infty) \quad (z \ge 0)$$
Ex 29 Easy domain-and-rangeinverse-trigonometric
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 29: Domain and Range of an Inverse Trigonometric Function

🎯 Frequent KFUPM Exam 1 question type involving inverse trigonometric domain restrictions ($\arcsin$ and $\arccos$).
Problem Statement
Find the domain and range of the function: $$f(x, y) = \arccos(x + y)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Domain: } \{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \mid -1 \le x + y \le 1\}, \quad \text{Range: } [0, \pi] \text{ (or } 0 \le z \le \pi\text{)}}$
**Step 1: Determine the Domain.** The inverse cosine function $\arccos(u)$ is defined if and only if its argument lies in the closed interval $[-1, 1]$: $$-1 \le u \le 1$$ Substitute $u = x + y$: $$-1 \le x + y \le 1$$ Geometrically, this represents the closed infinite strip between the two parallel lines: $$y = -x - 1 \quad \text{and} \quad y = -x + 1$$ Thus: $$\text{Domain: } D = \{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \mid -1 \le x + y \le 1\}$$ **Step 2: Determine the Range.** The standard range of the principal inverse cosine function $\arccos(u)$ is $[0, \pi]$. Because $u = x + y$ takes every value in $[-1, 1]$ across the strip domain: - At $x + y = 1$, $f(x, y) = \arccos(1) = 0$. - At $x + y = 0$, $f(x, y) = \arccos(0) = \frac{\pi}{2}$. - At $x + y = -1$, $f(x, y) = \arccos(-1) = \pi$. By continuity of $\arccos$, all values between $0$ and $\pi$ are attained. Therefore: $$\text{Range: } [0, \pi] \quad (0 \le z \le \pi)$$
Ex 32 Medium domain-and-rangelogarithmic-functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 32: Domain and Range of a Multivariable Logarithmic Function

🎯 Very common KFUPM Exam 1 question testing logarithmic domain inequalities and asymptotic range behavior.
Problem Statement
Find the domain and range of the function: $$f(x, y) = \ln(xy - 6)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Domain: } \{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \mid xy > 6\}, \quad \text{Range: } (-\infty, \infty) \text{ (all real numbers } \mathbb{R}\text{)}}$
**Step 1: Determine the Domain.** The natural logarithmic function $\ln(u)$ requires its argument to be strictly positive: $$xy - 6 > 0 \iff xy > 6$$ This condition divides the domain into two disjoint regions bounded by the rectangular hyperbola $xy = 6$ (or $y = \frac{6}{x}$): - In Quadrant I ($x > 0$): $y > \frac{6}{x}$ (the region strictly above the hyperbola branch). - In Quadrant III ($x < 0$): $y < \frac{6}{x}$ (the region strictly below the hyperbola branch). Note that in Quadrants II and IV, $xy < 0$, so $xy - 6 < -6 < 0$, which is completely outside the domain. The boundary hyperbola $xy = 6$ is strictly excluded. Thus: $$\text{Domain: } D = \{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \mid xy > 6\}$$ **Step 2: Determine the Range.** Let $u = xy - 6$. In the domain, $u$ takes on every value in the open interval $(0, \infty)$: - As $(x, y)$ approaches any point on the boundary hyperbola $xy = 6$ from within the domain, $u \to 0^+$, which means $\ln(u) \to -\infty$. - As $x \to \infty$ and $y \to \infty$, $u \to \infty$, which means $\ln(u) \to \infty$. Since the logarithmic function is continuous on $(0, \infty)$, its output spans all of $\mathbb{R}$. Therefore: $$\text{Range: } (-\infty, \infty) \quad (\text{all real numbers})$$
Ex 35 Easy surfaces-in-spaceplanes
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 35: Analytical Description of a Constant Surface in Space

🎯 Tests basic 3D Cartesian coordinates and recognition of constant functions as horizontal planes.
Problem Statement
Describe the surface given by the function: $$f(x, y) = 4$$ Specify its surface type, orientation, intercepts, domain, and range.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Horizontal plane parallel to the } xy\text{-plane with } z\text{-intercept } (0, 0, 4) \text{ and equation } z = 4.}$
**Step 1: Formulate the 3D surface equation.** Setting $z = f(x, y)$ gives: $$z = 4$$ **Step 2: Analytical Classification.** - **Surface Type:** Plane. - **Orientation:** Because the equation specifies that $z$ is constant regardless of $x$ and $y$, the surface is a horizontal plane parallel to the $xy$-coordinate plane at a fixed distance of $4$ units above it. - **Intercepts:** - $z$-intercept: Setting $x = 0$ and $y = 0$ yields $(0, 0, 4)$. - $x$-intercept and $y$-intercept: None, since $z = 4 \neq 0$ everywhere. - **Traces:** - Traces parallel to the $xz$-plane ($y = y_0$): Horizontal lines $z = 4$. - Traces parallel to the $yz$-plane ($x = x_0$): Horizontal lines $z = 4$. - Horizontal trace ($z = c$): Empty if $c \neq 4$; the entire plane if $c = 4$. - **Domain:** Entire $xy$-plane ($\mathbb{R}^2$). - **Range:** The single value $\{4\}$.
Ex 38 Easy surfaces-in-spaceplanes
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 38: Analytical Description of a Linear Surface in Space

🎯 Tests recognition of linear equations in 3D missing one coordinate variable, forming planes containing coordinate axes.
Problem Statement
Describe the surface given by the function: $$g(x, y) = \frac{1}{2}y$$ Specify its surface type, traces, axis intersection, domain, and range.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Plane containing the } x\text{-axis with generating trace } z = \frac{1}{2}y \text{ in the } yz\text{-plane (equation: } y - 2z = 0\text{).}}$
**Step 1: Formulate the surface equation.** Setting $z = g(x, y)$: $$z = \frac{1}{2}y \iff y - 2z = 0$$ **Step 2: Analytical Classification.** - **Surface Type:** Plane. - **Missing Variable Analysis:** The variable $x$ does not appear in the equation. In three dimensions, an equation in two variables represents a **cylinder** whose rulings are parallel to the axis of the missing variable. Here, the generating curve in the $yz$-plane is the straight line $z = \frac{1}{2}y$, so the cylinder is a **flat plane** generated by translating this line parallel to the $x$-axis. - **Line of Intersection:** When $y = 0$ and $z = 0$, $x$ can take any real value. Therefore, the plane passes directly through the entire $x$-axis. - **Traces:** - In the $yz$-plane ($x = 0$): The line $z = \frac{1}{2}y$. - Parallel to the $yz$-plane ($x = x_0$): The identical line $z = \frac{1}{2}y$. - In the $xy$-plane ($z = 0$): The line $y = 0$ (the $x$-axis). - In horizontal planes ($z = c$): The line $y = 2c$, parallel to the $x$-axis. - **Domain:** Entire $xy$-plane ($\mathbb{R}^2$). - **Range:** All real numbers $(-\infty, \infty)$.
Ex 40 Medium quadric-surfacescircular-cone
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 40: Analytical Classification of an Upper Half-Cone Surface

🎯 Distinguishing cones $z = k\sqrt{x^2+y^2}$ from paraboloids $z = k(x^2+y^2)$ is a classic KFUPM Exam 1 problem.
Problem Statement
Describe the surface given by the function: $$z = \frac{1}{2}\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$$ Specify its surface type, vertex, axis of symmetry, domain, range, and cross-sectional traces.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Upper nappe of a circular cone with vertex at } (0, 0, 0) \text{ and axis along the positive } z\text{-axis. Domain: } \mathbb{R}^2, \text{ Range: } [0, \infty).}$
**Step 1: Analyze the algebraic form.** Given: $$z = \frac{1}{2}\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$$ Because the principal square root is non-negative, $z \ge 0$. Squaring both sides: $$z^2 = \frac{1}{4}(x^2 + y^2) \iff 4z^2 = x^2 + y^2 \quad (z \ge 0)$$ This matches the standard equation of a circular cone $\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = \frac{z^2}{c^2}$ with $a = b = 2$ and $c = 1$. **Step 2: Surface Characteristics.** - **Surface Type:** Upper nappe of a right circular cone. - **Vertex:** The origin $(0, 0, 0)$ (the lowest point since $z = 0$ occurs only when $x = 0$ and $y = 0$). - **Axis of Symmetry:** The positive $z$-axis. - **Domain:** Entire $xy$-plane ($\mathbb{R}^2$), since $x^2 + y^2 \ge 0$ for all $(x, y)$. - **Range:** $[0, \infty)$ ($z \ge 0$). **Step 3: Cross-Sectional Traces.** - **Horizontal Traces ($z = c, c > 0$):** $$x^2 + y^2 = 4c^2$$ These are concentric circles centered at $(0, 0, c)$ with radius $r = 2c$. The radius expands linearly with height $c$. - **Vertical Traces parallel to coordinate planes:** - In the $xz$-plane ($y = 0$): $z = \frac{1}{2}|x|$ (a pair of rays from the origin with slope $\pm 1/2$). - In the $yz$-plane ($x = 0$): $z = \frac{1}{2}|y|$ (a pair of rays from the origin with slope $\pm 1/2$).
Ex 47 Medium level-curvescontour-maps
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 47: Matching a Surface to its Contour Map via Level Curves

🎯 Identifying contour maps by isolating $x^2+y^2 = g(c)$ is heavily tested in KFUPM Exam 1 matching questions.
Problem Statement
Analyze the level curves of the function to identify its contour map: $$f(x, y) = e^{1 - x^2 - y^2}$$ Describe the algebraic form of the level curves, their geometric shapes, and determine which contour map it matches: (a) Ellipses (b) Parabolas (c) Concentric circles centered at $(0, 0)$ (d) Hyperbolas
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Matches (c): Concentric circles centered at } (0, 0) \text{ with equation } x^2 + y^2 = 1 - \ln c \text{ for } 0 < c \le e.}$
**Step 1: Set up the level curve equation.** Let $f(x, y) = c$, where $c$ is a constant in the range of $f$. $$e^{1 - x^2 - y^2} = c$$ Since $e^u > 0$ for all real $u$, level curves exist only for $c > 0$. **Step 2: Solve algebraically for the curve in the $xy$-plane.** Take the natural logarithm of both sides: $$1 - x^2 - y^2 = \ln c$$ Rearrange to isolate the quadratic terms: $$x^2 + y^2 = 1 - \ln c$$ **Step 3: Analyze the geometry for different values of $c$.** - For a real curve to exist, the right-hand side must be non-negative: $$1 - \ln c \ge 0 \iff \ln c \le 1 \iff 0 < c \le e$$ - If $0 < c < e$, then $1 - \ln c > 0$, and the equation: $$x^2 + y^2 = 1 - \ln c$$ represents a **circle centered at $(0, 0)$** with radius $r = \sqrt{1 - \ln c}$. - If $c = e$, $x^2 + y^2 = 0$, which corresponds to the single point $(0, 0)$ (the global maximum of the bell-shaped surface, where $z = e$). - If $c > e$, there are no real points on the level curve. **Conclusion:** The level curves are a family of concentric circles centered at the origin, which corresponds to contour map **(c)**.
Ex 54 Medium level-curvescontour-maps
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 54: Finding and Describing Level Curves of a Hemisphere

🎯 Directly reflects standard KFUPM exam questions asking for the radius or equation of a specific level curve of a hemisphere.
Problem Statement
Describe the level curves of the function, and determine the exact equations and radii of the level curves corresponding to $c = 0, 1, 2, 3$: $$f(x, y) = \sqrt{9 - x^2 - y^2}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\begin{aligned} \text{Concentric circles } &x^2 + y^2 = 9 - c^2 \text{ centered at } (0, 0) \text{ with radius } r = \sqrt{9 - c^2}: \\ c = 0: &\quad x^2 + y^2 = 9 \quad (r = 3) \\ c = 1: &\quad x^2 + y^2 = 8 \quad (r = 2\sqrt{2}) \\ c = 2: &\quad x^2 + y^2 = 5 \quad (r = \sqrt{5}) \\ c = 3: &\quad x^2 + y^2 = 0 \quad (\text{the origin } (0, 0)) \end{aligned}}$
**Step 1: Set up the general equation for the level curves.** Set $f(x, y) = c$: $$\sqrt{9 - x^2 - y^2} = c$$ Since the square root is non-negative and $9 - x^2 - y^2 \le 9$, valid values of $c$ lie in the interval $0 \le c \le 3$. Squaring both sides: $$9 - x^2 - y^2 = c^2 \iff x^2 + y^2 = 9 - c^2$$ This is the equation of a circle centered at the origin $(0, 0)$ with radius $r = \sqrt{9 - c^2}$. **Step 2: Evaluate for each given value of $c$.** - **For $c = 0$:** $$x^2 + y^2 = 9 - 0^2 = 9 = 3^2$$ This is a circle centered at $(0, 0)$ with radius $r = 3$, representing the outer boundary of the hemisphere in the $xy$-plane. - **For $c = 1$:** $$x^2 + y^2 = 9 - 1^2 = 8$$ This is a circle centered at $(0, 0)$ with radius $r = \sqrt{8} = 2\sqrt{2} \approx 2.828$. - **For $c = 2$:** $$x^2 + y^2 = 9 - 2^2 = 5$$ This is a circle centered at $(0, 0)$ with radius $r = \sqrt{5} \approx 2.236$. - **For $c = 3$:** $$x^2 + y^2 = 9 - 3^2 = 0$$ This reduces to the single point $(0, 0)$, representing the peak of the hemisphere. **Geometric Summary:** The level curves form a system of concentric circles whose radii decrease from $3$ to $0$ as the elevation $c$ increases from $0$ to $3$.
Ex 58 Medium level-curvescontour-maps
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 58: Describing Level Curves of a Logarithmic Linear Difference

🎯 Tests finding level curves that result in families of parallel lines, a standard KFUPM conceptual multiple-choice question.
Problem Statement
Describe the level curves of the function, and determine the general equation of the level curves for arbitrary constant $c$: $$f(x, y) = \ln(x - y)$$ Specifically identify the geometry for $c = 0, \pm\frac{1}{2}, \pm 1, \pm\frac{3}{2}, \pm 2$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Parallel straight lines of slope } 1 \text{ with equations } y = x - e^c \text{ lying strictly in the half-plane } y < x.}$
**Step 1: Set up the level curve equation.** Set $f(x, y) = c$: $$\ln(x - y) = c$$ **Step 2: Solve for $y$ in terms of $x$.** Exponentiate both sides with base $e$: $$x - y = e^c \implies y = x - e^c$$ **Step 3: Geometric characterization.** - **Shape:** Each level curve is a straight line in slope-intercept form $y = mx + b$ with slope $m = 1$ and $y$-intercept $b = -e^c$. - **Domain Constraint:** Since the natural logarithm requires $x - y > 0$, all points must satisfy $y < x$. Since $e^c > 0$ for all real $c$, every line $y = x - e^c$ lies strictly below the boundary line $y = x$. - **Orientation:** Because $b = -e^c < 0$ and the $x$-intercept is $(e^c, 0) > 0$, all level curves traverse the fourth quadrant. - **Specific values of $c$:** - $c = -2: \quad y = x - e^{-2} \approx x - 0.135$ - $c = -3/2: \quad y = x - e^{-1.5} \approx x - 0.223$ - $c = -1: \quad y = x - e^{-1} \approx x - 0.368$ - $c = -1/2: \quad y = x - e^{-0.5} \approx x - 0.607$ - $c = 0: \quad y = x - e^0 = x - 1$ - $c = 1/2: \quad y = x - e^{0.5} \approx x - 1.649$ - $c = 1: \quad y = x - e^1 = x - e \approx x - 2.718$ - $c = 3/2: \quad y = x - e^{1.5} \approx x - 4.482$ - $c = 2: \quad y = x - e^2 \approx x - 7.389$ As $c$ increases, the lines remain parallel with slope $1$ while shifting progressively downward and to the right.
Ex 75 Medium level-surfacesfunctions-of-three-variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 75: Analytical Classification of a Level Surface as an Elliptic Cone

🎯 Core KFUPM topic: identifying level surfaces of functions $f(x, y, z) = c$ by classifying standard quadric surfaces.
Problem Statement
Describe the level surface $f(x, y, z) = c$ for: $$f(x, y, z) = 4x^2 + 4y^2 - z^2, \quad c = 0$$ Identify the surface type, vertex, axis of symmetry, and traces.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Right circular cone with vertex at } (0, 0, 0) \text{ and axis along the } z\text{-axis, with equation } z^2 = 4(x^2 + y^2).}$
**Step 1: Set up the level surface equation.** For a function of three variables $w = f(x, y, z)$, setting $w = c$ defines a surface in three-dimensional space $\mathbb{R}^3$. Here $c = 0$: $$4x^2 + 4y^2 - z^2 = 0$$ **Step 2: Rewrite in canonical quadric surface form.** $$z^2 = 4x^2 + 4y^2 \iff \frac{x^2}{1} + \frac{y^2}{1} - \frac{z^2}{4} = 0$$ Taking square roots gives: $$z = \pm 2\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$$ **Step 3: Surface Classification and Properties.** - **Surface Type:** Right circular cone (an elliptic cone with circular horizontal cross-sections). - **Vertex:** The origin $(0, 0, 0)$, where the two nappes meet. - **Axis of Symmetry:** The $z$-axis. - **Traces:** - In horizontal planes $z = k \neq 0$: $$4x^2 + 4y^2 = k^2 \iff x^2 + y^2 = \frac{k^2}{4}$$ These are circles centered at $(0, 0, k)$ with radius $r = \frac{|k|}{2}$. - At $z = 0$: $x^2 + y^2 = 0$, giving the single point $(0, 0, 0)$. - In the vertical planes: - $y = 0$ ($xz$-plane): $z^2 = 4x^2 \implies z = \pm 2x$ (two intersecting lines). - $x = 0$ ($yz$-plane): $z^2 = 4y^2 \implies z = \pm 2y$ (two intersecting lines).
Ex 76 Medium level-surfacescylinders
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 76: Analytical Classification of a Level Surface as a Cylinder

🎯 Frequently tested KFUPM concept: recognizing that an equation in $\mathbb{R}^3$ lacking one variable forms a cylinder parallel to that axis.
Problem Statement
Describe the level surface $f(x, y, z) = c$ for: $$f(x, y, z) = \sin x - z, \quad c = 0$$ Identify the surface type, generating curve, rulings, and orientation.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Sinusoidal cylinder with generating curve } z = \sin x \text{ in the } xz\text{-plane and rulings parallel to the } y\text{-axis.}}$
**Step 1: Set up the level surface equation.** Set $f(x, y, z) = 0$: $$\sin x - z = 0 \implies z = \sin x$$ **Step 2: Analytical Classification in $\mathbb{R}^3$.** - **Surface Type:** Cylinder. - **Generating Curve:** In the $xz$-plane ($y = 0$), the equation $z = \sin x$ is a standard sine wave oscillating between $z = -1$ and $z = 1$ with period $2\pi$. - **Rulings:** The variable $y$ is completely absent from the equation. In three-dimensional space, any equation in two variables defines a cylinder generated by translating the 2D curve along straight parallel lines (rulings) parallel to the axis of the missing variable. Therefore, the rulings are all parallel to the $y$-axis. - **Traces:** - For any fixed $y = y_0$, the cross-section parallel to the $xz$-plane is identical to $z = \sin x$. - For any fixed $z = z_0 \in [-1, 1]$, the trace is a collection of parallel lines $x = \arcsin(z_0) + 2\pi k$ and $x = \pi - \arcsin(z_0) + 2\pi k$, all parallel to the $y$-axis.

13.2 Limits & Continuity

Ex 10 Easy LimitsLimit Laws
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 10: Evaluating a Limit Using Algebraic Limit Laws

🎯 Tests standard multivariable limit laws; frequently appears in conceptual questions and multiple-choice items on KFUPM Exam 1.
Problem Statement
Given that $\lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} f(x, y) = 4$ and $\lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} g(x, y) = -5$, evaluate the limit: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} \left[\frac{f(x, y) + g(x, y)}{f(x, y)}\right]$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$-\frac{1}{4}$$
To evaluate the limit of the rational combination of $f$ and $g$, we apply the basic limit laws for functions of two variables (sum rule and quotient rule): 1. **Quotient Rule for Limits:** Provided the limit of the denominator is non-zero: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} \left[\frac{f(x, y) + g(x, y)}{f(x, y)}\right] = \frac{\lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} [f(x, y) + g(x, y)]}{\lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} f(x, y)}$$ 2. **Sum Rule for Limits:** $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} [f(x, y) + g(x, y)] = \lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} f(x, y) + \lim_{(x, y) \to (a, b)} g(x, y)$$ 3. **Substitute the given limit values:** $$\frac{4 + (-5)}{4} = \frac{-1}{4} = -\frac{1}{4}$$ Since the denominator limit is $4 \neq 0$, the quotient law is valid and the limit is $-\frac{1}{4}$.
Ex 15 Easy LimitsContinuity
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 15: Limit and Continuity of a Rational Function

🎯 Straightforward direct substitution and domain determination; standard warm-up type on KFUPM Exam 1.
Problem Statement
Find the limit and discuss the continuity of the function: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 2)} \frac{x}{y}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 2)} \frac{x}{y} = 0; \quad \text{Continuous for all } y \neq 0$$
1. **Limit Evaluation by Direct Substitution:** The function $f(x, y) = \frac{x}{y}$ is a rational function. Rational functions are continuous at every point in their domain where the denominator is non-zero. Since the point $(0, 2)$ has $y = 2 \neq 0$, we substitute directly: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 2)} \frac{x}{y} = \frac{0}{2} = 0$$ 2. **Continuity Analysis:** The denominator is zero whenever $y = 0$. Hence, $f(x, y)$ is defined and continuous on the open set $\{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 : y \neq 0\}$, which consists of all points in the $xy$-plane except those lying on the $x$-axis ($y = 0$).
Ex 16 Easy LimitsContinuity
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 16: Limit and Continuity of a Linear Rational Function

🎯 Tests limit evaluation by direct substitution and identifying the line of discontinuity in $\mathbb{R}^2$.
Problem Statement
Find the limit and discuss the continuity of the function: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (-1, 2)} \frac{x + y}{x - y}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$-\frac{1}{3}; \quad \text{Continuous for all } x \neq y$$
1. **Limit Evaluation by Direct Substitution:** The rational function $f(x, y) = \frac{x + y}{x - y}$ is continuous at all points where the denominator does not vanish. Evaluating the denominator at the target point $(-1, 2)$: $$x - y = -1 - 2 = -3 \neq 0$$ Because the denominator is non-zero, we evaluate the limit directly by substitution: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (-1, 2)} \frac{x + y}{x - y} = \frac{-1 + 2}{-1 - 2} = \frac{1}{-3} = -\frac{1}{3}$$ 2. **Continuity Analysis:** The denominator vanishes when $x - y = 0 \iff y = x$. Therefore, $f(x, y)$ is continuous at all points in $\mathbb{R}^2$ except along the line $y = x$.
Ex 22 Medium LimitsContinuity
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 22: Limit and Continuity of an Inverse Trigonometric Function

🎯 Combines inverse trigonometric definitions with multivariable domain and continuity constraints; high exam relevance for KFUPM.
Problem Statement
Find the limit and discuss the continuity of the function: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 1)} \frac{\arccos(x/y)}{1 + xy}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\frac{\pi}{2}; \quad \text{Continuous on the interior of its domain: } xy \neq -1,\; y \neq 0,\; -1 < \frac{x}{y} < 1$$
1. **Limit Evaluation by Direct Substitution:** Evaluate the components at the point $(0, 1)$: - Argument of $\arccos$: $\frac{x}{y} = \frac{0}{1} = 0$. - Numerator: $\arccos(0) = \frac{\pi}{2}$. - Denominator: $1 + xy = 1 + (0)(1) = 1 \neq 0$. Since the composite expression is continuous at $(0, 1)$: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 1)} \frac{\arccos(x/y)}{1 + xy} = \frac{\arccos(0/1)}{1 + (0)(1)} = \frac{\pi / 2}{1} = \frac{\pi}{2}$$ 2. **Continuity and Domain Analysis:** The function $f(x, y) = \frac{\arccos(x/y)}{1 + xy}$ requires: - The denominator must not be zero: $1 + xy \neq 0 \implies xy \neq -1$. - The argument of $\arccos(u)$ requires $u \in [-1, 1]$ and $y \neq 0$, so $-1 \le \frac{x}{y} \le 1$. Thus, the function is continuous on the interior of its domain, given by: $$\left\{(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2 : -1 < \frac{x}{y} < 1,\; xy \neq -1,\; y \neq 0\right\}$$
Ex 28 Easy LimitsInfinite Limits
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 28: Infinite Behavior and Non-Existence of a Multivariable Limit

🎯 Distinguishing between finite limits, infinite limits, and non-existence in multivariable calculus.
Problem Statement
Find the limit (if it exists). If the limit does not exist, explain why: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{1}{x^2 y^2}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{The limit does not exist (DNE) (approaches } \infty\text{)}$$
1. **Analyze the expression as $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$:** For any point $(x, y)$ where $x \neq 0$ and $y \neq 0$, the denominator is: $$x^2 y^2 = (xy)^2 > 0$$ As $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$, the product $xy \to 0$, which implies $x^2 y^2 \to 0^+$. 2. **Evaluate the limit:** $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{1}{x^2 y^2} = +\infty$$ Because the values of the function grow without bound, the limit does not exist as a finite real number (DNE). Additionally, along the coordinate axes ($x = 0$ or $y = 0$), the function is entirely undefined.
Ex 30 Medium LimitsIndeterminate Forms
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 30: Evaluating Indeterminate Form 0/0 via Rationalization

🎯 Resolving $0/0$ indeterminate forms using conjugates; standard algebraic question on KFUPM Exam 1.
Problem Statement
Find the limit (if it exists). If the limit does not exist, explain why: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (2, 1)} \frac{x - y - 1}{\sqrt{x - y} - 1}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$2$$
1. **Test for Indeterminate Form:** Substitute $(x, y) = (2, 1)$ directly: - Numerator: $2 - 1 - 1 = 0$ - Denominator: $\sqrt{2 - 1} - 1 = 1 - 1 = 0$ This yields the indeterminate form $\frac{0}{0}$. 2. **Algebraic Simplification (Conjugate Multiplication):** Multiply the numerator and denominator by the conjugate of the denominator, $(\sqrt{x - y} + 1)$: $$\frac{x - y - 1}{\sqrt{x - y} - 1} \cdot \frac{\sqrt{x - y} + 1}{\sqrt{x - y} + 1} = \frac{(x - y - 1)(\sqrt{x - y} + 1)}{(\sqrt{x - y})^2 - 1^2} = \frac{(x - y - 1)(\sqrt{x - y} + 1)}{(x - y - 1)}$$ 3. **Cancel Common Factors and Evaluate:** For $(x, y) \neq (2, 1)$ such that $x - y \neq 1$, we cancel the factor $(x - y - 1)$: $$= \sqrt{x - y} + 1$$ Now evaluate the limit by direct substitution: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (2, 1)} (\sqrt{x - y} + 1) = \sqrt{2 - 1} + 1 = 1 + 1 = 2$$
Ex 31 Medium LimitsTwo-Path Test
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 31: Two-Path Test for Non-Existence along Lines

🎯 Highly tested KFUPM exam skill: selecting appropriate linear and axial paths to prove non-existence.
Problem Statement
Find the limit (if it exists). If the limit does not exist, explain why: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{x + y}{x^2 + y}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{The limit does not exist (DNE)}$$
To determine whether the limit exists, we test paths approaching $(0, 0)$: 1. **Path 1: Along the line $y = 0$ ($x$-axis, with $x \to 0$):** $$\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x + 0}{x^2 + 0} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x}{x^2} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1}{x}$$ As $x \to 0^+$, $\frac{1}{x} \to +\infty$; as $x \to 0^-$, $\frac{1}{x} \to -\infty$. Hence, along this path, the limit does not exist. 2. **Path 2: Along the line $x = 0$ ($y$-axis, with $y \to 0$):** $$\lim_{y \to 0} \frac{0 + y}{0^2 + y} = \lim_{y \to 0} \frac{y}{y} = 1$$ 3. **Path 3: Along lines $y = mx$ ($m \neq 0$):** $$\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x + mx}{x^2 + mx} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x(1 + m)}{x(x + m)} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1 + m}{x + m} = \frac{1 + m}{m} = 1 + \frac{1}{m}$$ For $m = 1$ (the line $y = x$), the limit is $2$. For $m = 2$ (the line $y = 2x$), the limit is $\frac{3}{2}$. 4. **Conclusion:** Since different paths yield different limits (e.g., $1 \neq 2$), and along the $x$-axis the function is unbounded, by the Two-Path Test, the limit **does not exist (DNE)**.
Ex 35 Medium LimitsThree Variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 35: Non-Existence of a Three-Variable Multivariable Limit

🎯 Standard 3D multivariable limit problem; classic KFUPM Exam 1 conceptual problem.
Problem Statement
Find the limit (if it exists). If the limit does not exist, explain why: $$\lim_{(x, y, z) \to (0, 0, 0)} \frac{xy + yz + xz}{x^2 + y^2 + z^2}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{The limit does not exist (DNE)}$$
To evaluate this limit in three variables, we test different paths approaching $(0, 0, 0)$: 1. **Path 1: Along the $z$-axis ($x = 0, y = 0, z \to 0$):** $$\lim_{z \to 0} \frac{(0)(0) + (0)z + (0)z}{0^2 + 0^2 + z^2} = \lim_{z \to 0} \frac{0}{z^2} = 0$$ 2. **Path 2: Along the line $x = y = z = t$ as $t \to 0$:** $$\lim_{t \to 0} \frac{t \cdot t + t \cdot t + t \cdot t}{t^2 + t^2 + t^2} = \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{3t^2}{3t^2} = 1$$ 3. **Conclusion:** Along Path 1, the limit is $L_1 = 0$, while along Path 2, the limit is $L_2 = 1$. Because $L_1 \neq L_2$, by the Two-Path Test, the limit **does not exist (DNE)**.
Ex 42 Medium LimitsContinuity
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 42: Continuity and Limit Behavior of a Radially Symmetric Function

🎯 Tests radial substitution $r^2 = x^2 + y^2$ and identification of infinite discontinuities.
Problem Statement
Discuss the continuity of the function and evaluate the limit (if it exists) as $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$: $$f(x, y) = 1 - \frac{\cos(x^2 + y^2)}{x^2 + y^2}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{The limit does not exist (approaches } -\infty\text{); Continuous everywhere except at } (0, 0)$$
1. **Continuity Analysis:** The term $\cos(x^2 + y^2)$ is continuous everywhere on $\mathbb{R}^2$ as a composite of continuous functions. The denominator $x^2 + y^2$ is a polynomial that equals zero if and only if $(x, y) = (0, 0)$. Therefore, $f(x, y)$ is continuous at every point in the $xy$-plane except at the origin $(0, 0)$. 2. **Limit Evaluation as $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$:** Let $r^2 = x^2 + y^2$. As $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$, $r^2 \to 0^+$: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \left[1 - \frac{\cos(x^2 + y^2)}{x^2 + y^2}\right] = \lim_{r^2 \to 0^+} \left[1 - \frac{\cos(r^2)}{r^2}\right]$$ As $r^2 \to 0^+$, $\cos(r^2) \to 1$, so the fraction behaves as: $$\frac{\cos(r^2)}{r^2} \to \frac{1}{0^+} = +\infty$$ Thus: $$\lim_{r^2 \to 0^+} \left[1 - \frac{\cos(r^2)}{r^2}\right] = 1 - (+\infty) = -\infty$$ Because the function decreases without bound, the finite limit **does not exist (DNE)**.
Ex 44 Hard LimitsTwo-Path Test
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 44: Non-Existence of Limit via Degree-Balanced Parabolic Paths

🎯 Crucial KFUPM Exam 1 question type: straight line paths $y = mx$ yield $0$, requiring the parabolic path $x = ky^2$ to prove DNE.
Problem Statement
Discuss the continuity of the function and determine analytically whether the limit exists as $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$: $$f(x, y) = -\frac{x y^2}{x^2 + y^4}$$ Evaluate the limit along the parabolic paths $x = y^2$ and $x = -y^2$.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{The limit does not exist (DNE); Continuous everywhere except at } (0, 0)$$
1. **Continuity:** The function $f(x, y) = -\frac{x y^2}{x^2 + y^4}$ is a rational function with denominator $x^2 + y^4$. The denominator is zero only when $x = 0$ and $y = 0$. Hence, $f(x, y)$ is continuous at all points in $\mathbb{R}^2$ except at the origin $(0, 0)$. 2. **Degree Balancing and Path Analysis:** Notice the degree of $x^2$ is 2 and the degree of $y^4$ is 4. Setting $x = k y^2$ balances the terms in the denominator ($x^2 = k^2 y^4$). - **Path 1: Along the parabola $x = y^2$ as $y \to 0$:** $$f(y^2, y) = -\frac{(y^2) y^2}{(y^2)^2 + y^4} = -\frac{y^4}{y^4 + y^4} = -\frac{y^4}{2y^4} = -\frac{1}{2}$$ - **Path 2: Along the parabola $x = -y^2$ as $y \to 0$:** $$f(-y^2, y) = -\frac{(-y^2) y^2}{(-y^2)^2 + y^4} = -\frac{-y^4}{y^4 + y^4} = \frac{y^4}{2y^4} = \frac{1}{2}$$ 3. **Conclusion:** Along $x = y^2$, the limit is $-\frac{1}{2}$, whereas along $x = -y^2$, the limit is $\frac{1}{2}$. Since $-\frac{1}{2} \neq \frac{1}{2}$, by the Two-Path Test, the limit **does not exist (DNE)**.
Ex 47 Medium LimitsTwo-Path Test
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 47: Path-Dependent Limit Analysis along Lines and Parabolas

🎯 Comprehensive testing of path dependence with lines and parabolas; classic KFUPM major exam format.
Problem Statement
Consider the limit: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{x^2 + y^2}{xy}$$ (a) Determine (if possible) the limit along any line of the form $y = ax$. (b) Determine (if possible) the limit along the parabola $y = x^2$. (c) Does the limit exist? Explain.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{(a) } \frac{1 + a^2}{a} \text{ for } a \neq 0; \quad \text{(b) Does not exist (approaches } \pm\infty\text{)}; \quad \text{(c) No, the limit does not exist (DNE)}$$
**(a) Limit along lines $y = ax$:** Substitute $y = ax$ into the expression as $x \to 0$: $$\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x^2 + (ax)^2}{x(ax)} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x^2(1 + a^2)}{a x^2} = \frac{1 + a^2}{a} \quad (a \neq 0)$$ If $a = 0$ (the line $y = 0$), the function is undefined for all $x$ because the denominator $xy = 0$, so the limit along $y = 0$ does not exist. **(b) Limit along the parabola $y = x^2$:** Substitute $y = x^2$ into the expression as $x \to 0$: $$\lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x^2 + (x^2)^2}{x(x^2)} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x^2 + x^4}{x^3} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x^2(1 + x^2)}{x^3} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{1 + x^2}{x}$$ As $x \to 0^+$, $\frac{1 + x^2}{x} \to +\infty$, and as $x \to 0^-$, $\frac{1 + x^2}{x} \to -\infty$. Thus, the limit along this parabola does not exist. **(c) Existence of the overall limit:** No, the limit does not exist. The value of the limit along linear paths depends on the slope $a$ (for example, $a = 1 \implies 2$, while $a = 2 \implies \frac{5}{2}$), and the limit along $y = x^2$ is infinite. By the Two-Path Test, the limit **does not exist (DNE)**.
Ex 49 Medium ContinuityPiecewise Functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 49: Comparing Removable and Continuous Piecewise Extensions

🎯 Classifying points of continuity and removable discontinuities in piecewise multivariable functions; high KFUPM exam relevance.
Problem Statement
Discuss and compare the continuity of the functions $f$ and $g$: $$f(x, y) = \begin{cases} \frac{x^4 - y^4}{x^2 + y^2}, & (x, y) \neq (0, 0) \\[6pt] 0, & (x, y) = (0, 0) \end{cases}$$ $$g(x, y) = \begin{cases} \frac{x^4 - y^4}{x^2 + y^2}, & (x, y) \neq (0, 0) \\[6pt] 1, & (x, y) = (0, 0) \end{cases}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$f \text{ is continuous everywhere on } \mathbb{R}^2; \quad g \text{ has a removable discontinuity at } (0, 0) \text{ and is continuous elsewhere}$$
1. **Evaluate the limit of the common expression as $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$:** For $(x, y) \neq (0, 0)$, factor the numerator as a difference of squares: $$\frac{x^4 - y^4}{x^2 + y^2} = \frac{(x^2 - y^2)(x^2 + y^2)}{x^2 + y^2} = x^2 - y^2$$ Taking the limit as $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{x^4 - y^4}{x^2 + y^2} = \lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} (x^2 - y^2) = 0^2 - 0^2 = 0$$ 2. **Continuity of $f(x, y)$:** - For $(x, y) \neq (0, 0)$, $f$ is a rational function with non-zero denominator, so it is continuous. - At $(0, 0)$, $\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} f(x, y) = 0 = f(0, 0)$. Therefore, $f$ is continuous at $(0, 0)$, and hence **continuous everywhere on $\mathbb{R}^2$**. 3. **Continuity of $g(x, y)$:** - For $(x, y) \neq (0, 0)$, $g$ is continuous. - At $(0, 0)$, $\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} g(x, y) = 0$, but $g(0, 0) = 1$. Since $\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} g(x, y) \neq g(0, 0)$, $g$ is discontinuous at $(0, 0)$. Because the limit exists, this is a **removable discontinuity**.
Ex 51 Medium LimitsPolar Coordinates
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 51: Evaluating a Multivariable Limit Using Polar Coordinates

🎯 Polar coordinates conversion to prove limit existence is a core, guaranteed topic on KFUPM Math 201 Major 1.
Problem Statement
Use polar coordinates to find the limit: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{x y^2}{x^2 + y^2}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$0$$
1. **Convert to Polar Coordinates:** Let $x = r\cos\theta$ and $y = r\sin\theta$, so that $x^2 + y^2 = r^2$. Note that $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$ corresponds to $r \to 0^+$: $$\frac{x y^2}{x^2 + y^2} = \frac{(r\cos\theta)(r\sin\theta)^2}{r^2} = \frac{r^3 \cos\theta \sin^2\theta}{r^2} = r \cos\theta \sin^2\theta$$ 2. **Apply the Squeeze Theorem:** Since $|\cos\theta| \le 1$ and $|\sin\theta| \le 1$, the trigonometric factor is bounded: $$|\cos\theta \sin^2\theta| \le 1$$ Therefore: $$0 \le |r \cos\theta \sin^2\theta| \le r$$ As $r \to 0^+$, $r \to 0$. By the Squeeze Theorem: $$\lim_{r \to 0^+} r \cos\theta \sin^2\theta = 0$$ Hence: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{x y^2}{x^2 + y^2} = 0$$
Ex 53 Medium LimitsPolar Coordinates
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 53: Polar Coordinate Evaluation of a Quartic-over-Quadratic Limit

🎯 Standard polar coordinates limit problem; very common on KFUPM exams.
Problem Statement
Use polar coordinates to find the limit: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{x^2 y^2}{x^2 + y^2}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$0$$
1. **Convert to Polar Coordinates:** Substitute $x = r\cos\theta$ and $y = r\sin\theta$, with $x^2 + y^2 = r^2$. As $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$, $r \to 0^+$: $$\frac{x^2 y^2}{x^2 + y^2} = \frac{(r\cos\theta)^2 (r\sin\theta)^2}{r^2} = \frac{r^4 \cos^2\theta \sin^2\theta}{r^2} = r^2 \cos^2\theta \sin^2\theta$$ 2. **Apply the Squeeze Theorem:** Because $0 \le \cos^2\theta \sin^2\theta \le 1$, we have the inequality: $$0 \le r^2 \cos^2\theta \sin^2\theta \le r^2$$ Taking the limit as $r \to 0^+$: $$\lim_{r \to 0^+} 0 = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{r \to 0^+} r^2 = 0$$ By the Squeeze Theorem: $$\lim_{r \to 0^+} r^2 \cos^2\theta \sin^2\theta = 0$$ Thus: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{x^2 y^2}{x^2 + y^2} = 0$$
Ex 58 Medium LimitsPolar Coordinates
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 58: Polar Limit Evaluation via L'Hôpital's Rule

🎯 Combines polar coordinates with single-variable calculus limits (L'Hôpital / fundamental limit); high KFUPM frequency.
Problem Statement
Use polar coordinates and L'Hôpital's Rule to find the limit: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{\sin(x^2 + y^2)}{x^2 + y^2}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$1$$
1. **Convert to Polar Coordinates:** Let $x = r\cos\theta$ and $y = r\sin\theta$, so that $x^2 + y^2 = r^2$. As $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$, $r \to 0^+$: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} \frac{\sin(x^2 + y^2)}{x^2 + y^2} = \lim_{r \to 0^+} \frac{\sin(r^2)}{r^2}$$ 2. **Apply L'Hôpital's Rule:** As $r \to 0^+$, $\sin(r^2) \to 0$ and $r^2 \to 0$, giving an indeterminate form of type $\frac{0}{0}$. Differentiating the numerator and denominator with respect to $r$: $$\lim_{r \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{d}{dr}[\sin(r^2)]}{\frac{d}{dr}[r^2]} = \lim_{r \to 0^+} \frac{2r \cos(r^2)}{2r} = \lim_{r \to 0^+} \cos(r^2)$$ 3. **Evaluate the Limit:** $$\lim_{r \to 0^+} \cos(r^2) = \cos(0) = 1$$ *(Note: Alternatively, setting $u = x^2 + y^2 \to 0^+$, this is the fundamental trigonometric limit $\lim_{u \to 0^+} \frac{\sin u}{u} = 1$.)*
Ex 60 Hard LimitsPolar Coordinates
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 60: Polar Limit with Logarithmic Indeterminate Form 0·(-inf)

🎯 Classic indeterminate form $0 \cdot \infty$ converted to polar coordinates; popular KFUPM Exam 1 challenging problem.
Problem Statement
Use polar coordinates and L'Hôpital's Rule to find the limit: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} (x^2 + y^2) \ln(x^2 + y^2)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$0$$
1. **Convert to Polar Coordinates:** Let $x = r\cos\theta$ and $y = r\sin\theta$, so that $x^2 + y^2 = r^2$. Note that $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$ corresponds to $r \to 0^+$: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} (x^2 + y^2) \ln(x^2 + y^2) = \lim_{r \to 0^+} r^2 \ln(r^2) = \lim_{r \to 0^+} 2r^2 \ln r$$ 2. **Rewrite as an Indeterminate Quotient:** As $r \to 0^+$, this has the indeterminate form $0 \cdot (-\infty)$. Rewrite by moving $r^2$ to the denominator: $$\lim_{r \to 0^+} \frac{2 \ln r}{r^{-2}}$$ This is now of the indeterminate form $\frac{-\infty}{\infty}$. 3. **Apply L'Hôpital's Rule:** Differentiating numerator and denominator with respect to $r$: $$\lim_{r \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{d}{dr}[2 \ln r]}{\frac{d}{dr}[r^{-2}]} = \lim_{r \to 0^+} \frac{\frac{2}{r}}{-2 r^{-3}} = \lim_{r \to 0^+} \left(-\frac{2}{r} \cdot \frac{r^3}{2}\right) = \lim_{r \to 0^+} (-r^2) = 0$$ Therefore: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} (x^2 + y^2) \ln(x^2 + y^2) = 0$$
Ex 62 Easy ContinuityThree Variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 62: Identifying Discontinuity Surfaces of a Three-Variable Function

🎯 Identifying surfaces of discontinuity in 3D; direct alignment with KFUPM Chapter 11/13 geometry.
Problem Statement
Discuss the continuity of the function: $$f(x, y, z) = \frac{z}{x^2 + y^2 - 4}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{Continuous at all points in } \mathbb{R}^3 \text{ except on the circular cylinder } x^2 + y^2 = 4$$
1. **Domain and Continuity Analysis:** The function $f(x, y, z) = \frac{z}{x^2 + y^2 - 4}$ is a rational function in three variables (the quotient of the linear polynomial $z$ and the quadratic polynomial $x^2 + y^2 - 4$). By Theorem 13.1, a rational function is continuous at every point in its domain. 2. **Identify Points of Discontinuity:** The function fails to be continuous precisely where the denominator is zero: $$x^2 + y^2 - 4 = 0 \iff x^2 + y^2 = 4$$ 3. **Geometric Interpretation in $\mathbb{R}^3$:** In three-dimensional space $\mathbb{R}^3$, the equation $x^2 + y^2 = 4$ represents a right circular cylinder of radius $2$ centered along the $z$-axis ($z$ is free). Conclusion: $f(x, y, z)$ is continuous at all points $(x, y, z) \in \mathbb{R}^3$ except those lying on the cylinder $x^2 + y^2 = 4$.
Ex 63 Easy ContinuityThree Variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 63: Continuity of an Exponential-Trigonometric Quotient

🎯 Conceptual multivariable continuity question confirming strictly positive denominator.
Problem Statement
Discuss the continuity of the function: $$f(x, y, z) = \frac{\sin z}{e^x + e^y}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{Continuous everywhere on } \mathbb{R}^3$$
1. **Analyze the Numerator and Denominator:** - Numerator: $g(x, y, z) = \sin z$ is continuous for all $(x, y, z) \in \mathbb{R}^3$. - Denominator: $h(x, y, z) = e^x + e^y$ is a sum of continuous exponential functions, hence continuous on $\mathbb{R}^3$. 2. **Check the Denominator for Zeroes:** For any real numbers $x$ and $y$, the exponential function is strictly positive: $$e^x > 0 \quad \text{and} \quad e^y > 0$$ Therefore: $$e^x + e^y > 0 \quad \text{for all } (x, y, z) \in \mathbb{R}^3$$ The denominator is never zero. 3. **Conclusion:** Since $f$ is the quotient of two functions that are continuous everywhere and the denominator is strictly positive everywhere, $f(x, y, z)$ is **continuous everywhere on $\mathbb{R}^3$**.
Ex 66 Hard ContinuityPiecewise Functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 66: Global Continuity of a Sinc-Type Piecewise Function

🎯 Piecewise continuity involving the fundamental trigonometric limit $\lim_{u\to 0} \frac{\sin u}{u} = 1$; recurring KFUPM conceptual exam question.
Problem Statement
Discuss the continuity of the piecewise function: $$f(x, y) = \begin{cases} \frac{\sin(x^2 - y^2)}{x^2 - y^2}, & x^2 \neq y^2 \\[6pt] 1, & x^2 = y^2 \end{cases}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{Continuous everywhere on } \mathbb{R}^2$$
1. **Region where $x^2 \neq y^2$:** For all points where $x^2 \neq y^2$, $f(x, y) = \frac{\sin(x^2 - y^2)}{x^2 - y^2}$. The numerator and denominator are continuous polynomials/trigonometric compositions, and the denominator is non-zero. Hence, $f(x, y)$ is continuous at all points where $x^2 \neq y^2$. 2. **Boundary lines $x^2 = y^2$ (i.e., $y = x$ and $y = -x$):** Let $(x_0, y_0)$ be any point such that $x_0^2 = y_0^2$. By definition of the function: $$f(x_0, y_0) = 1$$ Now evaluate the limit as $(x, y) \to (x_0, y_0)$: Let $u = x^2 - y^2$. As $(x, y) \to (x_0, y_0)$, we have $u \to x_0^2 - y_0^2 = 0$. Since $\lim_{u \to 0} \frac{\sin u}{u} = 1$, we obtain: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (x_0, y_0)} f(x, y) = \lim_{u \to 0} \frac{\sin u}{u} = 1$$ 3. **Conclusion:** Because $\lim_{(x, y) \to (x_0, y_0)} f(x, y) = f(x_0, y_0) = 1$ for every point on the lines $x^2 = y^2$, the function is continuous on the lines $x^2 = y^2$ as well. Therefore, $f(x, y)$ is **continuous everywhere on $\mathbb{R}^2$**.
Ex 71 Medium LimitsPartial Derivatives
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 71: Partial Derivatives via Difference Quotient Limits

🎯 Connects Chapter 13.2 limits to Chapter 13.3 partial derivatives; foundational for KFUPM definition-based questions.
Problem Statement
For the function $f(x, y) = x^2 - 4y$, find each limit: (a) $$\lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{f(x + \Delta x, y) - f(x, y)}{\Delta x}$$ (b) $$\lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{f(x, y + \Delta y) - f(x, y)}{\Delta y}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{(a) } 2x; \quad \text{(b) } -4$$
These limits represent the formal limit definitions of the first-order partial derivatives $\frac{\partial f}{\partial x}$ and $\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}$. **(a) Limit with respect to $x$:** Substitute $f(x + \Delta x, y) = (x + \Delta x)^2 - 4y$: $$\begin{aligned} \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{f(x + \Delta x, y) - f(x, y)}{\Delta x} &= \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{[(x + \Delta x)^2 - 4y] - (x^2 - 4y)}{\Delta x} \\[6pt] &= \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{x^2 + 2x\Delta x + (\Delta x)^2 - 4y - x^2 + 4y}{\Delta x} \\[6pt] &= \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{2x\Delta x + (\Delta x)^2}{\Delta x} \\[6pt] &= \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} (2x + \Delta x) = 2x \end{aligned}$$ **(b) Limit with respect to $y$:** Substitute $f(x, y + \Delta y) = x^2 - 4(y + \Delta y)$: $$\begin{aligned} \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{f(x, y + \Delta y) - f(x, y)}{\Delta y} &= \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{[x^2 - 4(y + \Delta y)] - (x^2 - 4y)}{\Delta y} \\[6pt] &= \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{x^2 - 4y - 4\Delta y - x^2 + 4y}{\Delta y} \\[6pt] &= \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{-4\Delta y}{\Delta y} = -4 \end{aligned}$$
Ex 77 Medium LimitsThree Variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 77: Evaluating a 3D Multivariable Limit via Spherical Coordinates

🎯 Using spherical coordinates to evaluate 3D limits; standard advanced technique in multivariable calculus.
Problem Statement
Use spherical coordinates to find the limit: $$\lim_{(x, y, z) \to (0, 0, 0)} \frac{xyz}{x^2 + y^2 + z^2}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$0$$
1. **Convert to Spherical Coordinates:** Let: $$x = \rho \sin\phi \cos\theta, \quad y = \rho \sin\phi \sin\theta, \quad z = \rho \cos\phi$$ Then $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = \rho^2$. As $(x, y, z) \to (0, 0, 0)$, $\rho \to 0^+$. 2. **Substitute into the Limit Expression:** $$\begin{aligned} \lim_{(x, y, z) \to (0, 0, 0)} \frac{xyz}{x^2 + y^2 + z^2} &= \lim_{\rho \to 0^+} \frac{(\rho \sin\phi \cos\theta)(\rho \sin\phi \sin\theta)(\rho \cos\phi)}{\rho^2} \\[6pt] &= \lim_{\rho \to 0^+} \frac{\rho^3 \sin^2\phi \cos\phi \sin\theta \cos\theta}{\rho^2} \\[6pt] &= \lim_{\rho \to 0^+} \rho \left(\sin^2\phi \cos\phi \sin\theta \cos\theta\right) \end{aligned}$$ 3. **Apply the Squeeze Theorem:** Since $|\sin\phi| \le 1$, $|\cos\phi| \le 1$, $|\sin\theta| \le 1$, and $|\cos\theta| \le 1$, the angular term is bounded: $$|\sin^2\phi \cos\phi \sin\theta \cos\theta| \le 1$$ Therefore: $$0 \le \left|\rho \sin^2\phi \cos\phi \sin\theta \cos\theta\right| \le \rho$$ As $\rho \to 0^+$, the upper bound $\rho \to 0$. By the Squeeze Theorem: $$\lim_{(x, y, z) \to (0, 0, 0)} \frac{xyz}{x^2 + y^2 + z^2} = 0$$
Ex 83 Medium LimitsInverse Trigonometric
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 83: Limit of Inverse Tangent with Unbounded Argument

🎯 Limits of composite functions with infinite inner limits; very common KFUPM multiple choice question.
Problem Statement
Find the limit: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 1)} \arctan\left[\frac{x^2 + 1}{x^2 + (y - 1)^2}\right]$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\frac{\pi}{2}$$
1. **Analyze the Behavior of the Inside Expression:** Let $g(x, y) = \frac{x^2 + 1}{x^2 + (y - 1)^2}$. Evaluate the numerator and denominator as $(x, y) \to (0, 1)$: - Numerator: $x^2 + 1 \to 0^2 + 1 = 1 > 0$. - Denominator: $x^2 + (y - 1)^2 \to 0^2 + (1 - 1)^2 = 0$. 2. **Determine the Sign of the Denominator:** Since $x^2 \ge 0$ and $(y - 1)^2 \ge 0$, their sum $x^2 + (y - 1)^2$ is strictly positive for all $(x, y) \neq (0, 1)$. Thus, the denominator approaches $0$ strictly from the positive side ($0^+$): $$x^2 + (y - 1)^2 \to 0^+$$ 3. **Evaluate the Inner Limit and Arctangent:** $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 1)} \frac{x^2 + 1}{x^2 + (y - 1)^2} = \frac{1}{0^+} = +\infty$$ Since the arctangent function satisfies $\lim_{u \to +\infty} \arctan(u) = \frac{\pi}{2}$ and is continuous, we have: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 1)} \arctan\left[\frac{x^2 + 1}{x^2 + (y - 1)^2}\right] = \frac{\pi}{2}$$
Ex 84 Hard ContinuityPolar Coordinates
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 84: Defining a Function Value at the Origin to Ensure Continuity

🎯 Classic KFUPM Exam 1 problem on removable discontinuity and defining piecewise continuous extensions.
Problem Statement
For the function $$f(x, y) = xy\left(\frac{x^2 - y^2}{x^2 + y^2}\right)$$ define $f(0, 0)$ such that $f$ is continuous at the origin.
Boxed Final Answer
$$f(0, 0) = 0$$
1. **Condition for Continuity at the Origin:** By definition, $f$ is continuous at $(0, 0)$ if and only if: $$f(0, 0) = \lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} f(x, y) = \lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} xy\left(\frac{x^2 - y^2}{x^2 + y^2}\right)$$ 2. **Evaluate the Limit Using Polar Coordinates:** Let $x = r\cos\theta$ and $y = r\sin\theta$, with $x^2 + y^2 = r^2$. Note that $(x, y) \to (0, 0) \iff r \to 0^+$: $$\begin{aligned} f(r\cos\theta, r\sin\theta) &= (r\cos\theta)(r\sin\theta) \left(\frac{r^2\cos^2\theta - r^2\sin^2\theta}{r^2}\right) \\[6pt] &= r^2 \cos\theta \sin\theta (\cos^2\theta - \sin^2\theta) \\[6pt] &= r^2 \left(\frac{1}{2}\sin 2\theta\right)(\cos 2\theta) \\[6pt] &= \frac{1}{4} r^2 \sin 4\theta \end{aligned}$$ 3. **Apply the Squeeze Theorem:** Since $|\sin 4\theta| \le 1$ for all $\theta$: $$0 \le \left|\frac{1}{4} r^2 \sin 4\theta\right| \le \frac{1}{4} r^2$$ As $r \to 0^+$, $\frac{1}{4} r^2 \to 0$. By the Squeeze Theorem: $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} xy\left(\frac{x^2 - y^2}{x^2 + y^2}\right) = 0$$ 4. **Conclusion:** To ensure continuity at the origin, we must define: $$f(0, 0) = 0$$ *(This function is famous in multivariable calculus as Clairaut's Theorem counterexample when smooth second mixed partials differ at the origin).*
