Ex 5 easy partial-derivativesquotient-rule
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 5: Determining Need for Quotient Rule in Partial Differentiation

🎯 Conceptual KFUPM multivariable calculus questions frequently test whether students recognize constant expressions with respect to a variable of differentiation, preventing wasted time applying cumbersome quotient rules.
Problem Statement
Explain whether the Quotient Rule should be used to find the partial derivative. Do not differentiate. $$\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{x^2 y}{y^2 - 3}\right)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{No, the Quotient Rule is not needed because } x \text{ occurs only in the numerator.}}$
To find the partial derivative with respect to $x$, treat $y$ as a constant. Notice that the denominator $y^2 - 3$ depends solely on $y$, and the factor $y$ in the numerator also depends solely on $y$. Therefore, the fraction can be rewritten by factoring out the constant coefficient: $$\frac{x^2 y}{y^2 - 3} = \left(\frac{y}{y^2 - 3}\right) x^2$$ Since $\frac{y}{y^2 - 3}$ is treated as a constant with respect to $x$, we apply the **Constant Multiple Rule** and the **Power Rule** rather than the Quotient Rule: $$\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{x^2 y}{y^2 - 3}\right) = \left(\frac{y}{y^2 - 3}\right) \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2) = \left(\frac{y}{y^2 - 3}\right)(2x) = \frac{2xy}{y^2 - 3}$$ Therefore, the Quotient Rule is unnecessary because $x$ appears only in the numerator.
Ex 10 easy partial-derivativesquotient-rule
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 10: Analyzing Necessity of Quotient Rule with Respect to y

🎯 Essential foundational rule for KFUPM exams. Recognizing constants simplifies derivatives across multiple-choice questions on speed-sensitive tests.
Problem Statement
Explain whether the Quotient Rule should be used to find the partial derivative. Do not differentiate. $$\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{xy}{x^2 + 1}\right)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{No, the Quotient Rule is not needed because } y \text{ occurs only in the numerator.}}$
To compute the partial derivative with respect to $y$, the variable $x$ is held constant. The denominator $x^2 + 1$ contains only $x$, and the factor $x$ in the numerator is also independent of $y$. Hence, we can separate the function into a constant coefficient multiplied by $y$: $$\frac{xy}{x^2 + 1} = \left(\frac{x}{x^2 + 1}\right) y$$ Differentiating with respect to $y$ requires only the **Constant Multiple Rule**: $$\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{xy}{x^2 + 1}\right) = \left(\frac{x}{x^2 + 1}\right) \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(y) = \frac{x}{x^2 + 1}(1) = \frac{x}{x^2 + 1}$$ The Quotient Rule is only required when the independent variable of differentiation appears in both the numerator and the denominator.
Ex 19 easy partial-derivativesfirst-order
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 19: First-Order Partial Derivatives of Exponential-Polynomial Product

🎯 Standard direct computational problem commonly tested in Exam 1 or Midterm warm-up sections.
Problem Statement
Find both first partial derivatives of the function: $$z = x^2 e^{2y}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = 2x e^{2y}, \quad \frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = 2x^2 e^{2y}}$
To find the first-order partial derivatives of $z = x^2 e^{2y}$: 1. **Partial derivative with respect to $x$:** Treat $y$ as a constant (which makes $e^{2y}$ a constant factor): $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(x^2\right) \cdot e^{2y} = (2x)e^{2y} = 2x e^{2y}$$ 2. **Partial derivative with respect to $y$:** Treat $x$ as a constant (which makes $x^2$ a constant factor): $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = x^2 \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(e^{2y}\right) = x^2 \cdot \left(e^{2y} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(2y)\right) = x^2 \cdot 2e^{2y} = 2x^2 e^{2y}$$
Ex 22 easy partial-derivativeslogarithms
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 22: First Partial Derivatives of Logarithmic Radical Function

🎯 Applying logarithmic identities prior to differentiation is a favorite time-saving strategy tested in KFUPM calculus exams.
Problem Statement
Find both first partial derivatives of the function: $$z = \ln\sqrt{xy}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{1}{2x}, \quad \frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = \frac{1}{2y}}$
Before differentiating, simplify the expression using standard logarithm properties: $$z = \ln\left((xy)^{1/2}\right) = \frac{1}{2}\ln(xy) = \frac{1}{2}(\ln x + \ln y) = \frac{1}{2}\ln x + \frac{1}{2}\ln y$$ (for $xy > 0$, $x, y > 0$). 1. **Partial derivative with respect to $x$:** Treat $y$ as a constant: $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(\ln x) + 0 = \frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{1}{x} = \frac{1}{2x}$$ 2. **Partial derivative with respect to $y$:** Treat $x$ as a constant: $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = 0 + \frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{d}{dy}(\ln y) = \frac{1}{2} \cdot \frac{1}{y} = \frac{1}{2y}$$ *Alternative verification via Chain Rule:* $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{xy}} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\sqrt{xy}\right) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{xy}} \cdot \frac{y}{2\sqrt{xy}} = \frac{y}{2xy} = \frac{1}{2x}$$ Both methods yield the same result.
Ex 27 easy partial-derivativeschain-rule
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 27: First Partial Derivatives of Gaussian-Type Exponential Function

🎯 Gaussian distributions and radial functions appear extensively in physics and engineering applications at KFUPM.
Problem Statement
Find both first partial derivatives of the function: $$h(x, y) = e^{-(x^2 + y^2)}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{h_x(x, y) = -2x e^{-(x^2 + y^2)}, \quad h_y(x, y) = -2y e^{-(x^2 + y^2)}}$
Given $h(x, y) = e^{-(x^2 + y^2)}$: 1. **Compute $h_x(x, y)$:** Treat $y$ as a constant and apply the multivariable chain rule $\frac{\partial}{\partial x} e^{u} = e^u \frac{\partial u}{\partial x}$ where $u = -(x^2 + y^2)$: $$\frac{\partial u}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(-x^2 - y^2\right) = -2x$$ $$h_x(x, y) = e^{-(x^2 + y^2)} \cdot (-2x) = -2x e^{-(x^2 + y^2)}$$ 2. **Compute $h_y(x, y)$:** Treat $x$ as a constant and apply the chain rule with respect to $y$: $$\frac{\partial u}{\partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(-x^2 - y^2\right) = -2y$$ $$h_y(x, y) = e^{-(x^2 + y^2)} \cdot (-2y) = -2y e^{-(x^2 + y^2)}$$
Ex 38 easy partial-derivativeshyperbolic-functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 38: First Partial Derivatives of Hyperbolic Cosine Function

🎯 Tests mastery of hyperbolic derivatives $\frac{d}{du}\cosh(u) = +\sinh(u)$ (no negative sign, unlike $\cos$).
Problem Statement
Find both first partial derivatives of the function: $$z = \cosh(xy^2)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = y^2 \sinh(xy^2), \quad \frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = 2xy \sinh(xy^2)}$
Recall that the derivative of the hyperbolic cosine is $\frac{d}{du}[\cosh u] = \sinh u$. 1. **Differentiating with respect to $x$:** Treat $y$ as constant. Let $u = xy^2$, so $\frac{\partial u}{\partial x} = y^2$: $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \sinh(xy^2) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(xy^2\right) = \sinh(xy^2) \cdot (y^2) = y^2 \sinh(xy^2)$$ 2. **Differentiating with respect to $y$:** Treat $x$ as constant. Let $u = xy^2$, so $\frac{\partial u}{\partial y} = 2xy$: $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = \sinh(xy^2) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(xy^2\right) = \sinh(xy^2) \cdot (2xy) = 2xy \sinh(xy^2)$$
Ex 39 medium partial-derivativesfundamental-theorem-of-calculus
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 39: Partial Differentiation of Definite Integral with Variable Limits

🎯 High-yield KFUPM exam question! Combining the Fundamental Theorem of Calculus with multivariable partial derivatives tests deep conceptual understanding.
Problem Statement
Find both first partial derivatives of the function: $$f(x, y) = \int_x^y (t^2 - 1)\,dt$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{f_x(x, y) = 1 - x^2, \quad f_y(x, y) = y^2 - 1}$
We can solve this problem using two equivalent approaches: **Approach 1: Direct Integration** Evaluate the definite integral with respect to $t$: $$f(x, y) = \left[ \frac{t^3}{3} - t \right]_x^y = \left(\frac{y^3}{3} - y\right) - \left(\frac{x^3}{3} - x\right)$$ Now take the partial derivatives: - For $f_x(x, y)$, treat $y$ as constant: $$f_x(x, y) = -\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{x^3}{3} - x\right) = -(x^2 - 1) = 1 - x^2$$ - For $f_y(x, y)$, treat $x$ as constant: $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{y^3}{3} - y\right) = y^2 - 1$$ **Approach 2: Fundamental Theorem of Calculus (Leibniz Integral Rule)** Recall that $\frac{d}{du} \int_a^u g(t)\,dt = g(u)$ and $\frac{d}{du} \int_u^b g(t)\,dt = -g(u)$. - With respect to $y$ (the upper limit): $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y} \int_x^y (t^2 - 1)\,dt = y^2 - 1$$ - With respect to $x$ (the lower limit): $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x} \int_x^y (t^2 - 1)\,dt = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(-\int_y^x (t^2 - 1)\,dt\right) = -(x^2 - 1) = 1 - x^2$$
Ex 41 easy partial-derivativeslimit-definition
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 41: Limit Definition of First Partial Derivatives for Linear Function

🎯 Appears on written exams and conceptual MCQs to verify that students know the formal definition $\lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{f(x+\Delta x, y)-f(x,y)}{\Delta x}$.
Problem Statement
Use the limit definition of partial derivatives to find $f_x(x, y)$ and $f_y(x, y)$ for: $$f(x, y) = 3x + 2y$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{f_x(x, y) = 3, \quad f_y(x, y) = 2}$
The limit definitions of the first-order partial derivatives are: $$f_x(x, y) = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{f(x + \Delta x, y) - f(x, y)}{\Delta x}$$ $$f_y(x, y) = \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{f(x, y + \Delta y) - f(x, y)}{\Delta y}$$ 1. **Compute $f_x(x, y)$:** $$f(x + \Delta x, y) = 3(x + \Delta x) + 2y = 3x + 3\Delta x + 2y$$ $$f(x + \Delta x, y) - f(x, y) = (3x + 3\Delta x + 2y) - (3x + 2y) = 3\Delta x$$ Substitute into the difference quotient: $$f_x(x, y) = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{3\Delta x}{\Delta x} = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} 3 = 3$$ 2. **Compute $f_y(x, y)$:** $$f(x, y + \Delta y) = 3x + 2(y + \Delta y) = 3x + 2y + 2\Delta y$$ $$f(x, y + \Delta y) - f(x, y) = (3x + 2y + 2\Delta y) - (3x + 2y) = 2\Delta y$$ Substitute into the difference quotient: $$f_y(x, y) = \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{2\Delta y}{\Delta y} = \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} 2 = 2$$
Ex 48 easy partial-derivativespoint-evaluation
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 48: Evaluating First Partial Derivatives of Sine Product at a Point

🎯 Evaluations at standard angles ($\pi/4, \pi/2, \pi$) are standard in KFUPM calculus tests.
Problem Statement
Find $f_x$ and $f_y$, and evaluate each at the given point: $$f(x, y) = \sin(xy), \quad \left(2, \frac{\pi}{4}\right)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{f_x\left(2, \frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 0, \quad f_y\left(2, \frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 0}$
1. **Find the first partial derivatives:** Using the chain rule: $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\sin(xy)\right) = \cos(xy) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(xy) = y \cos(xy)$$ $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\sin(xy)\right) = \cos(xy) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(xy) = x \cos(xy)$$ 2. **Evaluate at the point $\left(2, \frac{\pi}{4}\right)$:** Note that $xy = 2 \cdot \frac{\pi}{4} = \frac{\pi}{2}$. Since $\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 0$: $$f_x\left(2, \frac{\pi}{4}\right) = \frac{\pi}{4} \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = \frac{\pi}{4}(0) = 0$$ $$f_y\left(2, \frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 2 \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 2(0) = 0$$
Ex 49 medium partial-derivativesinverse-trigonometric
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 49: Evaluating Partial Derivatives of Inverse Tangent at a Point

🎯 The derivatives of $\arctan(y/x)$ frequently appear in polar coordinate conversions, gradients, and vector field curls on KFUPM exams.
Problem Statement
Find $f_x$ and $f_y$, and evaluate each at the given point: $$f(x, y) = \arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right), \quad (2, -2)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{f_x(2, -2) = \frac{1}{4}, \quad f_y(2, -2) = \frac{1}{4}}$
Recall the derivative rule: $\frac{d}{du}[\arctan u] = \frac{1}{1 + u^2}$. 1. **Find $f_x(x, y)$:** $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{1}{1 + (y/x)^2} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{y}{x}\right) = \frac{1}{1 + \frac{y^2}{x^2}} \left(-\frac{y}{x^2}\right) = \frac{x^2}{x^2 + y^2} \left(-\frac{y}{x^2}\right) = -\frac{y}{x^2 + y^2}$$ 2. **Find $f_y(x, y)$:** $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{1}{1 + (y/x)^2} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{y}{x}\right) = \frac{1}{1 + \frac{y^2}{x^2}} \left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = \frac{x^2}{x^2 + y^2} \left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2}$$ 3. **Evaluate at the point $(2, -2)$:** Compute $x^2 + y^2 = 2^2 + (-2)^2 = 4 + 4 = 8$. $$f_x(2, -2) = -\frac{-2}{8} = \frac{2}{8} = \frac{1}{4}$$ $$f_y(2, -2) = \frac{2}{8} = \frac{1}{4}$$
Ex 51 medium partial-derivativesquotient-rule
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 51: Evaluating Partial Derivatives of Rational Function at a Point

🎯 Quotient rule simplifications with negative signs are notorious trap questions on KFUPM Exam 1.
Problem Statement
Find $f_x$ and $f_y$, and evaluate each at the given point: $$f(x, y) = \frac{xy}{x - y}, \quad (2, -2)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{f_x(2, -2) = -\frac{1}{4}, \quad f_y(2, -2) = \frac{1}{4}}$
1. **Find $f_x(x, y)$ using the Quotient Rule:** $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{(x - y)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(xy) - xy\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x - y)}{(x - y)^2} = \frac{(x - y)(y) - xy(1)}{(x - y)^2} = \frac{xy - y^2 - xy}{(x - y)^2} = -\frac{y^2}{(x - y)^2}$$ 2. **Find $f_y(x, y)$ using the Quotient Rule:** $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{(x - y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(xy) - xy\frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x - y)}{(x - y)^2} = \frac{(x - y)(x) - xy(-1)}{(x - y)^2} = \frac{x^2 - xy + xy}{(x - y)^2} = \frac{x^2}{(x - y)^2}$$ 3. **Evaluate at $(2, -2)$:** Notice that the denominator evaluates to: $$x - y = 2 - (-2) = 4 \implies (x - y)^2 = 4^2 = 16$$ Then: $$f_x(2, -2) = -\frac{(-2)^2}{16} = -\frac{4}{16} = -\frac{1}{4}$$ $$f_y(2, -2) = \frac{2^2}{16} = \frac{4}{16} = \frac{1}{4}$$
Ex 54 medium partial-derivativesgeometric-slopes
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 54: Slopes of a Hemispherical Surface in Coordinate Directions

🎯 Directly relates partial derivatives to geometric slopes of curves on surfaces, a core concept in KFUPM calculus.
Problem Statement
Find the slopes of the surface in the $x$- and $y$-directions at the given point: $$z = \sqrt{25 - x^2 - y^2}, \quad (3, 0, 4)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Slope in } x\text{-direction: } -\frac{3}{4}, \quad \text{Slope in } y\text{-direction: } 0}$
The slope of a surface $z = f(x, y)$ in the $x$-direction is given by the partial derivative $\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}$, and the slope in the $y$-direction is given by $\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}$. 1. **Compute $\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}$:** $$z = (25 - x^2 - y^2)^{1/2}$$ $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{1}{2}(25 - x^2 - y^2)^{-1/2}(-2x) = -\frac{x}{\sqrt{25 - x^2 - y^2}}$$ 2. **Compute $\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}$:** $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = \frac{1}{2}(25 - x^2 - y^2)^{-1/2}(-2y) = -\frac{y}{\sqrt{25 - x^2 - y^2}}$$ 3. **Evaluate at the point $(3, 0, 4)$ (where $x = 3, y = 0$):** $$\sqrt{25 - 3^2 - 0^2} = \sqrt{25 - 9} = \sqrt{16} = 4$$ - Slope in the $x$-direction: $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}(3, 0) = -\frac{3}{4}$$ - Slope in the $y$-direction: $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}(3, 0) = -\frac{0}{4} = 0$$ *Geometric interpretation:* The surface is the upper hemisphere of radius $5$. At $(3, 0, 4)$, moving in the $x$-direction goes down along the circular cross-section, while moving in the $y$-direction is at the crest of the cross-section parallel to the $yz$-plane, where the tangent is horizontal.
Ex 60 medium partial-derivativesmultivariable-functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 60: First Partial Derivatives of Three-Variable Rational Function

🎯 Commonly tested in multivariable functions of three or more variables, requiring students to treat unselected variables as strict constants.
Problem Statement
Find the first partial derivatives with respect to $x$, $y$, and $z$ of the function: $$w = \frac{7xz}{x + y}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = \frac{7yz}{(x + y)^2}, \quad \frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = -\frac{7xz}{(x + y)^2}, \quad \frac{\partial w}{\partial z} = \frac{7x}{x + y}}$
Given $w = \frac{7xz}{x + y}$: 1. **Partial derivative with respect to $x$:** Both the numerator and the denominator contain $x$. Apply the Quotient Rule treating $y$ and $z$ as constants: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = \frac{(x + y)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(7xz) - 7xz\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x + y)}{(x + y)^2} = \frac{(x + y)(7z) - 7xz(1)}{(x + y)^2}$$ $$= \frac{7xz + 7yz - 7xz}{(x + y)^2} = \frac{7yz}{(x + y)^2}$$ 2. **Partial derivative with respect to $y$:** Here $y$ appears only in the denominator. Rewrite $w$ as $7xz(x + y)^{-1}$ and differentiate treating $x$ and $z$ as constants: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = 7xz \cdot (-1)(x + y)^{-2} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x + y) = -\frac{7xz}{(x + y)^2}$$ 3. **Partial derivative with respect to $z$:** Here $z$ appears only in the numerator to the first power. The term $\frac{7x}{x + y}$ is constant with respect to $z$: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial z} = \left(\frac{7x}{x + y}\right) \frac{\partial}{\partial z}(z) = \frac{7x}{x + y}$$
Ex 67 medium partial-derivativesthree-variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 67: Evaluating First Partials of Three-Variable Trigonometric Function

🎯 Standard KFUPM Exam 1 question format: evaluating partials of multivariable trig functions at specific coordinates.
Problem Statement
Find $f_x$, $f_y$, and $f_z$, and evaluate each at the given point: $$f(x, y, z) = z \sin(x + 6y), \quad \left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}, -4\right)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{f_x\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}, -4\right) = 4, \quad f_y\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}, -4\right) = 24, \quad f_z\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}, -4\right) = 0}$
1. **Find the first partial derivatives:** - With respect to $x$ ($y$ and $z$ constant): $$f_x(x, y, z) = z \cos(x + 6y) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x + 6y) = z \cos(x + 6y)$$ - With respect to $y$ ($x$ and $z$ constant): $$f_y(x, y, z) = z \cos(x + 6y) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x + 6y) = 6z \cos(x + 6y)$$ - With respect to $z$ ($x$ and $y$ constant): $$f_z(x, y, z) = (1)\sin(x + 6y) = \sin(x + 6y)$$ 2. **Evaluate at the point $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}, -4\right)$:** The argument of the sine and cosine functions is: $$x + 6y = 0 + 6\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 3\pi$$ Using $\cos(3\pi) = \cos(\pi) = -1$ and $\sin(3\pi) = 0$: $$f_x\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}, -4\right) = (-4)\cos(3\pi) = (-4)(-1) = 4$$ $$f_y\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}, -4\right) = 6(-4)\cos(3\pi) = -24(-1) = 24$$ $$f_z\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}, -4\right) = \sin(3\pi) = 0$$
Ex 69 medium partial-derivativescritical-points
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 69: Finding Critical Points via Simultaneous Zero Partials

🎯 Direct prerequisite for Section 13.8 (Extrema of Functions of Two Variables) and heavily tested in KFUPM calculus.
Problem Statement
Find all values of $x$ and $y$ such that $f_x(x, y) = 0$ and $f_y(x, y) = 0$ simultaneously for: $$f(x, y) = x^2 + xy + y^2 - 2x + 2y$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{(2, -2)}$
1. **Find the first partial derivatives:** $$f_x(x, y) = 2x + y - 2$$ $$f_y(x, y) = x + 2y + 2$$ 2. **Set both partial derivatives equal to zero simultaneously:** $$\begin{cases} 2x + y - 2 = 0 & \text{(Equation 1)} \\[4pt] x + 2y + 2 = 0 & \text{(Equation 2)} \end{cases}$$ 3. **Solve the linear system:** From Equation 1, express $y$ in terms of $x$: $$y = 2 - 2x$$ Substitute this into Equation 2: $$x + 2(2 - 2x) + 2 = 0$$ $$x + 4 - 4x + 2 = 0 \implies -3x + 6 = 0 \implies 3x = 6 \implies x = 2$$ Now find $y$: $$y = 2 - 2(2) = 2 - 4 = -2$$ 4. **Verify the solution:** - $f_x(2, -2) = 2(2) + (-2) - 2 = 4 - 4 = 0$ - $f_y(2, -2) = 2 + 2(-2) + 2 = 2 - 4 + 2 = 0$ Therefore, the only point where both partial derivatives vanish simultaneously is $(2, -2)$.
Ex 73 medium partial-derivativescritical-points
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 73: Finding Critical Points for Non-Linear Sum Function

🎯 A classic optimization/critical-point problem in KFUPM calculus exams, often reappearing in 13.8 when classifying local extrema.
Problem Statement
Find all values of $x$ and $y$ such that $f_x(x, y) = 0$ and $f_y(x, y) = 0$ simultaneously for: $$f(x, y) = \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{y} + xy$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{(1, 1)}$
The domain of $f(x, y)$ is all $(x, y)$ such that $x \neq 0$ and $y \neq 0$. 1. **Find the first partial derivatives:** $$f_x(x, y) = -\frac{1}{x^2} + y$$ $$f_y(x, y) = -\frac{1}{y^2} + x$$ 2. **Set both partial derivatives equal to zero:** $$\begin{cases} -\frac{1}{x^2} + y = 0 \implies y = \frac{1}{x^2} & \text{(Equation 1)} \\[6pt] -\frac{1}{y^2} + x = 0 \implies x = \frac{1}{y^2} & \text{(Equation 2)} \end{cases}$$ 3. **Solve the non-linear system:** Substitute $y = \frac{1}{x^2}$ from Equation 1 into Equation 2: $$x = \frac{1}{\left(\frac{1}{x^2}\right)^2} = \frac{1}{\frac{1}{x^4}} = x^4$$ Rearranging gives: $$x^4 - x = 0 \implies x(x^3 - 1) = 0 \implies x(x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1) = 0$$ Since $x \neq 0$ (from the domain) and $x^2 + x + 1 = 0$ has no real solutions (discriminant $1 - 4 = -3 < 0$), the only real solution is: $$x = 1$$ Substitute $x = 1$ back into Equation 1: $$y = \frac{1}{1^2} = 1$$ 4. **Verify the critical point:** - $f_x(1, 1) = -\frac{1}{1^2} + 1 = -1 + 1 = 0$ - $f_y(1, 1) = -\frac{1}{1^2} + 1 = -1 + 1 = 0$ Thus, the only point is $(1, 1)$.
Ex 82 medium second-order-partialsmixed-partials
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 82: Finding Four Second-Order Partial Derivatives of Logarithmic Difference

🎯 Clairaut's theorem and the equality of mixed second-order partials is tested repeatedly on KFUPM Midterm and Final exams.
Problem Statement
Find the four second partial derivatives and observe that the mixed partials are equal: $$z = \ln(x - y)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2} = -\frac{1}{(x - y)^2}, \quad \frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial y^2} = -\frac{1}{(x - y)^2}, \quad \frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial y \partial x} = \frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x \partial y} = \frac{1}{(x - y)^2}}$
The function is defined for $x - y > 0$. 1. **First-order partial derivatives:** $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{1}{x - y} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x - y) = (x - y)^{-1}$$ $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = \frac{1}{x - y} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}(x - y) = -(x - y)^{-1}$$ 2. **Second-order partial derivatives:** - Differentiating $\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}$ with respect to $x$: $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[(x - y)^{-1}\right] = -(x - y)^{-2}(1) = -\frac{1}{(x - y)^2}$$ - Differentiating $\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}$ with respect to $y$: $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial y^2} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[-(x - y)^{-1}\right] = -(-1)(x - y)^{-2}(-1) = -\frac{1}{(x - y)^2}$$ - Differentiating $\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}$ with respect to $y$ (mixed partial): $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial y \partial x} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[(x - y)^{-1}\right] = -(x - y)^{-2}(-1) = \frac{1}{(x - y)^2}$$ - Differentiating $\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}$ with respect to $x$ (mixed partial): $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x \partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[-(x - y)^{-1}\right] = -(-1)(x - y)^{-2}(1) = \frac{1}{(x - y)^2}$$ 3. **Observation:** The second mixed partials are equal: $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial y \partial x} = \frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x \partial y} = \frac{1}{(x - y)^2}$$ confirming Clairaut's Theorem on the open domain $x > y$.
Ex 93 medium higher-order-partialsthird-order-partials
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 93: Equivalence of Third-Order Mixed Partials (Clairaut's Extension)

🎯 Higher-order mixed partials appear frequently in advanced conceptual and computational multiple-choice questions.
Problem Statement
Show that the mixed partial derivatives $f_{xyy}$, $f_{yxy}$, and $f_{yyx}$ are equal for: $$f(x, y, z) = e^{-x} \sin(yz)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{f_{xyy} = f_{yxy} = f_{yyx} = z^2 e^{-x} \sin(yz)}$
We compute each third-order mixed partial derivative step-by-step: 1. **Compute $f_{xyy}$ (order of differentiation: $x$, then $y$, then $y$):** - First derivative with respect to $x$: $$f_x = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[e^{-x} \sin(yz)\right] = -e^{-x} \sin(yz)$$ - Differentiate with respect to $y$: $$f_{xy} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[-e^{-x} \sin(yz)\right] = -e^{-x} \left(\cos(yz) \cdot z\right) = -z e^{-x} \cos(yz)$$ - Differentiate again with respect to $y$: $$f_{xyy} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[-z e^{-x} \cos(yz)\right] = -z e^{-x} \left(-\sin(yz) \cdot z\right) = z^2 e^{-x} \sin(yz)$$ 2. **Compute $f_{yxy}$ (order of differentiation: $y$, then $x$, then $y$):** - First derivative with respect to $y$: $$f_y = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[e^{-x} \sin(yz)\right] = z e^{-x} \cos(yz)$$ - Differentiate with respect to $x$: $$f_{yx} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[z e^{-x} \cos(yz)\right] = -z e^{-x} \cos(yz)$$ - Differentiate with respect to $y$: $$f_{yxy} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[-z e^{-x} \cos(yz)\right] = -z e^{-x} \left(-\sin(yz) \cdot z\right) = z^2 e^{-x} \sin(yz)$$ 3. **Compute $f_{yyx}$ (order of differentiation: $y$, then $y$, then $x$):** - Second derivative with respect to $y$: $$f_{yy} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[z e^{-x} \cos(yz)\right] = z e^{-x}\left(-\sin(yz) \cdot z\right) = -z^2 e^{-x} \sin(yz)$$ - Differentiate with respect to $x$: $$f_{yyx} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[-z^2 e^{-x} \sin(yz)\right] = -z^2 \left(-e^{-x}\right) \sin(yz) = z^2 e^{-x} \sin(yz)$$ **Conclusion:** All three higher-order mixed partial derivatives are equal: $$f_{xyy} = f_{yxy} = f_{yyx} = z^2 e^{-x} \sin(yz)$$ This verifies Clairaut's Theorem for third-order mixed partial derivatives on smooth functions.
Ex 98 medium laplaces-equationsecond-order-partials
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 98: Verifying Laplace's Equation for Inverse Tangent

🎯 Laplace's equation (harmonic functions) is a recurring KFUPM exam topic, connecting multivariable calculus with electrostatics and fluid mechanics.
Problem Statement
Show that the function satisfies Laplace's equation $\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial y^2} = 0$: $$z = \arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial y^2} = \frac{2xy}{(x^2 + y^2)^2} + \left(-\frac{2xy}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}\right) = 0}$
Laplace's equation in two dimensions is given by: $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial y^2} = 0$$ 1. **Compute the partial derivatives with respect to $x$:** From the chain rule: $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{1}{1 + (y/x)^2} \cdot \left(-\frac{y}{x^2}\right) = -\frac{y}{x^2 + y^2} = -y(x^2 + y^2)^{-1}$$ Differentiate again with respect to $x$: $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[-y(x^2 + y^2)^{-1}\right] = -y \cdot (-1)(x^2 + y^2)^{-2}(2x) = \frac{2xy}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}$$ 2. **Compute the partial derivatives with respect to $y$:** $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = \frac{1}{1 + (y/x)^2} \cdot \left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2} = x(x^2 + y^2)^{-1}$$ Differentiate again with respect to $y$: $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial y^2} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[x(x^2 + y^2)^{-1}\right] = x \cdot (-1)(x^2 + y^2)^{-2}(2y) = -\frac{2xy}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}$$ 3. **Substitute into Laplace's equation:** $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2} + \frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial y^2} = \frac{2xy}{(x^2 + y^2)^2} + \left(-\frac{2xy}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}\right) = 0$$ Hence, $z = \arctan(y/x)$ is a harmonic function satisfying Laplace's equation.
Ex 99 medium wave-equationsecond-order-partials
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 99: Verifying the One-Dimensional Wave Equation

🎯 Wave, heat, and Laplace equations are classical PDE verification questions prominently featured in KFUPM calculus syllabi.
Problem Statement
Show that the function satisfies the wave equation $\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial t^2} = c^2\left(\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2}\right)$: $$z = \sin(x - ct)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial t^2} = -c^2\sin(x - ct) = c^2\left(-\sin(x - ct)\right) = c^2\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2}}$
The one-dimensional wave equation is: $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial t^2} = c^2\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2}$$ 1. **Compute second partial derivative with respect to $t$:** - First derivative with respect to $t$ (using chain rule with $\frac{\partial}{\partial t}(x - ct) = -c$): $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial t} = \cos(x - ct) \cdot (-c) = -c\cos(x - ct)$$ - Second derivative with respect to $t$: $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial t^2} = \frac{\partial}{\partial t}\left[-c\cos(x - ct)\right] = -c \left(-\sin(x - ct) \cdot (-c)\right) = -c^2 \sin(x - ct)$$ 2. **Compute second partial derivative with respect to $x$:** - First derivative with respect to $x$ (with $\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x - ct) = 1$): $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \cos(x - ct) \cdot 1 = \cos(x - ct)$$ - Second derivative with respect to $x$: $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2} = -\sin(x - ct) \cdot 1 = -\sin(x - ct)$$ 3. **Compare both sides:** $$c^2 \frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2} = c^2\left(-\sin(x - ct)\right) = -c^2 \sin(x - ct)$$ Since $\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial t^2} = -c^2 \sin(x - ct)$, it follows that: $$\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial t^2} = c^2\frac{\partial^2 z}{\partial x^2}$$ Thus, $z = \sin(x - ct)$ satisfies the wave equation.
Ex 116 easy partial-derivativesapplied-rates-of-change
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 116: Rates of Change for Volume of a Right-Circular Cone

🎯 Practical applied rate of change problems frequently appear on KFUPM exams to test the physical meaning of partial derivatives.
Problem Statement
The volume of a right-circular cone of radius $r$ and height $h$ is represented by $V = \frac{1}{3}\pi r^2 h$. (a) Find the rate of change of $V$ with respect to $r$ for $r = 2$ and $h = 2$. (b) Find the rate of change of $V$ with respect to $h$ for $r = 2$ and $h = 2$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{(a) } \frac{\partial V}{\partial r}(2, 2) = \frac{8\pi}{3}, \quad \text{(b) } \frac{\partial V}{\partial h}(2, 2) = \frac{4\pi}{3}}$
Given the cone volume formula $V(r, h) = \frac{1}{3}\pi r^2 h$: **(a) Rate of change of $V$ with respect to $r$:** $$\frac{\partial V}{\partial r} = \frac{\partial}{\partial r}\left(\frac{1}{3}\pi r^2 h\right) = \frac{2}{3}\pi r h$$ Evaluate at $r = 2$ and $h = 2$: $$\left.\frac{\partial V}{\partial r}\right|_{(2, 2)} = \frac{2}{3}\pi (2)(2) = \frac{8\pi}{3}$$ **(b) Rate of change of $V$ with respect to $h$:** $$\frac{\partial V}{\partial h} = \frac{\partial}{\partial h}\left(\frac{1}{3}\pi r^2 h\right) = \frac{1}{3}\pi r^2$$ Evaluate at $r = 2$ and $h = 2$: $$\left.\frac{\partial V}{\partial h}\right|_{(2, 2)} = \frac{1}{3}\pi (2)^2 = \frac{4\pi}{3}$$
Ex 129 hard piecewise-functionslimit-definition
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 129: Counterexample to Clairaut's Theorem for Non-Continuous Mixed Partials

🎯 The premier theoretical counterexample in multivariable calculus. It demonstrates why the continuity hypothesis in Clairaut's Theorem is indispensable.
Problem Statement
Consider the function defined by: $$f(x, y) = \begin{cases} \frac{xy(x^2 - y^2)}{x^2 + y^2}, & (x, y) \neq (0, 0) \\[6pt] 0, & (x, y) = (0, 0) \end{cases}$$ (a) Find $f_x(x, y)$ and $f_y(x, y)$ for $(x, y) \neq (0, 0)$. (b) Use the definition of partial derivatives to find $f_x(0, 0)$ and $f_y(0, 0)$. (c) Use the definition of partial derivatives to find $f_{xy}(0, 0)$ and $f_{yx}(0, 0)$. (d) Using Theorem 13.3 (Clairaut's Theorem) and the result of part (c), what can be said about $f_{xy}$ or $f_{yx}$?
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\begin{aligned} &\text{(a) } f_x(x, y) = \frac{y(x^4 + 4x^2 y^2 - y^4)}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}, \quad f_y(x, y) = \frac{x(x^4 - 4x^2 y^2 - y^4)}{(x^2 + y^2)^2} \\ &\text{(b) } f_x(0, 0) = 0, \quad f_y(0, 0) = 0 \\ &\text{(c) } f_{xy}(0, 0) = -1, \quad f_{yx}(0, 0) = 1 \\ &\text{(d) At least one of } f_{xy} \text{ or } f_{yx} \text{ is not continuous at } (0, 0) \end{aligned}}$
This is the famous Peano counterexample showing that mixed partial derivatives need not be equal if they fail to be continuous. **(a) Find $f_x(x, y)$ and $f_y(x, y)$ for $(x, y) \neq (0, 0)$:** Write $f(x, y) = \frac{x^3 y - xy^3}{x^2 + y^2}$. Using the Quotient Rule for $f_x$: $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{(x^2 + y^2)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^3 y - xy^3) - (x^3 y - xy^3)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2 + y^2)}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}$$ $$= \frac{(x^2 + y^2)(3x^2 y - y^3) - (x^3 y - xy^3)(2x)}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}$$ $$= \frac{(3x^4 y - x^2 y^3 + 3x^2 y^3 - y^5) - (2x^4 y - 2x^2 y^3)}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}$$ $$= \frac{x^4 y + 4x^2 y^3 - y^5}{(x^2 + y^2)^2} = \frac{y(x^4 + 4x^2 y^2 - y^4)}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}$$ By antisymmetry, since $f(y, x) = -f(x, y)$, we have: $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{x(x^4 - 4x^2 y^2 - y^4)}{(x^2 + y^2)^2}$$ **(b) Find $f_x(0, 0)$ and $f_y(0, 0)$ using the limit definition:** Since $f(\Delta x, 0) = \frac{(\Delta x)(0)((\Delta x)^2 - 0)}{(\Delta x)^2 + 0} = 0$ for all $\Delta x \neq 0$: $$f_x(0, 0) = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{f(\Delta x, 0) - f(0, 0)}{\Delta x} = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{0 - 0}{\Delta x} = 0$$ Similarly, since $f(0, \Delta y) = 0$ for all $\Delta y \neq 0$: $$f_y(0, 0) = \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{f(0, \Delta y) - f(0, 0)}{\Delta y} = \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{0 - 0}{\Delta y} = 0$$ **(c) Find $f_{xy}(0, 0)$ and $f_{yx}(0, 0)$ using the limit definition:** By definition, $f_{xy}(0, 0) = (f_x)_y(0, 0)$: $$f_{xy}(0, 0) = \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{f_x(0, \Delta y) - f_x(0, 0)}{\Delta y}$$ From part (a), evaluate $f_x(0, \Delta y)$ for $\Delta y \neq 0$: $$f_x(0, \Delta y) = \frac{\Delta y(0 + 0 - (\Delta y)^4)}{(0 + (\Delta y)^2)^2} = \frac{-(\Delta y)^5}{(\Delta y)^4} = -\Delta y$$ Therefore: $$f_{xy}(0, 0) = \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{-\Delta y - 0}{\Delta y} = \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} (-1) = -1$$ By definition, $f_{yx}(0, 0) = (f_y)_x(0, 0)$: $$f_{yx}(0, 0) = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{f_y(\Delta x, 0) - f_y(0, 0)}{\Delta x}$$ From part (a), evaluate $f_y(\Delta x, 0)$ for $\Delta x \neq 0$: $$f_y(\Delta x, 0) = \frac{\Delta x((\Delta x)^4 - 0 - 0)}{((\Delta x)^2 + 0)^2} = \frac{(\Delta x)^5}{(\Delta x)^4} = \Delta x$$ Therefore: $$f_{yx}(0, 0) = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{\Delta x - 0}{\Delta x} = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} 1 = 1$$ **(d) Conclusion regarding Theorem 13.3 (Clairaut's Theorem):** Theorem 13.3 states that if both mixed second partial derivatives $f_{xy}$ and $f_{yx}$ are continuous on an open disk containing the origin, then they must be equal: $f_{xy}(0, 0) = f_{yx}(0, 0)$. Since here $f_{xy}(0, 0) = -1 \neq 1 = f_{yx}(0, 0)$, the condition of the theorem must fail. Therefore, $f_{xy}$, $f_{yx}$, or both are **not continuous** at $(0, 0)$.
Ex 131 hard piecewise-derivativelimit-definition
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 131: Piecewise Derivative of Fractional Power via Limit Definition

🎯 Frequently tested concept in KFUPM calculus: functions whose derivatives cannot be found at the origin via standard power/chain rules, requiring recourse to the fundamental limit definition.
Problem Statement
Consider the function $f(x, y) = (x^2 + y^2)^{2/3}$. Show that: $$f_x(x, y) = \begin{cases} \frac{4x}{3(x^2 + y^2)^{1/3}}, & (x, y) \neq (0, 0) \\[6pt] 0, & (x, y) = (0, 0) \end{cases}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{f_x(x, y) = \begin{cases} \frac{4x}{3(x^2 + y^2)^{1/3}}, & (x, y) \neq (0, 0) \\[6pt] 0, & (x, y) = (0, 0) \end{cases}}$
We analyze the partial derivative in two separate cases: **Case 1: For $(x, y) \neq (0, 0)$** Since $x^2 + y^2 > 0$, the function is differentiable by the standard Chain Rule: $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[(x^2 + y^2)^{2/3}\right] = \frac{2}{3}(x^2 + y^2)^{2/3 - 1} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}(x^2 + y^2)$$ $$= \frac{2}{3}(x^2 + y^2)^{-1/3} \cdot (2x) = \frac{4x}{3(x^2 + y^2)^{1/3}}$$ **Case 2: At $(x, y) = (0, 0)$** Direct substitution into the Case 1 formula yields $\frac{0}{0}$, which is undefined. Therefore, we must apply the formal limit definition of the partial derivative: $$f_x(0, 0) = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{f(0 + \Delta x, 0) - f(0, 0)}{\Delta x}$$ Evaluate each term: $$f(\Delta x, 0) = \left((\Delta x)^2 + 0^2\right)^{2/3} = ((\Delta x)^2)^{2/3} = (\Delta x)^{4/3}$$ $$f(0, 0) = (0^2 + 0^2)^{2/3} = 0$$ Now substitute into the limit: $$f_x(0, 0) = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{(\Delta x)^{4/3} - 0}{\Delta x} = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{(\Delta x)^{4/3}}{\Delta x} = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} (\Delta x)^{4/3 - 1} = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} (\Delta x)^{1/3} = 0$$ **Conclusion:** Combining Case 1 and Case 2 yields the piecewise definition: $$f_x(x, y) = \begin{cases} \frac{4x}{3(x^2 + y^2)^{1/3}}, & (x, y) \neq (0, 0) \\[6pt] 0, & (x, y) = (0, 0) \end{cases}$$ which proves the given statement.

13.4 Differentials & Linear Approx

Suggested Textbook Practice Problems • 12 Curated Exercises

Jump to problem:
Ex 5 Easy Total DifferentialExponential Functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 5: Total Differential of an Exponential Difference Function

🎯 Standard KFUPM Major Exam 2 question on computing the total differential dz = f_x dx + f_y dy for exponential compositions.
Problem Statement
Find the total differential of the function: $$z = \frac{1}{2}\left(e^{x^2+y^2} - e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\right)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{dz = \left(e^{x^2+y^2} + e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\right)(x\,dx + y\,dy) = 2\cosh(x^2+y^2)(x\,dx + y\,dy)}$$
**Step 1: State the definition of the total differential.** For a function of two variables $z = f(x, y)$, the total differential is defined as: $$dz = \frac{\partial z}{\partial x}\,dx + \frac{\partial z}{\partial y}\,dy$$ **Step 2: Differentiate partially with respect to $x$.** Treating $y$ as a constant and using the chain rule $\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(e^{u}\right) = e^u \frac{\partial u}{\partial x}$ where $u = x^2 + y^2$ (so $\frac{\partial u}{\partial x} = 2x$): $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{1}{2}\left[ e^{x^2+y^2}(2x) - e^{-(x^2+y^2)}(-2x) \right]$$ Factor out $2x$: $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{1}{2} \cdot 2x \left(e^{x^2+y^2} + e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\right) = x\left(e^{x^2+y^2} + e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\right)$$ **Step 3: Differentiate partially with respect to $y$.** By symmetry with respect to $y$ (where $\frac{\partial u}{\partial y} = 2y$): $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = \frac{1}{2}\left[ e^{x^2+y^2}(2y) - e^{-(x^2+y^2)}(-2y) \right] = y\left(e^{x^2+y^2} + e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\right)$$ **Step 4: Combine into the total differential.** $$dz = x\left(e^{x^2+y^2} + e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\right)dx + y\left(e^{x^2+y^2} + e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\right)dy$$ Factoring the common term $\left(e^{x^2+y^2} + e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\right)$ gives: $$dz = \left(e^{x^2+y^2} + e^{-(x^2+y^2)}\right)(x\,dx + y\,dy)$$ *(Optional Hyperbolic Representation)*: Recalling $\cosh(u) = \frac{e^u + e^{-u}}{2}$, this simplifies compactly to: $$dz = 2\cosh(x^2+y^2)(x\,dx + y\,dy)$$
Ex 7 Medium Total DifferentialFunctions of Three Variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 7: Total Differential of a Three-Variable Function with Trigonometry

🎯 High-yield KFUPM question testing the extension of the total differential to three independent variables: dw = w_x dx + w_y dy + w_z dz.
Problem Statement
Find the total differential of the function of three variables: $$w = x^2 y z^2 + \sin(yz)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{dw = 2xyz^2\,dx + \left(x^2 z^2 + z\cos(yz)\right)dy + \left(2x^2 yz + y\cos(yz)\right)dz}$$
**Step 1: State the formula for the total differential in three variables.** For $w = f(x, y, z)$, the total differential is: $$dw = \frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\,dx + \frac{\partial w}{\partial y}\,dy + \frac{\partial w}{\partial z}\,dz$$ **Step 2: Compute each first-order partial derivative.** 1. Differentiate with respect to $x$ (treating $y$ and $z$ as constants): $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[x^2 y z^2 + \sin(yz)\right] = 2x y z^2 + 0 = 2xyz^2$$ 2. Differentiate with respect to $y$ (treating $x$ and $z$ as constants): $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[x^2 z^2 \cdot y\right] + \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[\sin(yz)\right] = x^2 z^2 + z\cos(yz)$$ 3. Differentiate with respect to $z$ (treating $x$ and $y$ as constants): $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial z} = \frac{\partial}{\partial z}\left[x^2 y \cdot z^2\right] + \frac{\partial}{\partial z}\left[\sin(yz)\right] = 2x^2 y z + y\cos(yz)$$ **Step 3: Assemble the total differential.** Substitute the partial derivatives into the differential equation: $$dw = 2xyz^2\,dx + \left(x^2 z^2 + z\cos(yz)\right)dy + \left(2x^2 yz + y\cos(yz)\right)dz$$
Ex 14 Medium Linear ApproximationExact Increment
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 14: Comparing Exact Increment Delta z with Differential dz for a Trigonometric Product

🎯 Fundamental KFUPM concept comparing the true surface increment Delta z with the linear tangent plane approximation dz.
Problem Statement
For the function $f(x, y) = x\cos y$: (a) Find $f(2, 1)$ and $f(2.1, 1.05)$ and calculate the exact change $\Delta z = f(2.1, 1.05) - f(2, 1)$. (b) Use the total differential $dz$ to approximate $\Delta z$.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{(a) } f(2, 1) \approx 1.0806, \; f(2.1, 1.05) \approx 1.0449, \; \Delta z \approx \boxed{-0.0357}; \quad \text{(b) } dz \approx \boxed{-0.0301}$$
**Part (a): Calculate the exact values and increment $\Delta z$.** 1. Evaluate $f$ at the base point $(x_0, y_0) = (2, 1)$: $$f(2, 1) = 2\cos(1) \approx 2(0.5403023) \approx 1.080605$$ 2. Evaluate $f$ at the perturbed point $(x_0 + \Delta x, y_0 + \Delta y) = (2.1, 1.05)$: $$f(2.1, 1.05) = 2.1\cos(1.05) \approx 2.1(0.4975710) \approx 1.044899$$ 3. Compute the exact increment $\Delta z$: $$\Delta z = f(2.1, 1.05) - f(2, 1) = 1.044899 - 1.080605 = -0.035706 \approx -0.0357$$ **Part (b): Approximate $\Delta z$ using the total differential $dz$.** 1. Identify the increments in the independent variables: $$dx = \Delta x = 2.1 - 2 = 0.1, \qquad dy = \Delta y = 1.05 - 1 = 0.05$$ 2. Compute the first-order partial derivatives of $f(x, y) = x\cos y$: $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[x\cos y] = \cos y \implies f_x(2, 1) = \cos(1) \approx 0.540302$$ $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[x\cos y] = -x\sin y \implies f_y(2, 1) = -2\sin(1) \approx -2(0.841471) = -1.682942$$ 3. Compute the total differential $dz$: $$dz = f_x(2, 1)\,dx + f_y(2, 1)\,dy = \cos(1)(0.1) - 2\sin(1)(0.05)$$ $$dz \approx (0.540302)(0.1) - (1.682942)(0.05) = 0.054030 - 0.084147 = -0.030117 \approx -0.0301$$ **Comparison:** The linear approximation yields $dz \approx -0.0301$, which closely mirrors the true increment $\Delta z \approx -0.0357$, with an approximation error of $|\Delta z - dz| \approx 0.0056$.
Ex 17 Medium Linear ApproximationTotal Differential
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 17: Linear Approximation of a Trigonometric Sine Increment

🎯 Classic KFUPM exam problem using total differentials to estimate numerical differences without a calculator.
Problem Statement
Find an appropriate function $z = f(x, y)$ and use the total differential to approximate the quantity: $$\sin\left[(1.05)^2 + (0.95)^2\right] - \sin(1^2 + 1^2)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{dz = 0}$$
**Step 1: Define the function $z = f(x, y)$ and the base point.** Consider the multivariable function: $$f(x, y) = \sin(x^2 + y^2)$$ We want to estimate the increment $\Delta z = f(1.05, 0.95) - f(1, 1)$. The initial point is $(x_0, y_0) = (1, 1)$, and the perturbed point is $(x, y) = (1.05, 0.95)$. **Step 2: Determine the differentials $dx$ and $dy$.** $$dx = \Delta x = 1.05 - 1 = 0.05$$ $$dy = \Delta y = 0.95 - 1 = -0.05$$ **Step 3: Compute the partial derivatives at $(1, 1)$.** Using the chain rule: $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[\sin(x^2 + y^2)] = 2x\cos(x^2 + y^2)$$ $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[\sin(x^2 + y^2)] = 2y\cos(x^2 + y^2)$$ At $(x_0, y_0) = (1, 1)$: $$f_x(1, 1) = 2(1)\cos(1^2 + 1^2) = 2\cos(2)$$ $$f_y(1, 1) = 2(1)\cos(1^2 + 1^2) = 2\cos(2)$$ **Step 4: Evaluate the total differential $dz$.** $$dz = f_x(1, 1)\,dx + f_y(1, 1)\,dy = 2\cos(2)(0.05) + 2\cos(2)(-0.05)$$ $$dz = 2\cos(2)[0.05 - 0.05] = 2\cos(2)(0) = 0$$ **Conclusion:** The linear approximation of the change is $dz = 0$. (Notice that $(1.05)^2 + (0.95)^2 = 1.1025 + 0.9025 = 2.005 \approx 2$, so the exact value $\sin(2.005) - \sin(2) \approx -0.00122$ is indeed exceptionally close to $0$).
Ex 18 Easy Linear ApproximationTotal Differential
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 18: Linear Approximation of a Radial Hypotenuse Increment

🎯 Standard KFUPM Exam 2 problem: linearizing a distance/hypotenuse function near the integer point (4, 3).
Problem Statement
Find an appropriate function $z = f(x, y)$ and use the total differential to approximate the quantity: $$\sqrt{(4.03)^2 + (3.1)^2} - \sqrt{4^2 + 3^2}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{dz = 0.084 = \frac{21}{250}}$$
**Step 1: Choose the function $z = f(x, y)$ and identify base values.** Let $f(x, y) = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$. The base point with convenient exact values is $(x_0, y_0) = (4, 3)$, which corresponds to the standard $3\text{-}4\text{-}5$ right triangle: $$f(4, 3) = \sqrt{4^2 + 3^2} = \sqrt{16 + 9} = \sqrt{25} = 5$$ **Step 2: Find the differentials $dx$ and $dy$.** $$dx = \Delta x = 4.03 - 4 = 0.03$$ $$dy = \Delta y = 3.1 - 3 = 0.10$$ **Step 3: Calculate the partial derivatives at $(4, 3)$.** $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}} \implies f_x(4, 3) = \frac{4}{\sqrt{4^2 + 3^2}} = \frac{4}{5} = 0.8$$ $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{y}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}} \implies f_y(4, 3) = \frac{3}{\sqrt{4^2 + 3^2}} = \frac{3}{5} = 0.6$$ **Step 4: Compute the total differential $dz$.** $$dz = f_x(4, 3)\,dx + f_y(4, 3)\,dy = \left(\frac{4}{5}\right)(0.03) + \left(\frac{3}{5}\right)(0.10)$$ $$dz = 0.8(0.03) + 0.6(0.10) = 0.024 + 0.060 = 0.084 = \frac{21}{250}$$ *(Exact check: $\sqrt{(4.03)^2 + (3.1)^2} = \sqrt{25.8509} \approx 5.084378$, so $\Delta z \approx 0.084378$, matching $dz = 0.084$ to three decimal places).*
Ex 21 Easy Geometric InterpretationTotal Differential
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 21: Geometric Interpretation of Area Increment and Total Differential

🎯 KFUPM conceptual test favorite: identifying terms in Delta A = dA + Delta l Delta h geometrically.
Problem Statement
The area of a rectangle is $A = lh$. The possible errors in measuring the length and height are $\Delta l$ and $\Delta h$, respectively. Find $dA$ and identify the geometric regions whose areas are given by the terms of $dA$. What region represents the difference between $\Delta A$ and $dA$?
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{dA = h\,\Delta l + l\,\Delta h}, \quad \boxed{\Delta A - dA = \Delta l\,\Delta h}$$
**Step 1: Compute the total differential $dA$.** For $A(l, h) = lh$: $$dA = \frac{\partial A}{\partial l}\,\Delta l + \frac{\partial A}{\partial h}\,\Delta h = h\,\Delta l + l\,\Delta h$$ **Step 2: Compute the true increment $\Delta A$.** When the dimensions increase from $l$ to $l + \Delta l$ and from $h$ to $h + \Delta h$, the expanded area is: $$\Delta A = (l + \Delta l)(h + \Delta h) - lh = lh + l\,\Delta h + h\,\Delta l + \Delta l\,\Delta h - lh$$ $$\Delta A = h\,\Delta l + l\,\Delta h + \Delta l\,\Delta h$$ **Step 3: Geometric identification of each term.** 1. **The term $h\,\Delta l$:** Represents the area of the vertical rectangle of width $\Delta l$ and height $h$ attached to the right side of the original rectangle. 2. **The term $l\,\Delta h$:** Represents the area of the horizontal rectangle of length $l$ and height $\Delta h$ attached to the top of the original rectangle. 3. **The difference $\Delta A - dA = \Delta l\,\Delta h$:** Represents the small corner rectangle in the upper-right corner of width $\Delta l$ and height $\Delta h$, which accounts for the second-order cross-error.
Ex 22 Medium Geometric InterpretationCylinder Volume
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 22: Geometric Interpretation of Cylinder Volume Increment and Differential

🎯 Tests geometric understanding of multivariable differentials dV vs increment Delta V on KFUPM exams.
Problem Statement
The volume of a right circular cylinder is $V = \pi r^2 h$. The possible errors in measuring the radius and height are $\Delta r$ and $\Delta h$, respectively. Find $dV$ and identify the geometric solids whose volumes are given by the terms of $dV$. What solid represents the difference between $\Delta V$ and $dV$?
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{dV = 2\pi rh\,\Delta r + \pi r^2\,\Delta h}, \quad \boxed{\Delta V - dV = 2\pi r\,\Delta r\,\Delta h + \pi h(\Delta r)^2 + \pi (\Delta r)^2\,\Delta h}$$
**Step 1: Compute the total differential $dV$.** Differentiating $V(r, h) = \pi r^2 h$: $$\frac{\partial V}{\partial r} = 2\pi rh, \qquad \frac{\partial V}{\partial h} = \pi r^2$$ $$dV = 2\pi rh\,\Delta r + \pi r^2\,\Delta h$$ **Step 2: Expand the exact increment $\Delta V$.** $$\Delta V = \pi (r + \Delta r)^2 (h + \Delta h) - \pi r^2 h$$ $$= \pi\left(r^2 + 2r\Delta r + (\Delta r)^2\right)(h + \Delta h) - \pi r^2 h$$ $$= \pi r^2 h + 2\pi rh\,\Delta r + \pi h(\Delta r)^2 + \pi r^2\,\Delta h + 2\pi r\,\Delta r\,\Delta h + \pi (\Delta r)^2\,\Delta h - \pi r^2 h$$ $$= \underbrace{2\pi rh\,\Delta r + \pi r^2\,\Delta h}_{dV} + \underbrace{\left[ 2\pi r\,\Delta r\,\Delta h + \pi h(\Delta r)^2 + \pi (\Delta r)^2\,\Delta h \right]}_{\text{Difference } \Delta V - dV}$$ **Step 3: Geometric identification of solids.** 1. **The term $2\pi rh\,\Delta r$:** The volume of the cylindrical shell of radius $r$, height $h$, and wall thickness $\Delta r$ wrapping around the lateral surface. 2. **The term $\pi r^2\,\Delta h$:** The volume of the circular disk of radius $r$ and thickness $\Delta h$ added directly onto the top base. 3. **The difference $\Delta V - dV$:** The volume of the outer collar and rim solids surrounding the top edge, consisting of the annular ring atop the lateral shell of area $\pi((r+\Delta r)^2 - r^2)$ and height $\Delta h$, along with the curvature correction $\pi h(\Delta r)^2$.
Ex 23 Easy Propagated ErrorRelative Error
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 23: Propagated and Relative Error in the Volume of a Rectangular Box

🎯 Major Exam 2 staple: computing propagated error dV and relative percentage error dV/V for 3D boxes.
Problem Statement
The possible error involved in measuring each dimension of a rectangular box is $\pm 0.02\text{ inch}$. The dimensions of the box are $8\text{ inches}$ by $5\text{ inches}$ by $12\text{ inches}$. Approximate the propagated error and the relative error in the calculated volume of the box.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{Propagated Error: } \boxed{dV = \pm 3.92\text{ in.}^3}; \quad \text{Relative Error: } \boxed{\frac{dV}{V} \approx \pm 0.008167 \approx \pm 0.82\%}$$
**Step 1: Set up the volume formula and identify given measurements.** The volume of a rectangular box with dimensions $x, y, z$ is: $$V = xyz$$ Given dimensions: $$x = 8\text{ in.}, \quad y = 5\text{ in.}, \quad z = 12\text{ in.}$$ Given measurement errors: $$|dx| \le 0.02, \quad |dy| \le 0.02, \quad |dz| \le 0.02 \implies dx = dy = dz = \pm 0.02\text{ in.}$$ **Step 2: Approximate the propagated error using the total differential $dV$.** $$dV = \frac{\partial V}{\partial x}\,dx + \frac{\partial V}{\partial y}\,dy + \frac{\partial V}{\partial z}\,dz = yz\,dx + xz\,dy + xy\,dz$$ Substitute the given numerical values: $$dV = (5)(12)(\pm 0.02) + (8)(12)(\pm 0.02) + (8)(5)(\pm 0.02)$$ $$dV = \left(60 + 96 + 40\right)(\pm 0.02) = 196(\pm 0.02) = \pm 3.92\text{ in.}^3$$ **Step 3: Calculate the measured volume.** $$V = (8)(5)(12) = 480\text{ in.}^3$$ **Step 4: Compute the relative error and percentage error.** $$\frac{\Delta V}{V} \approx \frac{dV}{V} = \frac{\pm 3.92}{480} = \pm\frac{49}{6000} \approx \pm 0.008167$$ Multiplying by $100\%$ gives the percent error: $$\text{Percent Error} \approx \pm 0.82\%$$
Ex 24 Medium Propagated ErrorRelative Error
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 24: Propagated and Relative Error in the Volume of a Cylinder

🎯 Standard KFUPM Exam 2 problem: evaluating differential propagated error for a cylinder where the radius exponent doubles its error contribution.
Problem Statement
The possible error involved in measuring each dimension of a right circular cylinder is $\pm 0.05\text{ centimeter}$. The radius is $3\text{ centimeters}$ and the height is $10\text{ centimeters}$. Approximate the propagated error and the relative error in the calculated volume of the cylinder.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{Propagated Error: } \boxed{dV = \pm 3.45\pi\text{ cm}^3 \approx \pm 10.84\text{ cm}^3}; \quad \text{Relative Error: } \boxed{\frac{dV}{V} = \pm\frac{23}{600} \approx \pm 0.0383 \approx \pm 3.83\%}$$
**Step 1: State the cylinder volume formula and known values.** The volume of a right circular cylinder is: $$V = \pi r^2 h$$ Measured values: $$r = 3\text{ cm}, \quad h = 10\text{ cm}$$ Measurement errors: $$dr = \pm 0.05\text{ cm}, \quad dh = \pm 0.05\text{ cm}$$ **Step 2: Find the total differential $dV$ (propagated error).** $$dV = \frac{\partial V}{\partial r}\,dr + \frac{\partial V}{\partial h}\,dh = 2\pi rh\,dr + \pi r^2\,dh$$ Substitute $r = 3$ and $h = 10$: $$dV = 2\pi(3)(10)(\pm 0.05) + \pi(3)^2(\pm 0.05)$$ $$dV = 60\pi(\pm 0.05) + 9\pi(\pm 0.05) = 69\pi(\pm 0.05) = \pm 3.45\pi\text{ cm}^3$$ Numerically: $$dV \approx \pm 3.45(3.14159) \approx \pm 10.8385\text{ cm}^3 \approx \pm 10.84\text{ cm}^3$$ **Step 3: Compute the measured volume.** $$V = \pi(3)^2(10) = 90\pi\text{ cm}^3 \approx 282.74\text{ cm}^3$$ **Step 4: Determine the relative error and percentage error.** $$\frac{\Delta V}{V} \approx \frac{dV}{V} = \frac{\pm 3.45\pi}{90\pi} = \frac{\pm 3.45}{90} = \pm\frac{23}{600} \approx \pm 0.03833$$ Expressing as a percentage: $$\text{Percent Error} \approx \pm 3.83\%$$ *(Notice how $\frac{dV}{V} = 2\frac{dr}{r} + \frac{dh}{h} = 2\left(\frac{\pm 0.05}{3}\right) + \frac{\pm 0.05}{10} = \pm\left(\frac{1}{30} + \frac{1}{200}\right) = \pm\frac{23}{600}$, showing how logarithmic differentiation provides an immediate check!)*
Ex 28 Medium Parallel ResistorsTotal Differential
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 28: Approximate Change in Total Resistance of Parallel Resistors

🎯 Classic KFUPM exam problem testing implicit multivariable differentiation and differentials on reciprocal parallel resistance formulas.
Problem Statement
The total resistance $R$ (in ohms) of two resistors connected in parallel is given by: $$\frac{1}{R} = \frac{1}{R_1} + \frac{1}{R_2}$$ Approximate the change in $R$ as $R_1$ is increased from $10\text{ ohms}$ to $10.5\text{ ohms}$ and $R_2$ is decreased from $15\text{ ohms}$ to $13\text{ ohms}$.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\Delta R \approx dR = -0.14\ \Omega}$$
**Step 1: Express $R$ or its differential.** We are given the parallel resistance law: $$\frac{1}{R} = \frac{1}{R_1} + \frac{1}{R_2} \implies R = \frac{R_1 R_2}{R_1 + R_2}$$ Initial resistor values: $$R_1 = 10\ \Omega, \qquad R_2 = 15\ \Omega$$ Changes in resistance: $$\Delta R_1 = 10.5 - 10 = +0.5\ \Omega$$ $$\Delta R_2 = 13 - 15 = -2.0\ \Omega$$ **Step 2: Differentiate with respect to each resistance.** Differentiating $\frac{1}{R} = \frac{1}{R_1} + \frac{1}{R_2}$ implicitly: $$-\frac{1}{R^2}\,dR = -\frac{1}{R_1^2}\,dR_1 - \frac{1}{R_2^2}\,dR_2$$ Multiplying by $-R^2$ gives the total differential: $$dR = \left(\frac{R}{R_1}\right)^2 dR_1 + \left(\frac{R}{R_2}\right)^2 dR_2$$ Equivalently, substituting $R = \frac{R_1 R_2}{R_1 + R_2}$: $$\frac{\partial R}{\partial R_1} = \left(\frac{R_2}{R_1 + R_2}\right)^2, \qquad \frac{\partial R}{\partial R_2} = \left(\frac{R_1}{R_1 + R_2}\right)^2$$ **Step 3: Evaluate partial derivatives at $R_1 = 10, R_2 = 15$.** $$R_1 + R_2 = 10 + 15 = 25$$ $$\frac{\partial R}{\partial R_1} = \left(\frac{15}{25}\right)^2 = \left(\frac{3}{5}\right)^2 = \frac{9}{25} = 0.36$$ $$\frac{\partial R}{\partial R_2} = \left(\frac{10}{25}\right)^2 = \left(\frac{2}{5}\right)^2 = \frac{4}{25} = 0.16$$ **Step 4: Compute the approximate change $dR$.** $$\Delta R \approx dR = \frac{\partial R}{\partial R_1}\,\Delta R_1 + \frac{\partial R}{\partial R_2}\,\Delta R_2$$ $$dR = (0.36)(+0.5) + (0.16)(-2.0)$$ $$dR = 0.18 - 0.32 = -0.14\ \Omega$$ **Conclusion:** The total resistance decreases by approximately $0.14\ \Omega$.
Ex 36 Medium Definition of DifferentiabilityEpsilon Increments
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 36: Rigorous Proof of Differentiability via Epsilon Increment Definition

🎯 Directly aligned with KFUPM theoretical exam questions testing the precise epsilon-increment definition of differentiability.
Problem Statement
Show that the function $f(x, y) = x^2 + y^2$ is differentiable by finding values of $\varepsilon_1$ and $\varepsilon_2$ as designated in the definition of differentiability, and verify that both $\varepsilon_1 \to 0$ and $\varepsilon_2 \to 0$ as $(\Delta x, \Delta y) \to (0, 0)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\varepsilon_1 = \Delta x, \quad \varepsilon_2 = \Delta y}; \quad \lim_{(\Delta x, \Delta y) \to (0, 0)} \varepsilon_1 = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad \lim_{(\Delta x, \Delta y) \to (0, 0)} \varepsilon_2 = 0$$
**Step 1: State the formal definition of differentiability.** A function $z = f(x, y)$ is differentiable at $(x, y)$ if the total increment $\Delta z$ can be written in the form: $$\Delta z = f_x(x, y)\,\Delta x + f_y(x, y)\,\Delta y + \varepsilon_1\,\Delta x + \varepsilon_2\,\Delta y$$ where $\varepsilon_1 \to 0$ and $\varepsilon_2 \to 0$ as $(\Delta x, \Delta y) \to (0, 0)$. **Step 2: Calculate the partial derivatives of $f(x, y) = x^2 + y^2$.** $$f_x(x, y) = 2x, \qquad f_y(x, y) = 2y$$ **Step 3: Expand the increment $\Delta z$.** $$\Delta z = f(x + \Delta x, y + \Delta y) - f(x, y)$$ $$= (x + \Delta x)^2 + (y + \Delta y)^2 - (x^2 + y^2)$$ $$= x^2 + 2x\,\Delta x + (\Delta x)^2 + y^2 + 2y\,\Delta y + (\Delta y)^2 - x^2 - y^2$$ $$= 2x\,\Delta x + 2y\,\Delta y + (\Delta x)^2 + (\Delta y)^2$$ **Step 4: Group into the required form and extract $\varepsilon_1, \varepsilon_2$.** $$\Delta z = \underbrace{2x}_{f_x(x, y)}\Delta x + \underbrace{2y}_{f_y(x, y)}\Delta y + (\Delta x)\Delta x + (\Delta y)\Delta y$$ Comparing with the definition: $$\varepsilon_1 = \Delta x, \qquad \varepsilon_2 = \Delta y$$ **Step 5: Verify the limits.** $$\lim_{(\Delta x, \Delta y) \to (0, 0)} \varepsilon_1 = \lim_{(\Delta x, \Delta y) \to (0, 0)} \Delta x = 0$$ $$\lim_{(\Delta x, \Delta y) \to (0, 0)} \varepsilon_2 = \lim_{(\Delta x, \Delta y) \to (0, 0)} \Delta y = 0$$ Since both limits equal zero, $f(x, y) = x^2 + y^2$ is differentiable at every point $(x, y) \in \mathbb{R}^2$.
Ex 39 Hard Differentiability vs ContinuityCounterexample
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 39: Existence of Partial Derivatives Versus Non-Differentiability (Discontinuity)

🎯 Major KFUPM conceptual trap: proving that existence of both partial derivatives does NOT imply differentiability, utilizing the Two-Path Test to establish discontinuity.
Problem Statement
For the piecewise function: $$f(x, y) = \begin{cases} \frac{3x^2 y}{x^4 + y^2}, & (x, y) \neq (0, 0) \\[6pt] 0, & (x, y) = (0, 0) \end{cases}$$ show that $f_x(0, 0)$ and $f_y(0, 0)$ both exist, but that $f$ is not differentiable at $(0, 0)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{f_x(0, 0) = 0, \quad f_y(0, 0) = 0}; \quad \text{Because } \lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} f(x, y) \text{ does not exist, } f \text{ is not continuous at } (0, 0) \text{ and therefore not differentiable at } (0, 0).$$
**Step 1: Compute $f_x(0, 0)$ and $f_y(0, 0)$ using the limit definition.** Along the $x$-axis, $y = 0$, so $f(\Delta x, 0) = \frac{3(\Delta x)^2(0)}{(\Delta x)^4 + 0} = 0$ for $\Delta x \neq 0$: $$f_x(0, 0) = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{f(\Delta x, 0) - f(0, 0)}{\Delta x} = \lim_{\Delta x \to 0} \frac{0 - 0}{\Delta x} = 0$$ Along the $y$-axis, $x = 0$, so $f(0, \Delta y) = \frac{0}{0 + (\Delta y)^2} = 0$ for $\Delta y \neq 0$: $$f_y(0, 0) = \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{f(0, \Delta y) - f(0, 0)}{\Delta y} = \lim_{\Delta y \to 0} \frac{0 - 0}{\Delta y} = 0$$ Both partial derivatives exist and equal $0$. **Step 2: Test the continuity of $f$ at $(0, 0)$ using the Two-Path Test.** 1. **Approach along lines $y = mx$:** $$\lim_{x \to 0} f(x, mx) = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{3x^2(mx)}{x^4 + (mx)^2} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{3mx^3}{x^2(x^2 + m^2)} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{3mx}{x^2 + m^2} = \frac{0}{m^2} = 0$$ Along every straight line through the origin, $f(x, y) \to 0$. 2. **Approach along the parabola $y = x^2$ (degree balancing):** In the denominator, $x^4 + y^2$ has degree 4 in $x$ and degree 2 in $y$. Choosing $y = x^2$ balances both terms to degree 4: $$\lim_{x \to 0} f(x, x^2) = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{3x^2(x^2)}{x^4 + (x^2)^2} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{3x^4}{x^4 + x^4} = \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{3x^4}{2x^4} = \frac{3}{2}$$ Since the limits along different paths do not agree ($0 \neq \frac{3}{2}$): $$\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} f(x, y) \quad \text{does not exist (DNE)}$$ **Step 3: Conclude non-differentiability via Theorem 13.5.** Theorem 13.5 (Differentiability Implies Continuity) states: *If $f$ is differentiable at $(x_0, y_0)$, then $f$ must be continuous at $(x_0, y_0)$.* By contraposition: *If $f$ is discontinuous at $(x_0, y_0)$, then $f$ is NOT differentiable at $(x_0, y_0)$.* Because $\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} f(x, y)$ DNE, $f$ is discontinuous at $(0, 0)$, and hence $f$ is **not differentiable** at $(0, 0)$, despite both partial derivatives existing.

13.5 The Multivariable Chain Rule
