Jump to problem:
Ex 4 Easy Chain Rule Case 1Parametric Curves
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 4: Multivariable Chain Rule with One Independent Parameter

🎯 Standard KFUPM Major Exam 2 problem: evaluating dw/dt = (dw/dx)(dx/dt) + (dw/dy)(dy/dt) along a parametric curve.
Problem Statement
Use the appropriate Chain Rule to find $\frac{dw}{dt}$ and evaluate it at the given value of $t$: $$w = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}, \quad x = \cos t, \quad y = e^t; \quad t = 0$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\left.\frac{dw}{dt}\right|_{t=0} = \boxed{\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}}$$
**Step 1: Apply Chain Rule Case 1.** Here $w = f(x, y)$ depends on two intermediate variables $x$ and $y$, each of which is a function of a single independent variable $t$. The Chain Rule gives: $$\frac{dw}{dt} = \frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\frac{dx}{dt} + \frac{\partial w}{\partial y}\frac{dy}{dt}$$ **Step 2: Compute the partial derivatives of $w$.** $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[(x^2 + y^2)^{1/2}\right] = \frac{1}{2}(x^2 + y^2)^{-1/2}(2x) = \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}}$$ $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[(x^2 + y^2)^{1/2}\right] = \frac{1}{2}(x^2 + y^2)^{-1/2}(2y) = \frac{y}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}}$$ **Step 3: Compute the derivatives of $x$ and $y$ with respect to $t$.** $$\frac{dx}{dt} = \frac{d}{dt}[\cos t] = -\sin t$$ $$\frac{dy}{dt} = \frac{d}{dt}[e^t] = e^t$$ **Step 4: Substitute into the Chain Rule formula.** $$\frac{dw}{dt} = \left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}}\right)(-\sin t) + \left(\frac{y}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}}\right)(e^t) = \frac{-x\sin t + y e^t}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}}$$ **Step 5: Evaluate at $t = 0$.** At $t = 0$: $$x(0) = \cos(0) = 1, \qquad y(0) = e^0 = 1$$ Substitute $t = 0, x = 1, y = 1$: $$\left.\frac{dw}{dt}\right|_{t=0} = \frac{-(1)\sin(0) + (1)e^0}{\sqrt{1^2 + 1^2}} = \frac{0 + 1}{\sqrt{2}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} = \frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}$$
Ex 9 Easy Chain RuleThree Variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 9: Chain Rule for a Function of Three Variables and Comparison with Direct Substitution

🎯 Common KFUPM problem testing both the Multivariable Chain Rule and parametric substitution verification.
Problem Statement
Find $\frac{dw}{dt}$ (a) by using the appropriate Chain Rule and (b) by converting $w$ to a function of $t$ before differentiating: $$w = x^2 + y^2 + z^2, \quad x = \cos t, \quad y = \sin t, \quad z = e^t$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\frac{dw}{dt} = 2e^{2t}}$$
**Part (a): Find $\frac{dw}{dt}$ using the Chain Rule.** 1. Write out the Chain Rule for three intermediate variables: $$\frac{dw}{dt} = \frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\frac{dx}{dt} + \frac{\partial w}{\partial y}\frac{dy}{dt} + \frac{\partial w}{\partial z}\frac{dz}{dt}$$ 2. Compute the partial derivatives of $w = x^2 + y^2 + z^2$: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = 2x, \qquad \frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = 2y, \qquad \frac{\partial w}{\partial z} = 2z$$ 3. Compute the derivatives of the intermediate variables: $$\frac{dx}{dt} = -\sin t, \qquad \frac{dy}{dt} = \cos t, \qquad \frac{dz}{dt} = e^t$$ 4. Substitute and simplify: $$\frac{dw}{dt} = (2x)(-\sin t) + (2y)(\cos t) + (2z)(e^t)$$ $$= 2(\cos t)(-\sin t) + 2(\sin t)(\cos t) + 2(e^t)(e^t)$$ $$= -2\sin t\cos t + 2\sin t\cos t + 2e^{2t} = 2e^{2t}$$ **Part (b): Find $\frac{dw}{dt}$ by direct substitution.** 1. Express $w$ directly as a single-variable function of $t$: $$w(t) = (\cos t)^2 + (\sin t)^2 + (e^t)^2$$ Using the fundamental Pythagorean identity $\cos^2 t + \sin^2 t = 1$: $$w(t) = 1 + e^{2t}$$ 2. Differentiate with respect to $t$: $$\frac{dw}{dt} = \frac{d}{dt}\left[1 + e^{2t}\right] = 2e^{2t}$$ Both methods confirm the identical derivative: $\frac{dw}{dt} = 2e^{2t}$.
Ex 13 Hard Related RatesDistance Function
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 13: Rate of Change of Distance Between Two Moving Objects

🎯 Challenging KFUPM application problem applying multivariable Chain Rule to geometric distances between moving particles.
Problem Statement
The parametric equations for the paths of two objects are given by: $$\text{First object: } x_1 = 10\cos 2t, \; y_1 = 6\sin 2t$$ $$\text{Second object: } x_2 = 7\cos t, \; y_2 = 4\sin t$$ At what rate is the distance between the two objects changing at $t = \pi/2$?
Boxed Final Answer
$$\left.\frac{dD}{dt}\right|_{t=\pi/2} = \boxed{-\frac{11}{\sqrt{29}} = -\frac{11\sqrt{29}}{29} \approx -2.04}$$
**Step 1: Define the distance function $D(t)$.** The Euclidean distance between $(x_1, y_1)$ and $(x_2, y_2)$ is: $$D(t) = \sqrt{(x_1 - x_2)^2 + (y_1 - y_2)^2}$$ **Step 2: Differentiate with respect to $t$ using the Chain Rule.** $$\frac{dD}{dt} = \frac{(x_1 - x_2)\left(\frac{dx_1}{dt} - \frac{dx_2}{dt}\right) + (y_1 - y_2)\left(\frac{dy_1}{dt} - \frac{dy_2}{dt}\right)}{\sqrt{(x_1 - x_2)^2 + (y_1 - y_2)^2}}$$ **Step 3: Evaluate coordinates and velocities at $t = \pi/2$.** 1. For the first object at $t = \pi/2$ (so $2t = \pi$): $$x_1 = 10\cos(\pi) = -10, \qquad y_1 = 6\sin(\pi) = 0$$ $$\frac{dx_1}{dt} = -20\sin(2t) \implies \left.\frac{dx_1}{dt}\right|_{t=\pi/2} = -20\sin(\pi) = 0$$ $$\frac{dy_1}{dt} = 12\cos(2t) \implies \left.\frac{dy_1}{dt}\right|_{t=\pi/2} = 12\cos(\pi) = -12$$ 2. For the second object at $t = \pi/2$: $$x_2 = 7\cos(\pi/2) = 0, \qquad y_2 = 4\sin(\pi/2) = 4$$ $$\frac{dx_2}{dt} = -7\sin t \implies \left.\frac{dx_2}{dt}\right|_{t=\pi/2} = -7\sin(\pi/2) = -7$$ $$\frac{dy_2}{dt} = 4\cos t \implies \left.\frac{dy_2}{dt}\right|_{t=\pi/2} = 4\cos(\pi/2) = 0$$ **Step 4: Compute the position differences and distance.** $$x_1 - x_2 = -10 - 0 = -10$$ $$y_1 - y_2 = 0 - 4 = -4$$ $$D\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = \sqrt{(-10)^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{100 + 16} = \sqrt{116} = 2\sqrt{29}$$ **Step 5: Compute relative velocity components.** $$\frac{dx_1}{dt} - \frac{dx_2}{dt} = 0 - (-7) = 7$$ $$\frac{dy_1}{dt} - \frac{dy_2}{dt} = -12 - 0 = -12$$ **Step 6: Calculate $\frac{dD}{dt}$.** $$\left.\frac{dD}{dt}\right|_{t=\pi/2} = \frac{(-10)(7) + (-4)(-12)}{2\sqrt{29}} = \frac{-70 + 48}{2\sqrt{29}} = \frac{-22}{2\sqrt{29}} = -\frac{11}{\sqrt{29}} = -\frac{11\sqrt{29}}{29} \approx -2.04$$ The distance is decreasing at approximately $2.04$ units per second.
Ex 18 Medium Chain Rule Case 2Polar Coordinates
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 18: Multivariable Chain Rule with Polar Coordinates Substitution

🎯 High-frequency KFUPM Exam 2 problem: transforming to polar coordinates (s, t) = (r, theta) and computing partial derivatives.
Problem Statement
Find $\frac{\partial w}{\partial s}$ and $\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}$ using the appropriate Chain Rule, and evaluate each partial derivative at $s = 3, t = \pi/4$: $$w = x^2 - y^2, \quad x = s\cos t, \quad y = s\sin t$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\left.\frac{\partial w}{\partial s}\right|_{(3, \pi/4)} = \boxed{0}, \qquad \left.\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}\right|_{(3, \pi/4)} = \boxed{-18}$$
**Step 1: Set up the Chain Rule for two independent variables.** Here $w = f(x, y)$ where $x = g(s, t)$ and $y = h(s, t)$: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial s} = \frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\frac{\partial x}{\partial s} + \frac{\partial w}{\partial y}\frac{\partial y}{\partial s}$$ $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial t} = \frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\frac{\partial x}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial w}{\partial y}\frac{\partial y}{\partial t}$$ **Step 2: Calculate the required partial derivatives.** $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = 2x, \qquad \frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = -2y$$ $$\frac{\partial x}{\partial s} = \cos t, \qquad \frac{\partial x}{\partial t} = -s\sin t$$ $$\frac{\partial y}{\partial s} = \sin t, \qquad \frac{\partial y}{\partial t} = s\cos t$$ **Step 3: Assemble $\frac{\partial w}{\partial s}$ and simplify.** $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial s} = (2x)(\cos t) + (-2y)(\sin t) = 2(s\cos t)\cos t - 2(s\sin t)\sin t$$ $$= 2s\left(\cos^2 t - \sin^2 t\right) = 2s\cos(2t)$$ **Step 4: Assemble $\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}$ and simplify.** $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial t} = (2x)(-s\sin t) + (-2y)(s\cos t) = 2(s\cos t)(-s\sin t) - 2(s\sin t)(s\cos t)$$ $$= -4s^2 \sin t\cos t = -2s^2 \sin(2t)$$ **Step 5: Evaluate at $s = 3$ and $t = \pi/4$.** Notice that $2t = 2\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = \frac{\pi}{2}$: $$\left.\frac{\partial w}{\partial s}\right|_{(3, \pi/4)} = 2(3)\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 6(0) = 0$$ $$\left.\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}\right|_{(3, \pi/4)} = -2(3)^2\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = -18(1) = -18$$
Ex 22 Hard Chain RuleThree Intermediate Variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 22: Chain Rule with Three Intermediate and Two Independent Variables

🎯 Advanced KFUPM exam problem testing tree diagrams with 3 intermediate branches (x, y, z) leading to 2 independent parameters (s, t).
Problem Statement
Find $\frac{\partial w}{\partial s}$ and $\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}$ (a) by using the appropriate Chain Rule and (b) by converting $w$ to a function of $s$ and $t$ before differentiating: $$w = x\cos(yz), \quad x = s^2, \quad y = t^2, \quad z = s - 2t$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\frac{\partial w}{\partial s} = 2s\cos(st^2 - 2t^3) - s^2 t^2 \sin(st^2 - 2t^3)}, \quad \boxed{\frac{\partial w}{\partial t} = (6s^2 t^2 - 2s^3 t)\sin(st^2 - 2t^3)}$$
**Part (a): Find partial derivatives using the Chain Rule.** 1. Tree diagram structure: $w \to (x, y, z) \to (s, t)$. The Chain Rule formulas are: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial s} = \frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\frac{\partial x}{\partial s} + \frac{\partial w}{\partial y}\frac{\partial y}{\partial s} + \frac{\partial w}{\partial z}\frac{\partial z}{\partial s}$$ $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial t} = \frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\frac{\partial x}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial w}{\partial y}\frac{\partial y}{\partial t} + \frac{\partial w}{\partial z}\frac{\partial z}{\partial t}$$ 2. Compute partial derivatives of $w$: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = \cos(yz), \qquad \frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = -xz\sin(yz), \qquad \frac{\partial w}{\partial z} = -xy\sin(yz)$$ 3. Compute partial derivatives of $x, y, z$: $$\frac{\partial x}{\partial s} = 2s, \quad \frac{\partial x}{\partial t} = 0; \qquad \frac{\partial y}{\partial s} = 0, \quad \frac{\partial y}{\partial t} = 2t; \qquad \frac{\partial z}{\partial s} = 1, \quad \frac{\partial z}{\partial t} = -2$$ 4. Substitute into $\frac{\partial w}{\partial s}$: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial s} = \cos(yz)(2s) + (-xz\sin(yz))(0) + (-xy\sin(yz))(1) = 2s\cos(yz) - xy\sin(yz)$$ Substitute $x = s^2, y = t^2, z = s - 2t$ (so $yz = t^2(s - 2t) = st^2 - 2t^3$ and $xy = s^2 t^2$): $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial s} = 2s\cos(st^2 - 2t^3) - s^2 t^2 \sin(st^2 - 2t^3)$$ 5. Substitute into $\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}$: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial t} = \cos(yz)(0) + (-xz\sin(yz))(2t) + (-xy\sin(yz))(-2)$$ $$= -2txz\sin(yz) + 2xy\sin(yz) = 2x(y - tz)\sin(yz)$$ Notice $y - tz = t^2 - t(s - 2t) = t^2 - st + 2t^2 = 3t^2 - st$. Thus: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial t} = 2(s^2)(3t^2 - st)\sin(st^2 - 2t^3) = (6s^2 t^2 - 2s^3 t)\sin(st^2 - 2t^3)$$ **Part (b): Find partial derivatives by direct conversion to $s$ and $t$.** 1. Substitute $x, y, z$ directly into $w$: $$w(s, t) = s^2 \cos\left(t^2(s - 2t)\right) = s^2 \cos(st^2 - 2t^3)$$ 2. Differentiate with respect to $s$ via the product rule: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial s} = \frac{\partial}{\partial s}[s^2]\cos(st^2 - 2t^3) + s^2\frac{\partial}{\partial s}[\cos(st^2 - 2t^3)]$$ $$= 2s\cos(st^2 - 2t^3) + s^2\left[-\sin(st^2 - 2t^3) \cdot t^2\right] = 2s\cos(st^2 - 2t^3) - s^2 t^2 \sin(st^2 - 2t^3)$$ 3. Differentiate with respect to $t$: $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial t} = s^2\left[-\sin(st^2 - 2t^3) \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial t}(st^2 - 2t^3)\right] = -s^2(2st - 6t^2)\sin(st^2 - 2t^3)$$ $$= (6s^2 t^2 - 2s^3 t)\sin(st^2 - 2t^3)$$ Both methods produce identical results.
Ex 23 Easy Implicit DifferentiationImplicit Function Theorem
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 23: Implicit Differentiation of a Second-Degree Curve via Partial Derivatives

🎯 Standard KFUPM Exam 2 formula: dy/dx = -F_x / F_y replaces traditional tedious implicit differentiation.
Problem Statement
Differentiate implicitly to find $\frac{dy}{dx}$ for the equation: $$x^2 - xy + y^2 - x + y = 0$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{2x - y - 1}{2y - x + 1} = \frac{1 + y - 2x}{2y - x + 1}}$$
**Step 1: Define the multivariable function $F(x, y) = 0$.** Let: $$F(x, y) = x^2 - xy + y^2 - x + y$$ **Step 2: State the Implicit Differentiation Theorem formula.** By Theorem 13.8 (Implicit Function Theorem): $$\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{F_x(x, y)}{F_y(x, y)}, \qquad \text{provided } F_y(x, y) \neq 0$$ **Step 3: Compute the partial derivatives $F_x$ and $F_y$.** $$F_x = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[x^2 - xy + y^2 - x + y\right] = 2x - y - 1$$ $$F_y = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[x^2 - xy + y^2 - x + y\right] = -x + 2y + 1$$ **Step 4: Substitute into the theorem formula.** $$\frac{dy}{dx} = -\frac{2x - y - 1}{-x + 2y + 1} = \frac{-(2x - y - 1)}{2y - x + 1} = \frac{1 + y - 2x}{2y - x + 1}$$
Ex 31 Medium Implicit Partial DifferentiationTrigonometric Functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 31: Implicit Partial Differentiation with Trigonometric Functions

🎯 High-yield KFUPM question testing dz/dx = -F_x/F_z and dz/dy = -F_y/F_z with trigonometric arguments.
Problem Statement
Differentiate implicitly to find the first partial derivatives $\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}$ and $\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}$ for: $$\tan(x + y) + \cos z = 2$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{\sec^2(x + y)}{\sin z} = \sec^2(x+y)\csc z}, \qquad \boxed{\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = \frac{\sec^2(x + y)}{\sin z} = \sec^2(x+y)\csc z}$$
**Step 1: Set up the implicit function $F(x, y, z) = 0$.** Define: $$F(x, y, z) = \tan(x + y) + \cos z - 2 = 0$$ **Step 2: State the implicit partial derivative formulas.** By Theorem 13.8: $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = -\frac{F_x}{F_z}, \qquad \frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = -\frac{F_y}{F_z}$$ **Step 3: Compute the first-order partial derivatives of $F$.** $$F_x = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[\tan(x + y)] = \sec^2(x + y)$$ $$F_y = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[\tan(x + y)] = \sec^2(x + y)$$ $$F_z = \frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\cos z] = -\sin z$$ **Step 4: Compute $\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}$ and $\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}$.** $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = -\frac{F_x}{F_z} = -\frac{\sec^2(x + y)}{-\sin z} = \frac{\sec^2(x + y)}{\sin z} = \sec^2(x + y)\csc z$$ $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = -\frac{F_y}{F_z} = -\frac{\sec^2(x + y)}{-\sin z} = \frac{\sec^2(x + y)}{\sin z} = \sec^2(x + y)\csc z$$ *(Notice that because $x$ and $y$ enter symmetrically into $x + y$, their partial derivatives are identical).*
Ex 34 Medium Implicit Partial DifferentiationLogarithmic Functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 34: Implicit Partial Differentiation Involving Logarithmic and Polynomial Terms

🎯 Common KFUPM problem testing partial implicit differentiation where algebraic denominators must be simplified.
Problem Statement
Differentiate implicitly to find the first partial derivatives $\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}$ and $\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}$ for: $$x\ln y + y^2 z + z^2 = 8$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = -\frac{\ln y}{y^2 + 2z}}, \qquad \boxed{\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = -\frac{x + 2y^2 z}{y(y^2 + 2z)}}$$
**Step 1: Define the function $F(x, y, z) = 0$.** Let: $$F(x, y, z) = x\ln y + y^2 z + z^2 - 8 = 0$$ **Step 2: Compute the partial derivatives of $F$.** 1. With respect to $x$: $$F_x = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[x\ln y + y^2 z + z^2 - 8\right] = \ln y$$ 2. With respect to $y$: $$F_y = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[x\ln y + y^2 z + z^2 - 8\right] = \frac{x}{y} + 2yz = \frac{x + 2y^2 z}{y}$$ 3. With respect to $z$: $$F_z = \frac{\partial}{\partial z}\left[x\ln y + y^2 z + z^2 - 8\right] = y^2 + 2z$$ **Step 3: Apply the implicit partial differentiation formulas.** $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = -\frac{F_x}{F_z} = -\frac{\ln y}{y^2 + 2z}$$ $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = -\frac{F_y}{F_z} = -\frac{\frac{x + 2y^2 z}{y}}{y^2 + 2z} = -\frac{x + 2y^2 z}{y(y^2 + 2z)}$$
Ex 37 Medium Implicit DifferentiationFour Variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 37: Implicit Partial Differentiation of a Four-Variable Surface

🎯 Standard KFUPM theoretical question extending implicit partial differentiation to 4-variable relations.
Problem Statement
Differentiate implicitly to find the first partial derivatives of $w$ with respect to $x$, $y$, and $z$ for: $$\cos(xy) + \sin(yz) + wz = 20$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = \frac{y\sin(xy)}{z}}, \quad \boxed{\frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = \frac{x\sin(xy) - z\cos(yz)}{z}}, \quad \boxed{\frac{\partial w}{\partial z} = -\frac{w + y\cos(yz)}{z}}$$
**Step 1: Set up the implicit function of four variables.** Define: $$F(x, y, z, w) = \cos(xy) + \sin(yz) + wz - 20 = 0$$ Here $w$ is defined implicitly as a differentiable function of $x, y, z$. **Step 2: State the implicit derivative formulas for $w$.** $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = -\frac{F_x}{F_w}, \qquad \frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = -\frac{F_y}{F_w}, \qquad \frac{\partial w}{\partial z} = -\frac{F_z}{F_w}$$ **Step 3: Calculate the partial derivatives of $F$.** $$F_x = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[\cos(xy)] = -y\sin(xy)$$ $$F_y = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[\cos(xy) + \sin(yz)] = -x\sin(xy) + z\cos(yz)$$ $$F_z = \frac{\partial}{\partial z}[\sin(yz) + wz] = y\cos(yz) + w$$ $$F_w = \frac{\partial}{\partial w}[wz] = z$$ **Step 4: Compute the partial derivatives of $w$.** $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = -\frac{-y\sin(xy)}{z} = \frac{y\sin(xy)}{z}$$ $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = -\frac{-x\sin(xy) + z\cos(yz)}{z} = \frac{x\sin(xy) - z\cos(yz)}{z}$$ $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial z} = -\frac{y\cos(yz) + w}{z} = -\frac{w + y\cos(yz)}{z}$$
Ex 39 Easy Homogeneous FunctionsEuler's Theorem
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 39: Homogeneous Functions and Verification of Euler's Theorem

🎯 Core KFUPM topic on testing homogeneous functions and verifying Euler's Identity x f_x + y f_y = n f(x, y).
Problem Statement
For the function $f(x, y) = 2x^2 - 5xy$: (a) Show that the function is homogeneous and determine its degree $n$. (b) Show that $x f_x(x, y) + y f_y(x, y) = n f(x, y)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{(a) Homogeneous of degree } \boxed{n = 2}; \quad \text{(b) } x(4x - 5y) + y(-5x) = 4x^2 - 10xy = \boxed{2(2x^2 - 5xy) = 2f(x, y)}$$
**Part (a): Test for homogeneity and determine the degree $n$.** A function $f(x, y)$ is homogeneous of degree $n$ if $f(tx, ty) = t^n f(x, y)$ for all $t > 0$. Evaluate $f(tx, ty)$: $$f(tx, ty) = 2(tx)^2 - 5(tx)(ty) = 2 t^2 x^2 - 5 t^2 xy$$ Factor out $t^2$: $$f(tx, ty) = t^2\left(2x^2 - 5xy\right) = t^2 f(x, y)$$ Therefore, $f$ is a **homogeneous function of degree $n = 2$**. **Part (b): Verify Euler's Theorem for homogeneous functions.** 1. Find the first partial derivatives of $f(x, y) = 2x^2 - 5xy$: $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[2x^2 - 5xy\right] = 4x - 5y$$ $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[2x^2 - 5xy\right] = -5x$$ 2. Evaluate the linear differential operator expression $x f_x + y f_y$: $$x f_x(x, y) + y f_y(x, y) = x(4x - 5y) + y(-5x)$$ $$= 4x^2 - 5xy - 5xy = 4x^2 - 10xy$$ $$= 2\left(2x^2 - 5xy\right) = 2f(x, y)$$ Since $n = 2$, this rigorously proves: $$x f_x(x, y) + y f_y(x, y) = n f(x, y)$$
Ex 44 Easy Chain Rule from TablesNumerical Evaluation
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 44: Chain Rule Evaluation Using a Table of Values

🎯 Popular KFUPM conceptual exam problem testing functional understanding of multivariable Chain Rule indices without formulas.
Problem Statement
Let $w = f(x, y)$, $x = g(s, t)$, and $y = h(s, t)$, where $f, g$, and $h$ are differentiable. Use the appropriate Chain Rule and the table of values to find $w_s(1, 2)$: $$\begin{array}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline g(1, 2) & h(1, 2) & g_s(1, 2) & h_s(1, 2) & f_x(4, 3) & f_y(4, 3) \\\hline 4 & 3 & -3 & 5 & -5 & 7 \\\hline\end{array}$$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\boxed{w_s(1, 2) = 50}$$
**Step 1: State the Chain Rule formula for $w_s$.** Since $w = f(g(s, t), h(s, t))$, differentiating partially with respect to $s$ yields: $$w_s(s, t) = \frac{\partial w}{\partial s} = \frac{\partial w}{\partial x}\frac{\partial x}{\partial s} + \frac{\partial w}{\partial y}\frac{\partial y}{\partial s} = f_x(x, y) g_s(s, t) + f_y(x, y) h_s(s, t)$$ **Step 2: Identify the evaluation point in the domain of $f$.** At $(s, t) = (1, 2)$: $$x = g(1, 2) = 4, \qquad y = h(1, 2) = 3$$ Therefore, the partial derivatives $f_x$ and $f_y$ must be evaluated at $(x, y) = (4, 3)$: $$w_s(1, 2) = f_x(4, 3) \cdot g_s(1, 2) + f_y(4, 3) \cdot h_s(1, 2)$$ **Step 3: Extract values from the given table and compute.** - $f_x(4, 3) = -5$ - $g_s(1, 2) = -3$ - $f_y(4, 3) = 7$ - $h_s(1, 2) = 5$ Substitute these numerical values: $$w_s(1, 2) = (-5)(-3) + (7)(5) = 15 + 35 = 50$$
Ex 47 Medium General Chain RuleAbstract Functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 47: Chain Rule for Single-Variable Composite Projections of F(u, v)

🎯 Favorite KFUPM theoretical question testing abstract multivariable Chain Rule notation where composite curves are projected onto surfaces.
Problem Statement
Let $F(u, v)$ be a differentiable function of two variables. Find a formula for $f'(x)$ when: (a) $f(x) = F(4x, 4)$ (b) $f(x) = F(-2x, x^2)$
Boxed Final Answer
$$\text{(a) } \boxed{f'(x) = 4 F_u(4x, 4) = 4\frac{\partial F}{\partial u}}, \qquad \text{(b) } \boxed{f'(x) = -2 F_u(-2x, x^2) + 2x F_v(-2x, x^2) = -2\frac{\partial F}{\partial u} + 2x\frac{\partial F}{\partial v}}$$
**Part (a): Find $f'(x)$ for $f(x) = F(4x, 4)$.** 1. Define the intermediate variable functions: $$u(x) = 4x, \qquad v(x) = 4$$ 2. Apply the multivariable Chain Rule for a function of one independent variable $x$: $$f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}F(u, v) = \frac{\partial F}{\partial u}\frac{du}{dx} + \frac{\partial F}{\partial v}\frac{dv}{dx}$$ 3. Compute the single-variable derivatives: $$\frac{du}{dx} = \frac{d}{dx}[4x] = 4, \qquad \frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{d}{dx}[4] = 0$$ 4. Substitute into the Chain Rule formula: $$f'(x) = \frac{\partial F}{\partial u}(4) + \frac{\partial F}{\partial v}(0) = 4\frac{\partial F}{\partial u} = 4 F_u(4x, 4)$$ **Part (b): Find $f'(x)$ for $f(x) = F(-2x, x^2)$.** 1. Define the intermediate variable functions: $$u(x) = -2x, \qquad v(x) = x^2$$ 2. Apply the Chain Rule: $$f'(x) = \frac{\partial F}{\partial u}\frac{du}{dx} + \frac{\partial F}{\partial v}\frac{dv}{dx}$$ 3. Compute the single-variable derivatives: $$\frac{du}{dx} = \frac{d}{dx}[-2x] = -2, \qquad \frac{dv}{dx} = \frac{d}{dx}[x^2] = 2x$$ 4. Substitute into the formula: $$f'(x) = \frac{\partial F}{\partial u}(-2) + \frac{\partial F}{\partial v}(2x) = -2 F_u(-2x, x^2) + 2x F_v(-2x, x^2)$$ Equivalently in Leibniz partial notation: $$f'(x) = -2\frac{\partial F}{\partial u} + 2x\frac{\partial F}{\partial v}$$

13.6 Directional Derivatives & Gradients

Suggested Textbook Practice Problems • 13 Curated Exercises

Jump to problem:
Ex 5 Medium directional-derivativegradient
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 5: Directional Derivative of a Trigonometric Function with Given Angle

🎯 Frequent KFUPM Major Exam problem format requiring computing directional derivative using angle $\theta$ to construct the unit vector $\mathbf{u}$.
Problem Statement
Find the directional derivative of the function $$f(x, y) = \sin(2x + y)$$ at the point $P(0, \pi)$ in the direction of the unit vector $\mathbf{u} = \cos\theta\,\mathbf{i} + \sin\theta\,\mathbf{j}$ where $\theta = -\frac{5\pi}{6}$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\frac{2\sqrt{3} + 1}{2}} = \sqrt{3} + \frac{1}{2}$
1. **Determine the unit vector $\mathbf{u}$:** Given $\theta = -\frac{5\pi}{6}$, the unit vector is: $$\mathbf{u} = \cos\left(-\frac{5\pi}{6}\right)\mathbf{i} + \sin\left(-\frac{5\pi}{6}\right)\mathbf{j} = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\mathbf{i} - \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{j} = \left\langle -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, -\frac{1}{2} \right\rangle$$ 2. **Compute the partial derivatives of $f(x, y)$:** $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[\sin(2x + y)] = 2\cos(2x + y)$$ $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[\sin(2x + y)] = \cos(2x + y)$$ 3. **Evaluate the gradient at $P(0, \pi)$:** At $(x, y) = (0, \pi)$, we have $2x + y = 2(0) + \pi = \pi$. $$f_x(0, \pi) = 2\cos(\pi) = 2(-1) = -2$$ $$f_y(0, \pi) = \cos(\pi) = -1$$ So the gradient vector is: $$\nabla f(0, \pi) = -2\mathbf{i} - \mathbf{j} = \langle -2, -1 \rangle$$ 4. **Calculate the directional derivative $D_{\mathbf{u}} f(0, \pi)$:** By Theorem 13.9: $$D_{\mathbf{u}} f(0, \pi) = \nabla f(0, \pi) \cdot \mathbf{u} = f_x(0, \pi)\cos\theta + f_y(0, \pi)\sin\theta$$ $$D_{\mathbf{u}} f(0, \pi) = (-2)\left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right) + (-1)\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) = \sqrt{3} + \frac{1}{2} = \frac{2\sqrt{3} + 1}{2}$$
Ex 9 Medium directional-derivativevector-normalization
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 9: Directional Derivative of a Distance Function Along a Vector

🎯 KFUPM Exam staple: testing vector normalization $\mathbf{u} = \mathbf{v}/\|\mathbf{v}\|$ prior to taking the dot product with the gradient vector.
Problem Statement
Find the directional derivative of the function $$g(x, y) = \sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$$ at the point $P(3, 4)$ in the direction of the vector $\mathbf{v} = 3\mathbf{i} - 4\mathbf{j}$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{-\frac{7}{25}}$
1. **Find the unit vector $\mathbf{u}$ in the direction of $\mathbf{v}$:** $$\|\mathbf{v}\| = \sqrt{3^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5$$ $$\mathbf{u} = \frac{\mathbf{v}}{\|\mathbf{v}\|} = \frac{3}{5}\mathbf{i} - \frac{4}{5}\mathbf{j} = \left\langle \frac{3}{5}, -\frac{4}{5} \right\rangle$$ 2. **Compute the partial derivatives of $g(x, y)$:** $$g_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left[(x^2 + y^2)^{1/2}\right] = \frac{2x}{2\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}} = \frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}}$$ $$g_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left[(x^2 + y^2)^{1/2}\right] = \frac{2y}{2\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}} = \frac{y}{\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}}$$ 3. **Evaluate the gradient at $P(3, 4)$:** At $(3, 4)$, $\sqrt{x^2 + y^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = 5$. $$g_x(3, 4) = \frac{3}{5}, \quad g_y(3, 4) = \frac{4}{5}$$ $$\nabla g(3, 4) = \frac{3}{5}\mathbf{i} + \frac{4}{5}\mathbf{j} = \left\langle \frac{3}{5}, \frac{4}{5} \right\rangle$$ 4. **Compute the directional derivative:** $$D_{\mathbf{u}} g(3, 4) = \nabla g(3, 4) \cdot \mathbf{u} = \left(\frac{3}{5}\right)\left(\frac{3}{5}\right) + \left(\frac{4}{5}\right)\left(-\frac{4}{5}\right)$$ $$D_{\mathbf{u}} g(3, 4) = \frac{9}{25} - \frac{16}{25} = -\frac{7}{25}$$
Ex 12 Medium directional-derivativetwo-points
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 12: Directional Derivative in the Direction Between Two Points

🎯 Common KFUPM problem testing constructing displacement vector $\vec{PQ}$, normalizing to a unit vector, and evaluating the directional derivative.
Problem Statement
Find the directional derivative of the function $$f(x, y) = \cos(x + y)$$ at the point $P(0, \pi)$ in the direction of $\vec{PQ}$, where $Q\left(\frac{\pi}{2}, 0\right)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{0}$
1. **Determine the direction vector $\mathbf{v} = \vec{PQ}$:** $$\mathbf{v} = \vec{PQ} = \left(\frac{\pi}{2} - 0\right)\mathbf{i} + (0 - \pi)\mathbf{j} = \frac{\pi}{2}\mathbf{i} - \pi\mathbf{j}$$ $$\|\mathbf{v}\| = \sqrt{\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)^2 + (-\pi)^2} = \sqrt{\frac{\pi^2}{4} + \pi^2} = \sqrt{\frac{5\pi^2}{4}} = \frac{\pi\sqrt{5}}{2}$$ $$\mathbf{u} = \frac{\mathbf{v}}{\|\mathbf{v}\|} = \frac{\frac{\pi}{2}\mathbf{i} - \pi\mathbf{j}}{\frac{\pi\sqrt{5}}{2}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\mathbf{i} - \frac{2}{\sqrt{5}}\mathbf{j} = \left\langle \frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}, -\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}} \right\rangle$$ 2. **Compute the partial derivatives of $f(x, y)$:** $$f_x(x, y) = -\sin(x + y)$$ $$f_y(x, y) = -\sin(x + y)$$ 3. **Evaluate the gradient at $P(0, \pi)$:** At $(0, \pi)$, $x + y = \pi$, so $\sin(\pi) = 0$. $$f_x(0, \pi) = -\sin(\pi) = 0$$ $$f_y(0, \pi) = -\sin(\pi) = 0$$ $$\nabla f(0, \pi) = 0\mathbf{i} + 0\mathbf{j} = \langle 0, 0 \rangle$$ 4. **Evaluate the directional derivative:** $$D_{\mathbf{u}} f(0, \pi) = \nabla f(0, \pi) \cdot \mathbf{u} = 0\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}} ight) + 0\left(-\frac{2}{\sqrt{5}} ight) = 0$$
Ex 17 Medium gradientlogarithmic-differentiation
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 17: Gradient Vector of a Logarithmic Quotient Function

🎯 KFUPM Exam favorite combining chain rule and quotient rule in multivariable partial differentiation.
Problem Statement
Find the gradient of the function $$z = \frac{\ln(x^2 - y)}{x} - 4$$ at the point $(2, 3)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{2\mathbf{i} - \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{j}} = \left\langle 2, -\frac{1}{2} \right\rangle$
1. **Compute $\frac{\partial z}{\partial x}$ using the quotient rule:** $$z(x, y) = \frac{\ln(x^2 - y)}{x} - 4$$ $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{x \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[\ln(x^2 - y)] - \ln(x^2 - y) \cdot \frac{d}{dx}[x]}{x^2}$$ Since $\frac{\partial}{\partial x}[\ln(x^2 - y)] = \frac{2x}{x^2 - y}$: $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = \frac{x\left(\frac{2x}{x^2 - y}\right) - \ln(x^2 - y)}{x^2} = \frac{2}{x^2 - y} - \frac{\ln(x^2 - y)}{x^2}$$ 2. **Compute $\frac{\partial z}{\partial y}$:** $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = \frac{1}{x} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[\ln(x^2 - y)] = \frac{1}{x} \left(\frac{-1}{x^2 - y}\right) = -\frac{1}{x(x^2 - y)}$$ 3. **Evaluate at the point $(2, 3)$:** Notice that $x^2 - y = 2^2 - 3 = 4 - 3 = 1$, so $\ln(x^2 - y) = \ln(1) = 0$. $$z_x(2, 3) = \frac{2}{1} - \frac{0}{4} = 2$$ $$z_y(2, 3) = -\frac{1}{2(1)} = -\frac{1}{2}$$ 4. **Write the gradient vector:** $$\nabla z(2, 3) = z_x(2, 3)\mathbf{i} + z_y(2, 3)\mathbf{j} = 2\mathbf{i} - \frac{1}{2}\mathbf{j} = \left\langle 2, -\frac{1}{2} \right\rangle$$
Ex 20 Medium gradientthree-variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 20: Gradient Vector of a Three-Variable Trigonometric Function

🎯 KFUPM Exam question testing gradient computation for functions of three variables $w = f(x, y, z)$.
Problem Statement
Find the gradient of the function $$w = x \tan(y + z)$$ at the point $(4, 3, -1)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\tan(2)\,\mathbf{i} + 4\sec^2(2)\,\mathbf{j} + 4\sec^2(2)\,\mathbf{k}} = \langle \tan 2, 4\sec^2 2, 4\sec^2 2 \rangle$
1. **Compute the partial derivatives of $w(x, y, z)$:** $$w_x(x, y, z) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[x \tan(y + z)] = \tan(y + z)$$ $$w_y(x, y, z) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[x \tan(y + z)] = x \sec^2(y + z)$$ $$w_z(x, y, z) = \frac{\partial}{\partial z}[x \tan(y + z)] = x \sec^2(y + z)$$ 2. **Evaluate the partial derivatives at $(4, 3, -1)$:** Here $y + z = 3 + (-1) = 2$ radians. $$w_x(4, 3, -1) = \tan(2)$$ $$w_y(4, 3, -1) = 4\sec^2(2)$$ $$w_z(4, 3, -1) = 4\sec^2(2)$$ 3. **Form the gradient vector:** $$\nabla w(4, 3, -1) = \tan(2)\,\mathbf{i} + 4\sec^2(2)\,\mathbf{j} + 4\sec^2(2)\,\mathbf{k} = \langle \tan 2, 4\sec^2 2, 4\sec^2 2 \rangle$$
Ex 24 Medium directional-derivativethree-variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 24: Directional Derivative of a Three-Variable Function via Gradient

🎯 Standard KFUPM Major Exam question computing $D_{\mathbf{u}} f = \nabla f \cdot \mathbf{u}$ in three dimensions.
Problem Statement
Use the gradient to find the directional derivative of the function $$f(x, y, z) = xy + yz + xz$$ at the point $P(1, 2, -1)$ in the direction of $\mathbf{v} = 2\mathbf{i} + \mathbf{j} - \mathbf{k}$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{-\frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}} = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}$
1. **Find the unit vector $\mathbf{u}$ in the direction of $\mathbf{v}$:** $$\|\mathbf{v}\| = \sqrt{2^2 + 1^2 + (-1)^2} = \sqrt{4 + 1 + 1} = \sqrt{6}$$ $$\mathbf{u} = \frac{\mathbf{v}}{\|\mathbf{v}\|} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{6}}\mathbf{i} + \frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\mathbf{j} - \frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\mathbf{k} = \left\langle \frac{2}{\sqrt{6}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}, -\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}} \right\rangle$$ 2. **Compute the gradient vector $\nabla f(x, y, z)$:** $$f_x(x, y, z) = y + z$$ $$f_y(x, y, z) = x + z$$ $$f_z(x, y, z) = y + x$$ $$\nabla f(x, y, z) = (y + z)\mathbf{i} + (x + z)\mathbf{j} + (y + x)\mathbf{k}$$ 3. **Evaluate $\nabla f$ at $P(1, 2, -1)$:** $$f_x(1, 2, -1) = 2 + (-1) = 1$$ $$f_y(1, 2, -1) = 1 + (-1) = 0$$ $$f_z(1, 2, -1) = 2 + 1 = 3$$ $$\nabla f(1, 2, -1) = \mathbf{i} + 3\mathbf{k} = \langle 1, 0, 3 \rangle$$ 4. **Calculate the directional derivative:** $$D_{\mathbf{u}} f(1, 2, -1) = \nabla f(1, 2, -1) \cdot \mathbf{u} = 1\left(\frac{2}{\sqrt{6}}\right) + 0\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\right) + 3\left(-\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\right)$$ $$D_{\mathbf{u}} f(1, 2, -1) = \frac{2 - 3}{\sqrt{6}} = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}} = -\frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}$$
Ex 28 Medium directional-derivativegradient
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 28: Directional Derivative Along Displacement Vector in Space

🎯 Classic KFUPM Major 2 question testing 3D directional derivative along displacement vector $\vec{PQ}$.
Problem Statement
Use the gradient to find the directional derivative of the function $$h(x, y, z) = \ln(x + y + z)$$ at the point $P(1, 0, 0)$ in the direction of $\vec{PQ}$, where $Q(4, 3, 1)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\frac{7\sqrt{19}}{19}} = \frac{7}{\sqrt{19}}$
1. **Determine the direction vector $\mathbf{v} = \vec{PQ}$ and unit vector $\mathbf{u}$:** $$\mathbf{v} = \vec{PQ} = (4 - 1)\mathbf{i} + (3 - 0)\mathbf{j} + (1 - 0)\mathbf{k} = 3\mathbf{i} + 3\mathbf{j} + \mathbf{k}$$ $$\|\mathbf{v}\| = \sqrt{3^2 + 3^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{9 + 9 + 1} = \sqrt{19}$$ $$\mathbf{u} = \frac{\mathbf{v}}{\|\mathbf{v}\|} = \frac{3}{\sqrt{19}}\mathbf{i} + \frac{3}{\sqrt{19}}\mathbf{j} + \frac{1}{\sqrt{19}}\mathbf{k} = \left\langle \frac{3}{\sqrt{19}}, \frac{3}{\sqrt{19}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{19}} \right\rangle$$ 2. **Compute the gradient of $h(x, y, z)$:** $$h_x = \frac{1}{x + y + z}, \quad h_y = \frac{1}{x + y + z}, \quad h_z = \frac{1}{x + y + z}$$ $$\nabla h(x, y, z) = \frac{1}{x + y + z}(\mathbf{i} + \mathbf{j} + \mathbf{k})$$ 3. **Evaluate $\nabla h$ at $P(1, 0, 0)$:** $$x + y + z = 1 + 0 + 0 = 1$$ $$\nabla h(1, 0, 0) = \mathbf{i} + \mathbf{j} + \mathbf{k} = \langle 1, 1, 1 \rangle$$ 4. **Evaluate the directional derivative:** $$D_{\mathbf{u}} h(1, 0, 0) = \nabla h(1, 0, 0) \cdot \mathbf{u} = 1\left(\frac{3}{\sqrt{19}}\right) + 1\left(\frac{3}{\sqrt{19}}\right) + 1\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{19}}\right)$$ $$D_{\mathbf{u}} h(1, 0, 0) = \frac{3 + 3 + 1}{\sqrt{19}} = \frac{7}{\sqrt{19}} = \frac{7\sqrt{19}}{19}$$
Ex 29 Medium gradientmaximum-rate-of-change
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 29: Gradient and Maximum Rate of Change of a Two-Variable Function

🎯 Core KFUPM Exam concept: the maximum value of $D_{\mathbf{u}} f$ equals $\|\nabla f\|$ and occurs in the direction of $\nabla f$.
Problem Statement
Find the gradient of the function and the maximum value of the directional derivative at the given point: $$f(x, y) = y^2 - x\sqrt{y}, \quad (0, 3)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\nabla f(0, 3) = \boxed{-\sqrt{3}\,\mathbf{i} + 6\mathbf{j}}, \quad \text{Maximum Rate of Change} = \boxed{\sqrt{39}}$
1. **Compute the partial derivatives of $f(x, y)$:** $$f(x, y) = y^2 - x y^{1/2}$$ $$f_x(x, y) = -\sqrt{y}$$ $$f_y(x, y) = 2y - \frac{x}{2\sqrt{y}}$$ 2. **Evaluate the gradient at $(0, 3)$:** $$f_x(0, 3) = -\sqrt{3}$$ $$f_y(0, 3) = 2(3) - \frac{0}{2\sqrt{3}} = 6$$ $$\nabla f(0, 3) = -\sqrt{3}\,\mathbf{i} + 6\mathbf{j} = \langle -\sqrt{3}, 6 \rangle$$ 3. **Compute the maximum value of the directional derivative:** By Theorem 13.11, the maximum value of the directional derivative occurs in the direction of the gradient $\nabla f$ and is equal to its magnitude $\|\nabla f\|$: $$\text{Maximum } D_{\mathbf{u}} f(0, 3) = \|\nabla f(0, 3)\| = \sqrt{(-\sqrt{3})^2 + 6^2} = \sqrt{3 + 36} = \sqrt{39}$$
Ex 36 Easy gradientthree-variables
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 36: Gradient and Maximum Rate of Change of a Symmetric 3D Function

🎯 Conceptual KFUPM question testing gradient evaluation at the origin $(0, 0, 0)$ and its resulting zero magnitude.
Problem Statement
Find the gradient of the function and the maximum value of the directional derivative at the given point: $$w = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2 - y^2 - z^2}}, \quad (0, 0, 0)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\nabla w(0, 0, 0) = \boxed{\mathbf{0}} = \langle 0, 0, 0 \rangle, \quad \text{Maximum Rate of Change} = \boxed{0}$
1. **Express $w$ using negative power notation:** $$w = (1 - x^2 - y^2 - z^2)^{-1/2}$$ 2. **Differentiate with respect to $x$, $y$, and $z$ using the chain rule:** $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial x} = -\frac{1}{2}(1 - x^2 - y^2 - z^2)^{-3/2}(-2x) = \frac{x}{(1 - x^2 - y^2 - z^2)^{3/2}}$$ $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial y} = -\frac{1}{2}(1 - x^2 - y^2 - z^2)^{-3/2}(-2y) = \frac{y}{(1 - x^2 - y^2 - z^2)^{3/2}}$$ $$\frac{\partial w}{\partial z} = -\frac{1}{2}(1 - x^2 - y^2 - z^2)^{-3/2}(-2z) = \frac{z}{(1 - x^2 - y^2 - z^2)^{3/2}}$$ 3. **Evaluate the gradient at $(0, 0, 0)$:** $$\nabla w(0, 0, 0) = \frac{0\mathbf{i} + 0\mathbf{j} + 0\mathbf{k}}{(1 - 0)^{3/2}} = 0\mathbf{i} + 0\mathbf{j} + 0\mathbf{k} = \mathbf{0} = \langle 0, 0, 0 \rangle$$ 4. **Maximum value of the directional derivative:** $$\text{Maximum } D_{\mathbf{u}} w(0, 0, 0) = \|\nabla w(0, 0, 0)\| = \|\mathbf{0}\| = 0$$
Ex 39 Easy normal-vectorlevel-curve
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 39: Normal Vector to a Linear Level Curve

🎯 Fundamental KFUPM concept: $\nabla f(x_0, y_0)$ is orthogonal (normal) to the level curve $f(x, y) = c$.
Problem Statement
Find a normal vector to the level curve $f(x, y) = c$ at the point $P$: $$f(x, y) = 6 - 2x - 3y, \quad c = 6, \quad P(0, 0)$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{-2\mathbf{i} - 3\mathbf{j}} = \langle -2, -3 \rangle \quad (\text{or } 2\mathbf{i} + 3\mathbf{j})$
1. **Analyze the level curve:** Setting $f(x, y) = c$ gives: $$6 - 2x - 3y = 6 \implies 2x + 3y = 0$$ This is a straight line through the origin $(0, 0)$. 2. **Apply Theorem 13.12 (Gradient is Normal to Level Curves):** If $f$ is differentiable, $\nabla f(x_0, y_0)$ is normal to the level curve through $(x_0, y_0)$. Compute the gradient vector: $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[6 - 2x - 3y] = -2$$ $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[6 - 2x - 3y] = -3$$ $$\nabla f(x, y) = -2\mathbf{i} - 3\mathbf{j} = \langle -2, -3 \rangle$$ 3. **Evaluate at $P(0, 0)$:** $$\nabla f(0, 0) = -2\mathbf{i} - 3\mathbf{j} = \langle -2, -3 \rangle$$ Any non-zero scalar multiple, such as $2\mathbf{i} + 3\mathbf{j}$, is also a normal vector to the level curve.
Ex 45 Medium gradientunit-normal
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 45: Gradient, Unit Normal Vector, and Tangent Line to a Hyperbolic Level Curve

🎯 Highly tested in KFUPM Major 2: finding tangent line and unit normal vector to a level curve $f(x, y) = c$.
Problem Statement
For the function $f(x, y) = 3x^2 - 2y^2$, level curve $c = 1$, and point $P(1, 1)$: (a) Find the gradient of $f$ at $P$. (b) Find a unit normal vector to the level curve $f(x, y) = c$ at $P$. (c) Find an equation of the tangent line to the level curve at $P$. (d) Describe the analytical characteristics of the level curve and the tangent line.
Boxed Final Answer
(a) $\nabla f(1, 1) = \boxed{6\mathbf{i} - 4\mathbf{j}}$ (b) $\mathbf{u} = \boxed{\frac{3}{\sqrt{13}}\,\mathbf{i} - \frac{2}{\sqrt{13}}\,\mathbf{j}}$ (c) Tangent line: $\boxed{3x - 2y = 1} \iff y = \frac{3}{2}x - \frac{1}{2}$ (d) Level curve is a hyperbola $\frac{x^2}{1/3} - \frac{y^2}{1/2} = 1$ with vertices at $\left(\pm\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}, 0\right)$.
1. **(a) Find the gradient at $P(1, 1)$:** $$f_x(x, y) = 6x \implies f_x(1, 1) = 6(1) = 6$$ $$f_y(x, y) = -4y \implies f_y(1, 1) = -4(1) = -4$$ $$\nabla f(1, 1) = 6\mathbf{i} - 4\mathbf{j} = \langle 6, -4 \rangle$$ 2. **(b) Find a unit normal vector to the level curve at $P(1, 1)$:** The magnitude of the gradient is: $$\|\nabla f(1, 1)\| = \sqrt{6^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{36 + 16} = \sqrt{52} = 2\sqrt{13}$$ A unit normal vector is: $$\mathbf{u} = \frac{\nabla f(1, 1)}{\|\nabla f(1, 1)\|} = \frac{6\mathbf{i} - 4\mathbf{j}}{2\sqrt{13}} = \frac{3}{\sqrt{13}}\mathbf{i} - \frac{2}{\sqrt{13}}\mathbf{j} = \left\langle \frac{3}{\sqrt{13}}, -\frac{2}{\sqrt{13}} \right\rangle$$ 3. **(c) Find an equation of the tangent line at $P(1, 1)$:** Since $\mathbf{n} = \langle 6, -4 \rangle = 2\langle 3, -2 \rangle$ is normal to the curve, the equation of the tangent line through $(1, 1)$ is: $$3(x - 1) - 2(y - 1) = 0$$ $$3x - 3 - 2y + 2 = 0 \implies 3x - 2y - 1 = 0 \iff y = \frac{3}{2}x - \frac{1}{2}$$ 4. **(d) Analytical description of the level curve:** The level curve $3x^2 - 2y^2 = 1$ can be written in standard form as: $$\frac{x^2}{1/3} - \frac{y^2}{1/2} = 1$$ This is a hyperbola opening along the $x$-axis with center at $(0, 0)$, vertices at $\left(\pm \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}, 0\right)$, and asymptotes $y = \pm \sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}x$. At the point $P(1, 1)$, the slope of the curve is $m = \frac{3}{2}$ and the outward normal vector points in the direction of $\langle 3, -2 \rangle$.
Ex 57 Medium maximum-increasegradient-applications
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 57: Direction and Rate of Maximum Temperature Increase on a Plate

🎯 Classic KFUPM applied word problem format: determining direction of fastest temperature increase and maximum rate.
Problem Statement
The temperature in degrees Celsius on the surface of a metal plate is given by $$T(x, y) = 80 - 3x^2 - y^2$$ where $x$ and $y$ are measured in centimeters. Find the direction from the point $P(-1, 5)$ where the temperature increases most rapidly, and find this maximum rate of increase.
Boxed Final Answer
Direction: $\boxed{6\mathbf{i} - 10\mathbf{j}}$ (or unit direction $\frac{3}{\sqrt{34}}\,\mathbf{i} - \frac{5}{\sqrt{34}}\,\mathbf{j}$); Maximum Rate: $\boxed{2\sqrt{34}} \approx 11.66^\circ\text{C/cm}$
1. **Compute the gradient of the temperature distribution $T(x, y)$:** $$T_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[80 - 3x^2 - y^2] = -6x$$ $$T_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[80 - 3x^2 - y^2] = -2y$$ $$\nabla T(x, y) = -6x\mathbf{i} - 2y\mathbf{j}$$ 2. **Evaluate the gradient at $P(-1, 5)$:** $$\nabla T(-1, 5) = -6(-1)\mathbf{i} - 2(5)\mathbf{j} = 6\mathbf{i} - 10\mathbf{j} = \langle 6, -10 \rangle$$ The temperature increases most rapidly in the direction of the gradient vector $\nabla T(-1, 5) = 6\mathbf{i} - 10\mathbf{j}$. In terms of a unit vector, this direction is: $$\mathbf{u} = \frac{\nabla T(-1, 5)}{\|\nabla T(-1, 5)\|} = \frac{6\mathbf{i} - 10\mathbf{j}}{\sqrt{6^2 + (-10)^2}} = \frac{6\mathbf{i} - 10\mathbf{j}}{\sqrt{136}} = \frac{6\mathbf{i} - 10\mathbf{j}}{2\sqrt{34}} = \frac{3}{\sqrt{34}}\mathbf{i} - \frac{5}{\sqrt{34}}\mathbf{j}$$ 3. **Compute the maximum rate of increase:** The maximum rate of increase is the magnitude of the gradient: $$\|\nabla T(-1, 5)\| = \sqrt{6^2 + (-10)^2} = \sqrt{36 + 100} = \sqrt{136} = 2\sqrt{34} \approx 11.66^\circ\text{C/cm}$$
Ex 68 Hard directional-derivative-definitionlimits
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 68: Directional Derivative of a Non-Differentiable Piecewise Function

🎯 KFUPM Exam conceptual/proof question testing the formal limit definition of directional derivatives when differentiability fails.
Problem Statement
Consider the function $$f(x, y) = \begin{cases} \frac{4xy}{x^2 + y^2}, & (x, y) \neq (0, 0) \\ 0, & (x, y) = (0, 0) \end{cases}$$ and the unit vector $\mathbf{u} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{i} + \mathbf{j})$. Does the directional derivative of $f$ at $P(0, 0)$ in the direction of $\mathbf{u}$ exist? If $f(0, 0)$ were defined as $2$ instead of $0$, would the directional derivative exist? Explain.
Boxed Final Answer
For $f(0, 0) = 0$, $D_{\mathbf{u}} f(0, 0)$ $\boxed{\text{does not exist}}$. If $f(0, 0) = 2$, $D_{\mathbf{u}} f(0, 0) = \boxed{0}$ (exists).
1. **Check applicability of Theorem 13.9:** Theorem 13.9 ($D_{\mathbf{u}} f = \nabla f \cdot \mathbf{u}$) requires $f$ to be differentiable at $(0, 0)$. However, along the line $y = kx$ ($x \neq 0$): $$f(x, kx) = \frac{4x(kx)}{x^2 + (kx)^2} = \frac{4kx^2}{x^2(1 + k^2)} = \frac{4k}{1 + k^2}$$ As $(x, y) \to (0, 0)$, the limit depends on the path (slope $k$). For instance, along $y = x$, $f \to 2$, but along $y = 0$, $f \to 0$. Since $\lim_{(x, y) \to (0, 0)} f(x, y)$ does not exist, $f$ is not continuous at $(0, 0)$ and therefore is not differentiable at $(0, 0)$. We must use the limit definition of the directional derivative. 2. **Apply the limit definition of directional derivative for $f(0, 0) = 0$:** With $\mathbf{u} = \left\langle \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}, \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} \right\rangle$: $$D_{\mathbf{u}} f(0, 0) = \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{f\left(0 + t\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}, 0 + t\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right) - f(0, 0)}{t}$$ For $t \neq 0$: $$f\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt{2}}, \frac{t}{\sqrt{2}}\right) = \frac{4\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt{2}}\right)}{\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{t}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^2} = \frac{4\left(\frac{t^2}{2}\right)}{\frac{t^2}{2} + \frac{t^2}{2}} = \frac{2t^2}{t^2} = 2$$ Then the difference quotient becomes: $$\lim_{t \to 0} \frac{2 - 0}{t} = \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{2}{t}$$ As $t \to 0^+$, $\frac{2}{t} \to +\infty$, and as $t \to 0^-$, $\frac{2}{t} \to -\infty$. Because this two-sided limit does not exist, the directional derivative $D_{\mathbf{u}} f(0, 0)$ **does not exist**. 3. **Examine the case where $f(0, 0) = 2$:** If $f(0, 0)$ is redefined to equal $2$: $$D_{\mathbf{u}} f(0, 0) = \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{f\left(\frac{t}{\sqrt{2}}, \frac{t}{\sqrt{2}}\right) - f(0, 0)}{t} = \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{2 - 2}{t} = \lim_{t \to 0} \frac{0}{t} = \lim_{t \to 0} 0 = 0$$ Therefore, if $f(0, 0) = 2$, the directional derivative **exists** and equals $0$.

13.7 Tangent Planes & Normal Lines

Ex 5 Easy level-surfacequadric-surface
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 5: Geometric Description of a Quadric Level Surface

🎯 KFUPM Exam classification of quadric level surfaces $F(x, y, z) = 0$.
Problem Statement
Describe the level surface $F(x, y, z) = 0$ for the function $$F(x, y, z) = 4x^2 + 9y^2 - 4z^2$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\text{Elliptic cone}}$ with axis along the $z$-axis and vertex at the origin $(0, 0, 0)$.
1. **Set the function equal to zero:** $$F(x, y, z) = 0 \implies 4x^2 + 9y^2 - 4z^2 = 0$$ 2. **Rewrite in canonical quadric surface form:** $$4x^2 + 9y^2 = 4z^2 \iff x^2 + \frac{9}{4}y^2 = z^2 \iff \frac{x^2}{1} + \frac{y^2}{(2/3)^2} = z^2$$ Dividing by $36$ also gives the standard form: $$\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = \frac{z^2}{9}$$ 3. **Identify the quadric surface type:** This is the standard equation of an **elliptic cone** with vertex at $(0, 0, 0)$ and its axis along the $z$-axis. 4. **Analyze the traces:** - **Horizontal traces ($z = k \neq 0$):** Ellipses given by $\frac{x^2}{k^2} + \frac{y^2}{(2k/3)^2} = 1$. - **Horizontal trace at $z = 0$:** The single point $(0, 0, 0)$. - **Vertical traces in $xz$-plane ($y = 0$):** Pair of intersecting lines $4x^2 - 4z^2 = 0 \implies x = \pm z$. - **Vertical traces in $yz$-plane ($x = 0$):** Pair of intersecting lines $9y^2 - 4z^2 = 0 \implies y = \pm \frac{2}{3}z$.
Ex 7 Easy tangent-planeparaboloid
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 7: Tangent Plane to an Explicit Paraboloid Surface

🎯 Core KFUPM Major Exam question on finding tangent planes to explicit surfaces $z = f(x, y)$.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the tangent plane to the surface $$z = x^2 + y^2 + 3$$ at the point $(2, 1, 8)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{4x + 2y - z = 2}$ (or $4x + 2y - z - 2 = 0$)
1. **Verify the point lies on the surface:** At $(x, y) = (2, 1)$: $$z = 2^2 + 1^2 + 3 = 4 + 1 + 3 = 8 \quad \checkmark$$ 2. **Compute the partial derivatives of $z = f(x, y)$:** $$f_x(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[x^2 + y^2 + 3] = 2x$$ $$f_y(x, y) = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[x^2 + y^2 + 3] = 2y$$ 3. **Evaluate at $(2, 1)$:** $$f_x(2, 1) = 2(2) = 4$$ $$f_y(2, 1) = 2(1) = 2$$ 4. **Apply the tangent plane formula for $z = f(x, y)$:** $$z - z_0 = f_x(x_0, y_0)(x - x_0) + f_y(x_0, y_0)(y - y_0)$$ $$z - 8 = 4(x - 2) + 2(y - 1)$$ $$z - 8 = 4x - 8 + 2y - 2$$ $$4x + 2y - z - 2 = 0 \iff 4x + 2y - z = 2$$ *(Alternatively, using $F(x, y, z) = x^2 + y^2 - z + 3 = 0$: $\nabla F(2, 1, 8) = \langle 4, 2, -1 \rangle$, giving $4(x - 2) + 2(y - 1) - 1(z - 8) = 0 \implies 4x + 2y - z = 2$.)*
Ex 10 Medium tangent-planearctan
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 10: Tangent Plane to an Inverse Trigonometric Surface

🎯 KFUPM Exam favorite testing derivatives of inverse trigonometric functions and quotient/chain rules.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the tangent plane to the surface $$g(x, y) = \arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)$$ at the point $(1, 0, 0)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{y - z = 0}$
1. **Express the surface as a level surface $G(x, y, z) = 0$:** $$G(x, y, z) = \arctan\left(\frac{y}{x}\right) - z = 0$$ 2. **Compute the partial derivatives of $G$:** Using the derivative of $\arctan(u)$ where $u = \frac{y}{x}$: $$G_x = \frac{1}{1 + (y/x)^2} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial x}\left(\frac{y}{x}\right) = \frac{x^2}{x^2 + y^2} \left(-\frac{y}{x^2}\right) = -\frac{y}{x^2 + y^2}$$ $$G_y = \frac{1}{1 + (y/x)^2} \cdot \frac{\partial}{\partial y}\left(\frac{y}{x}\right) = \frac{x^2}{x^2 + y^2} \left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = \frac{x}{x^2 + y^2}$$ $$G_z = -1$$ 3. **Evaluate the gradient at $(1, 0, 0)$:** $$G_x(1, 0, 0) = -\frac{0}{1^2 + 0^2} = 0$$ $$G_y(1, 0, 0) = \frac{1}{1^2 + 0^2} = 1$$ $$G_z(1, 0, 0) = -1$$ $$\nabla G(1, 0, 0) = \langle 0, 1, -1 \rangle$$ 4. **Write the equation of the tangent plane:** $$G_x(1, 0, 0)(x - 1) + G_y(1, 0, 0)(y - 0) + G_z(1, 0, 0)(z - 0) = 0$$ $$0(x - 1) + 1(y - 0) - 1(z - 0) = 0 \implies y - z = 0$$
Ex 14 Medium tangent-planetrigonometric-functions
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 14: Tangent Plane to a Surface Defined by Product of Trigonometric Functions

🎯 KFUPM Exam problem combining trigonometric differentiation with tangent planes.
Problem Statement
Find an equation of the tangent plane to the surface $$f(x, y) = \sin x \cos y$$ at the point $\left(\frac{\pi}{3}, \frac{\pi}{6}, \frac{3}{4}\right)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\sqrt{3}x - \sqrt{3}y - 4z = \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{6} - 3}$ (or $4z - \sqrt{3}x + \sqrt{3}y = 3 - \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{6}$)
1. **Define the level surface $F(x, y, z) = 0$:** $$F(x, y, z) = \sin x \cos y - z = 0$$ 2. **Compute the partial derivatives of $F$:** $$F_x = \cos x \cos y$$ $$F_y = -\sin x \sin y$$ $$F_z = -1$$ 3. **Evaluate at $\left(\frac{\pi}{3}, \frac{\pi}{6}, \frac{3}{4}\right)$:** $$\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = \frac{1}{2}, \quad \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{6}\right) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \implies F_x = \left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}$$ $$\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}, \quad \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{6}\right) = \frac{1}{2} \implies F_y = -\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}$$ $$F_z = -1$$ 4. **Construct the equation of the tangent plane:** $$F_x\left(x - \frac{\pi}{3}\right) + F_y\left(y - \frac{\pi}{6}\right) + F_z\left(z - \frac{3}{4}\right) = 0$$ $$\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\left(x - \frac{\pi}{3}\right) - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\left(y - \frac{\pi}{6}\right) - 1\left(z - \frac{3}{4}\right) = 0$$ Multiply through by $4$: $$\sqrt{3}\left(x - \frac{\pi}{3}\right) - \sqrt{3}\left(y - \frac{\pi}{6}\right) - 4\left(z - \frac{3}{4}\right) = 0$$ $$\sqrt{3}x - \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{3} - \sqrt{3}y + \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{6} - 4z + 3 = 0$$ Combining the constant terms: $$-\frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{3} + \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{6} = \frac{-2\sqrt{3}\pi + \sqrt{3}\pi}{6} = -\frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{6}$$ $$\sqrt{3}x - \sqrt{3}y - 4z = \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{6} - 3$$
Ex 23 Medium tangent-planenormal-line
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 23: Tangent Plane and Symmetric Equations of Normal Line to a Hyperbolic Surface

🎯 KFUPM Exam question testing both tangent plane equation and symmetric normal line equations.
Problem Statement
For the surface $$xyz = 10$$ at the point $(1, 2, 5)$: (a) Find an equation of the tangent plane. (b) Find a set of symmetric equations for the normal line.
Boxed Final Answer
(a) Tangent plane: $\boxed{10x + 5y + 2z = 30}$ (b) Normal line: $\boxed{\frac{x - 1}{10} = \frac{y - 2}{5} = \frac{z - 5}{2}}$
1. **Define the level surface $F(x, y, z) = 0$:** $$F(x, y, z) = xyz - 10 = 0$$ 2. **Compute the gradient vector $\nabla F$:** $$F_x = yz, \quad F_y = xz, \quad F_z = xy$$ $$\nabla F(x, y, z) = \langle yz, xz, xy \rangle$$ 3. **Evaluate $\nabla F$ at $(1, 2, 5)$:** $$F_x(1, 2, 5) = (2)(5) = 10$$ $$F_y(1, 2, 5) = (1)(5) = 5$$ $$F_z(1, 2, 5) = (1)(2) = 2$$ The normal vector has direction numbers $a = 10, b = 5, c = 2$. 4. **(a) Equation of the tangent plane:** $$10(x - 1) + 5(y - 2) + 2(z - 5) = 0$$ $$10x - 10 + 5y - 10 + 2z - 10 = 0$$ $$10x + 5y + 2z = 30$$ 5. **(b) Symmetric equations of the normal line:** The normal line passes through $(1, 2, 5)$ with direction vector $\langle 10, 5, 2 \rangle$: $$\frac{x - 1}{10} = \frac{y - 2}{5} = \frac{z - 5}{2}$$
Ex 25 Medium tangent-planenormal-line
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 25: Tangent Plane and Normal Line to an Exponential Surface

🎯 KFUPM Exam favorite testing multivariable product rule and normal line formulation.
Problem Statement
For the surface $$z = y e^{2xy}$$ at the point $(0, 2, 2)$: (a) Find an equation of the tangent plane. (b) Find a set of symmetric equations for the normal line.
Boxed Final Answer
(a) Tangent plane: $\boxed{8x + y - z = 0}$ (b) Normal line: $\boxed{\frac{x}{8} = \frac{y - 2}{1} = \frac{z - 2}{-1}}$
1. **Define the level surface $F(x, y, z) = 0$:** $$F(x, y, z) = y e^{2xy} - z = 0$$ 2. **Compute the partial derivatives of $F$:** $$F_x = \frac{\partial}{\partial x}[y e^{2xy}] = y(2y e^{2xy}) = 2y^2 e^{2xy}$$ $$F_y = \frac{\partial}{\partial y}[y e^{2xy}] = 1 \cdot e^{2xy} + y(2x e^{2xy}) = (1 + 2xy)e^{2xy}$$ $$F_z = -1$$ 3. **Evaluate at $(0, 2, 2)$:** Note that $2xy = 2(0)(2) = 0$, so $e^0 = 1$. $$F_x(0, 2, 2) = 2(2^2)(1) = 8$$ $$F_y(0, 2, 2) = (1 + 0)(1) = 1$$ $$F_z(0, 2, 2) = -1$$ Normal direction numbers are $a = 8, b = 1, c = -1$. 4. **(a) Equation of the tangent plane:** $$8(x - 0) + 1(y - 2) - 1(z - 2) = 0$$ $$8x + y - 2 - z + 2 = 0 \implies 8x + y - z = 0$$ 5. **(b) Symmetric equations of the normal line:** Passing through $(0, 2, 2)$ with direction vector $\langle 8, 1, -1 \rangle$: $$\frac{x - 0}{8} = \frac{y - 2}{1} = \frac{z - 2}{-1} \iff \frac{x}{8} = y - 2 = -(z - 2)$$
Ex 27 Medium curve-of-intersectiontangent-line
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 27: Tangent Line to the Curve of Intersection of Two Surfaces

🎯 Classic KFUPM Exam question: finding tangent line to intersection curve via cross product of surface gradients $\nabla F \times \nabla G$.
Problem Statement
Find a set of parametric equations for the tangent line to the curve of intersection of the surfaces $$x^2 + y^2 = 2 \quad \text{and} \quad z = x$$ at the point $(1, 1, 1)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{x = 1 + t, \quad y = 1 - t, \quad z = 1 + t}$
1. **Define the surfaces as level surfaces:** $$F(x, y, z) = x^2 + y^2 - 2 = 0$$ $$G(x, y, z) = x - z = 0$$ 2. **Compute the gradients of both surfaces:** $$\nabla F(x, y, z) = 2x\mathbf{i} + 2y\mathbf{j} + 0\mathbf{k} = \langle 2x, 2y, 0 \rangle$$ $$\nabla G(x, y, z) = \mathbf{i} + 0\mathbf{j} - \mathbf{k} = \langle 1, 0, -1 \rangle$$ 3. **Evaluate the gradients at $(1, 1, 1)$:** $$\nabla F(1, 1, 1) = 2\mathbf{i} + 2\mathbf{j} = \langle 2, 2, 0 \rangle$$ $$\nabla G(1, 1, 1) = \mathbf{i} - \mathbf{k} = \langle 1, 0, -1 \rangle$$ 4. **Find the tangent direction via cross product:** The tangent vector $\mathbf{v}$ to the curve of intersection is orthogonal to both normal vectors: $$\mathbf{v} = \nabla F(1, 1, 1) \times \nabla G(1, 1, 1) = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 2 & 2 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 & -1 \end{vmatrix}$$ $$\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{i}(-2 - 0) - \mathbf{j}(-2 - 0) + \mathbf{k}(0 - 2) = -2\mathbf{i} + 2\mathbf{j} - 2\mathbf{k} = \langle -2, 2, -2 \rangle$$ Dividing by $-2$ gives simplified direction numbers: $\langle 1, -1, 1 \rangle$. 5. **Write parametric equations for the tangent line through $(1, 1, 1)$:** $$x = 1 + t, \quad y = 1 - t, \quad z = 1 + t$$
Ex 31 Medium curve-of-intersectiontangent-line
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 31: Tangent Line to Intersection of Sphere and Plane

🎯 KFUPM Major Exam staple: intersection of a quadric surface and a plane.
Problem Statement
Find a set of parametric equations for the tangent line to the curve of intersection of the surfaces $$x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = 14 \quad \text{and} \quad x - y - z = 0$$ at the point $(3, 1, 2)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{x = 3 + t, \quad y = 1 + 5t, \quad z = 2 - 4t}$
1. **Define the surfaces:** $$F(x, y, z) = x^2 + y^2 + z^2 - 14 = 0$$ $$G(x, y, z) = x - y - z = 0$$ 2. **Compute the gradients:** $$\nabla F(x, y, z) = \langle 2x, 2y, 2z \rangle$$ $$\nabla G(x, y, z) = \langle 1, -1, -1 \rangle$$ 3. **Evaluate the gradients at $(3, 1, 2)$:** $$\nabla F(3, 1, 2) = \langle 6, 2, 4 \rangle$$ $$\nabla G(3, 1, 2) = \langle 1, -1, -1 \rangle$$ 4. **Calculate the direction vector via cross product:** $$\mathbf{v} = \nabla F(3, 1, 2) \times \nabla G(3, 1, 2) = \begin{vmatrix} \mathbf{i} & \mathbf{j} & \mathbf{k} \\ 6 & 2 & 4 \\ 1 & -1 & -1 \end{vmatrix}$$ $$\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{i}(2(-1) - 4(-1)) - \mathbf{j}(6(-1) - 4(1)) + \mathbf{k}(6(-1) - 2(1))$$ $$\mathbf{v} = \mathbf{i}(-2 + 4) - \mathbf{j}(-6 - 4) + \mathbf{k}(-6 - 2) = 2\mathbf{i} + 10\mathbf{j} - 8\mathbf{k} = \langle 2, 10, -8 \rangle$$ Factoring out $2$ yields direction numbers: $\langle 1, 5, -4 \rangle$. 5. **Write parametric equations through $(3, 1, 2)$:** $$x = 3 + t, \quad y = 1 + 5t, \quad z = 2 - 4t$$
Ex 35 Medium angle-of-inclinationtangent-plane
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 35: Angle of Inclination of a Tangent Plane to a Surface

🎯 Frequently tested KFUPM concept: angle between tangent plane and $xy$-plane using $\cos\theta = \frac{|\mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{k}|}{\|\mathbf{n}\|}$.
Problem Statement
Find the angle of inclination of the tangent plane to the surface $$x^2 - y^2 + z = 0$$ at the point $(1, 2, 3)$.
Boxed Final Answer
$\theta = \boxed{\arccos\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{21}}\right)} \approx 77.40^\circ$
1. **Define the surface $F(x, y, z) = 0$:** $$F(x, y, z) = x^2 - y^2 + z = 0$$ 2. **Compute the gradient normal vector:** $$\nabla F(x, y, z) = 2x\mathbf{i} - 2y\mathbf{j} + \mathbf{k}$$ At the point $(1, 2, 3)$: $$\mathbf{n} = \nabla F(1, 2, 3) = 2(1)\mathbf{i} - 2(2)\mathbf{j} + \mathbf{k} = 2\mathbf{i} - 4\mathbf{j} + \mathbf{k} = \langle 2, -4, 1 \rangle$$ 3. **Compute the magnitude of $\mathbf{n}$:** $$\|\mathbf{n}\| = \sqrt{2^2 + (-4)^2 + 1^2} = \sqrt{4 + 16 + 1} = \sqrt{21}$$ 4. **Calculate the angle of inclination $\theta$:** The angle of inclination $\theta$ ($0 \le \theta \le \pi/2$) is the angle between the tangent plane's normal $\mathbf{n}$ and the unit normal to the $xy$-plane, $\mathbf{k} = \langle 0, 0, 1 \rangle$: $$\cos\theta = \frac{|\mathbf{n} \cdot \mathbf{k}|}{\|\mathbf{n}\|} = \frac{|(2)(0) + (-4)(0) + (1)(1)|}{\sqrt{21}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{21}}$$ $$\theta = \arccos\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{21}}\right) = \arccos\left(\frac{\sqrt{21}}{21}\right) \approx 77.40^\circ \approx 1.35 \text{ rad}$$
Ex 37 Easy horizontal-tangent-planeextrema
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 37: Points with Horizontal Tangent Plane on an Elliptic Paraboloid

🎯 Fundamental KFUPM concept connecting horizontal tangent planes ($f_x = 0, f_y = 0$) to critical points.
Problem Statement
Find the point(s) on the surface $$z = 3 - x^2 - y^2 + 6y$$ at which the tangent plane is horizontal.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{(0, 3, 12)}$
1. **Condition for horizontal tangent plane:** The tangent plane to $z = f(x, y)$ is horizontal if and only if both first partial derivatives vanish: $$f_x(x, y) = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad f_y(x, y) = 0$$ 2. **Compute partial derivatives:** $$f_x(x, y) = -2x$$ $$f_y(x, y) = -2y + 6$$ 3. **Solve the system:** $$-2x = 0 \implies x = 0$$ $$-2y + 6 = 0 \implies y = 3$$ 4. **Find the corresponding $z$-coordinate:** $$z = 3 - 0^2 - 3^2 + 6(3) = 3 - 9 + 18 = 12$$ 5. **Geometric verification:** Completing the square gives $z = -(x^2) - (y - 3)^2 + 12$. This is an elliptic paraboloid opening downward with vertex at $(0, 3, 12)$, where the unique horizontal tangent plane is $z = 12$.
Ex 40 Medium horizontal-tangent-planepartial-derivatives
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 40: Points with Horizontal Tangent Plane via Linear System

🎯 KFUPM Exam favorite requiring setting partial derivatives to zero and solving a $2 \times 2$ linear system.
Problem Statement
Find the point(s) on the surface $$z = 4x^2 + 4xy - 2y^2 + 8x - 5y - 4$$ at which the tangent plane is horizontal.
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\left(-\frac{1}{4}, -\frac{3}{2}, -\frac{5}{4}\right)}$
1. **Set partial derivatives to zero:** $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial x} = 8x + 4y + 8 = 0 \implies 2x + y = -2 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}$$ $$\frac{\partial z}{\partial y} = 4x - 4y - 5 = 0 \implies 4x - 4y = 5 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}$$ 2. **Solve the system for $x$ and $y$:** Multiply Equation 1 by $4$: $$8x + 4y = -8$$ Add this to Equation 2: $$(8x + 4y) + (4x - 4y) = -8 + 5$$ $$12x = -3 \implies x = -\frac{3}{12} = -\frac{1}{4}$$ Substitute $x = -\frac{1}{4}$ into Equation 1: $$2\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right) + y = -2 \implies -\frac{1}{2} + y = -2 \implies y = -\frac{3}{2}$$ 3. **Compute the corresponding $z$-value:** $$z = 4\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right)^2 + 4\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right)\left(-\frac{3}{2}\right) - 2\left(-\frac{3}{2}\right)^2 + 8\left(-\frac{1}{4}\right) - 5\left(-\frac{3}{2}\right) - 4$$ $$z = 4\left(\frac{1}{16}\right) + \frac{3}{2} - 2\left(\frac{9}{4}\right) - 2 + \frac{15}{2} - 4$$ $$z = \frac{1}{4} + \frac{3}{2} - \frac{9}{2} + \frac{15}{2} - 6 = \frac{1}{4} + \frac{9}{2} - 6 = \frac{1}{4} + \frac{18}{4} - \frac{24}{4} = -\frac{5}{4}$$ 4. **Conclusion:** The unique point is $\left(-\frac{1}{4}, -\frac{3}{2}, -\frac{5}{4}\right)$.
Ex 43 Medium tangent-surfacestangent-plane
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 43: Tangency of Two Surfaces at a Common Point

🎯 High KFUPM conceptual value: demonstrating surface tangency by showing their normal vectors are scalar multiples at the point.
Problem Statement
Show that the surfaces $$x^2 + 2y^2 + 3z^2 = 3 \quad \text{and} \quad x^2 + y^2 + z^2 + 6x - 10y + 14 = 0$$ are tangent to each other at the point $(-1, 1, 0)$ by showing that they have the same tangent plane at this point.
Boxed Final Answer
Both surfaces share the identical tangent plane $\boxed{x - 2y + 3 = 0}$ at $(-1, 1, 0)$ because their gradients $\nabla F = \langle -2, 4, 0 \rangle$ and $\nabla G = \langle 4, -8, 0 \rangle$ are parallel ($\,\nabla G = -2\nabla F\,$).
1. **Verify that $(-1, 1, 0)$ lies on both surfaces:** - For $F(x, y, z) = x^2 + 2y^2 + 3z^2 - 3$: $$F(-1, 1, 0) = (-1)^2 + 2(1)^2 + 3(0)^2 - 3 = 1 + 2 + 0 - 3 = 0 \quad \checkmark$$ - For $G(x, y, z) = x^2 + y^2 + z^2 + 6x - 10y + 14$: $$G(-1, 1, 0) = (-1)^2 + 1^2 + 0^2 + 6(-1) - 10(1) + 14 = 1 + 1 - 6 - 10 + 14 = 0 \quad \checkmark$$ 2. **Find the tangent plane to the first surface at $(-1, 1, 0)$:** $$\nabla F = \langle 2x, 4y, 6z \rangle$$ $$\nabla F(-1, 1, 0) = \langle 2(-1), 4(1), 6(0) \rangle = \langle -2, 4, 0 \rangle$$ The tangent plane equation is: $$-2(x + 1) + 4(y - 1) + 0(z - 0) = 0$$ $$-2x - 2 + 4y - 4 = 0 \implies -2x + 4y - 6 = 0 \iff x - 2y + 3 = 0$$ 3. **Find the tangent plane to the second surface at $(-1, 1, 0)$:** $$\nabla G = \langle 2x + 6, 2y - 10, 2z \rangle$$ $$\nabla G(-1, 1, 0) = \langle 2(-1) + 6, 2(1) - 10, 2(0) \rangle = \langle 4, -8, 0 \rangle$$ The tangent plane equation is: $$4(x + 1) - 8(y - 1) + 0(z - 0) = 0$$ $$4x + 4 - 8y + 8 = 0 \implies 4x - 8y + 12 = 0 \iff x - 2y + 3 = 0$$ 4. **Conclusion:** Notice that $\nabla G(-1, 1, 0) = -2\nabla F(-1, 1, 0)$, showing the two normal vectors are parallel. Since both tangent planes reduce to the identical linear equation $x - 2y + 3 = 0$, the surfaces are tangent to each other at $(-1, 1, 0)$.
Ex 46 Medium orthogonal-surfacesperpendicular-planes
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 46: Orthogonal Surfaces and Perpendicular Tangent Planes

🎯 KFUPM Exam favorite: proving orthogonality of two intersecting surfaces using $\nabla F \cdot \nabla G = 0$.
Problem Statement
For the surfaces $$x^2 + y^2 + z^2 + 2x - 4y - 4z - 12 = 0 \quad \text{and} \quad 4x^2 + y^2 + 16z^2 = 24$$ at the point $(1, -2, 1)$: (a) Show that the surfaces intersect at the given point. (b) Show that the surfaces have perpendicular tangent planes at this point.
Boxed Final Answer
(a) Direct substitution gives $F(1, -2, 1) = 0$ and $G(1, -2, 1) = 0$. (b) The gradients are $\nabla F = \langle 4, -8, -2 \rangle$ and $\nabla G = \langle 8, -4, 32 \rangle$. Their dot product is $\nabla F \cdot \nabla G = 32 + 32 - 64 = \boxed{0}$, confirming perpendicular tangent planes.
1. **(a) Verify the point of intersection:** Let $F(x, y, z) = x^2 + y^2 + z^2 + 2x - 4y - 4z - 12$ and $G(x, y, z) = 4x^2 + y^2 + 16z^2 - 24$. Evaluate $F$ at $(1, -2, 1)$: $$F(1, -2, 1) = 1^2 + (-2)^2 + 1^2 + 2(1) - 4(-2) - 4(1) - 12 = 1 + 4 + 1 + 2 + 8 - 4 - 12 = 0$$ Evaluate $G$ at $(1, -2, 1)$: $$G(1, -2, 1) = 4(1)^2 + (-2)^2 + 16(1)^2 - 24 = 4 + 4 + 16 - 24 = 0$$ Since both functions evaluate to $0$, $(1, -2, 1)$ lies on both surfaces, confirming intersection. 2. **(b) Show the tangent planes are perpendicular:** The tangent planes are perpendicular if and only if their normal vectors are orthogonal, which means their gradient dot product is zero: $\nabla F \cdot \nabla G = 0$. - Gradient of $F$: $$\nabla F = \langle 2x + 2, 2y - 4, 2z - 4 \rangle$$ $$\nabla F(1, -2, 1) = \langle 2(1) + 2, 2(-2) - 4, 2(1) - 4 \rangle = \langle 4, -8, -2 \rangle$$ - Gradient of $G$: $$\nabla G = \langle 8x, 2y, 32z \rangle$$ $$\nabla G(1, -2, 1) = \langle 8(1), 2(-2), 32(1) \rangle = \langle 8, -4, 32 \rangle$$ - Dot product: $$\nabla F(1, -2, 1) \cdot \nabla G(1, -2, 1) = (4)(8) + (-8)(-4) + (-2)(32) = 32 + 32 - 64 = 0$$ Since the dot product is $0$, the normal vectors are perpendicular, and thus the tangent planes to the two surfaces are perpendicular at $(1, -2, 1)$.
Ex 49 Hard ellipsoidtangent-plane
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 49: Points on an Ellipsoid with Tangent Plane Parallel to a Given Plane

🎯 Classic KFUPM Exam question testing finding points on a quadric surface where the tangent plane satisfies a geometric condition (parallelism).
Problem Statement
Find the points on the ellipsoid $$3x^2 + y^2 + 3z^2 = 1$$ where the tangent plane is parallel to the plane $$-12x + 2y + 6z = 0$$
Boxed Final Answer
$\boxed{\left(-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{4}\right)} \quad \text{and} \quad \boxed{\left(\frac{1}{2}, -\frac{1}{4}, -\frac{1}{4}\right)}$
1. **Define the ellipsoid level surface:** $$F(x, y, z) = 3x^2 + y^2 + 3z^2 - 1 = 0$$ The normal vector to the tangent plane at $(x, y, z)$ is: $$\nabla F(x, y, z) = \langle 6x, 2y, 6z \rangle$$ 2. **Identify the normal vector to the given plane:** The plane is $-12x + 2y + 6z = 0$, with normal vector $\mathbf{n}_0 = \langle -12, 2, 6 \rangle = 2\langle -6, 1, 3 \rangle$. A parallel normal vector is $\mathbf{n} = \langle -6, 1, 3 \rangle$. 3. **Set up the proportionality condition for parallel planes:** The tangent plane is parallel to the given plane when $\nabla F$ is proportional to $\mathbf{n}$: $$\langle 6x, 2y, 6z \rangle = t \langle -6, 1, 3 \rangle$$ Equating components: $$6x = -6t \implies x = -t$$ $$2y = t \implies y = \frac{t}{2}$$ $$6z = 3t \implies z = \frac{t}{2}$$ 4. **Substitute into the ellipsoid equation:** $$3(-t)^2 + \left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^2 + 3\left(\frac{t}{2}\right)^2 = 1$$ $$3t^2 + \frac{t^2}{4} + \frac{3t^2}{4} = 1$$ $$3t^2 + t^2 = 1 \implies 4t^2 = 1 \implies t^2 = \frac{1}{4} \implies t = \pm \frac{1}{2}$$ 5. **Determine the coordinates of the two points:** - For $t = \frac{1}{2}$: $$x = -\frac{1}{2}, \quad y = \frac{1/2}{2} = \frac{1}{4}, \quad z = \frac{1/2}{2} = \frac{1}{4} \implies \left(-\frac{1}{2}, \frac{1}{4}, \frac{1}{4}\right)$$ - For $t = -\frac{1}{2}$: $$x = -\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) = \frac{1}{2}, \quad y = \frac{-1/2}{2} = -\frac{1}{4}, \quad z = \frac{-1/2}{2} = -\frac{1}{4} \implies \left(\frac{1}{2}, -\frac{1}{4}, -\frac{1}{4}\right)$$
Ex 59 Medium conetangent-plane
★ KFUPM Aligned

Exercise 59: Proving Tangent Planes to an Elliptic Cone Pass Through the Origin

🎯 KFUPM Exam proof/analytical question on tangent plane properties of homogeneous quadric cones.
Problem Statement
Show that any tangent plane to the cone $$z^2 = a^2 x^2 + b^2 y^2$$ passes through the origin $(0, 0, 0)$.
Boxed Final Answer
The tangent plane at any point $(x_0, y_0, z_0)$ on the cone is $\boxed{a^2 x_0 x + b^2 y_0 y - z_0 z = 0}$, which has no constant term and therefore passes through $(0, 0, 0)$.
1. **Define the cone as a level surface:** $$F(x, y, z) = a^2 x^2 + b^2 y^2 - z^2 = 0$$ 2. **Let $(x_0, y_0, z_0)$ be any point on the cone:** Since $(x_0, y_0, z_0)$ lies on the surface, it satisfies: $$a^2 x_0^2 + b^2 y_0^2 - z_0^2 = 0 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}$$ 3. **Compute the gradient normal vector at $(x_0, y_0, z_0)$:** $$\nabla F(x, y, z) = \langle 2a^2 x, 2b^2 y, -2z \rangle$$ $$\nabla F(x_0, y_0, z_0) = \langle 2a^2 x_0, 2b^2 y_0, -2z_0 \rangle$$ 4. **Write the equation of the tangent plane at $(x_0, y_0, z_0)$:** $$2a^2 x_0(x - x_0) + 2b^2 y_0(y - y_0) - 2z_0(z - z_0) = 0$$ Divide both sides by $2$: $$a^2 x_0(x - x_0) + b^2 y_0(y - y_0) - z_0(z - z_0) = 0$$ Expand the equation: $$a^2 x_0 x - a^2 x_0^2 + b^2 y_0 y - b^2 y_0^2 - z_0 z + z_0^2 = 0$$ Rearrange terms: $$a^2 x_0 x + b^2 y_0 y - z_0 z = a^2 x_0^2 + b^2 y_0^2 - z_0^2$$ 5. **Apply Equation 1:** Since $a^2 x_0^2 + b^2 y_0^2 - z_0^2 = 0$, the equation of the tangent plane simplifies to: $$a^2 x_0 x + b^2 y_0 y - z_0 z = 0$$ 6. **Verify the origin lies on the tangent plane:** Substituting $(x, y, z) = (0, 0, 0)$ into this equation: $$a^2 x_0(0) + b^2 y_0(0) - z_0(0) = 0 \implies 0 = 0$$ Thus, $(0, 0, 0)$ satisfies the equation of the tangent plane for every choice of $(x_0, y_0, z_0)$ on the cone. This proves that every tangent plane passes through the origin.
